Flag Hardy Space Theory on Heisenberg Groups and Applications Thanks: P. Chen was supported by National Key R&D Program of China 2022YFA1005700 and by NNSF of China 12171489 Thanks: Cowling acknowledges support from the Australian Research Council, grants DP170103025 and DP220100285. Thanks: Li acknowledges support from the Australian Research Council, grants DP170101060 and DP220100285. Thanks: Ottazzi acknowledges support from the Australian Research Council, grant DP220100285.
Peng Chen, Michael G. Cowling, Ming-Yi Lee, Ji Li, Alessandro Ottazzi

Abstract
. We develop a complete theory of the flag Hardy space 𝖧 F 1 ​ ( ℍ ν ) \mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}) on the Heisenberg group ℍ ν \mathbb{H}^{\nu} with characterisations by atomic decompositions, area functions, square functions, maximal functions and singular integrals. We introduce new techniques to overcome the difficulties caused by the noncommutativity of the Heisenberg group and the lack of a suitable Fourier transformation and Cauchy–Riemann type equations. Applications include the boundedness on 𝖧 F 1 ​ ( ℍ ν ) \mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}) of various singular integral operators that arise in complex analysis, a sharp boundedness result on 𝖧 F 1 ​ ( ℍ ν ) \mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}) of the Marcinkiewicz-type multipliers introduced by Müller, Ricci and Stein, and the characterisation of the flag BMO space by singular integrals.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 22E30, 43A15, Secondary 42B30

0.
Introduction

The point of this paper is to completely characterise a flag Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
on the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
Our space is a proper subspace of the one-parameter Hardy space of Folland and Stein
[
24
]
that was developed by Christ and Geller
[
10
]
, and is a simple modification of the Hardy space proposed by Han, Lu and Sawyer
[
30
]
, who initiated the study of this question and characterised their proposed space by square functions.
Our work answers a question of Stein, who asked for a Hardy space theory in the flag setting.

Our space is useful in several applications:

1) endpoint boundedness of certain singular integrals
, including the Hilbert transform in the central variable, the homogeneous kernels considered first by Folland and Stein
[
24
]
, the Cauchy–Szegõ projection on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and the singular integrals considered by Phong and Stein
[
50
]
in their study of the
\bar{\partial}
-Neumann problem (see also
[
54
]
);

2) endpoint boundedness of two-parameter Marcinkiewicz multipliers
as studied by Müller, Ricci and Stein
[
43
,
44
]
, and the one-parameter multipliers associated to the sub-Laplacian in the Heisenberg group considered by Hebisch
[
31
]
and by Müller and Stein
[
45
]
;

3) representation of functions
in the flag BMO space on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.

This work opens the door to characterising Hardy spaces on more general homogeneous Lie groups with implicit multiparameter structures, and has potential applications to the study of the
\bar{\partial}_{b}
-complex on different domains, continuing the path blazed by Nagel, Ricci and Stein
[
46
]
and Nagel, Ricci, Stein and Wainger
[
47
,
48
]
, and more generally to the development of the
\mathsf{L}^{p}
and
\mathsf{H}^{p}
theory of flag singular integral operators on nilpotent Lie groups.

In this section, we present the background to our results and the difficulties, state our results, outline the rest of the paper and explain what is new in our work.

Background and questions

Modern approaches to one-parameter harmonic analysis have been developed from the 1950s on; the Calderón–Zygmund theory of singular integrals and the related function spaces are central to this theory.
In the setting of Euclidean spaces
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, a notable property of standard Calderón–Zygmund singular integral operators and also of the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator is that they commute with the classical dilations
D_{t}
, given by
D_{t}x=(tx_{1},\dots,tx_{n})
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
(see, for example,
[
52
,
53
]
).
Multi-parameter harmonic analysis, with emphasis on the special case of product spaces (that is, singular integrals commuting with multiparameter families of dilations on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
), has been studied extensively since the 1970s by Gundy, Stein, Chang, R. Fefferman, Journé, Pipher, Lacey, and many others (see, for example,
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
17
,
19
,
20
,
27
,
37
,
49
]
).
To show the boundedness of Calderón–Zygmund singular integrals, there are corresponding function spaces, notably Hardy spaces and their dual spaces, which provide a natural way to do this easily.
These spaces were developed in
[
11
,
16
]
in the one-parameter setting, and later in
[
4
,
37
]
in the multiparameter product setting.

A more recent breakthrough is due to Müller, Ricci and Stein
[
43
]
, who
introduced a new type of multiparameter structure, between the one-parameter and multiparameter cases, and studied the
\mathsf{L}^{p}
boundedness of Marcinkiewicz multiplier operators
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,i\mathcal{T})
on the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
is the sub-Laplacian on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
,
\mathcal{T}
is the central invariant vector field, and
m
is a multiplier of Marcinkiewicz type.
They proved the
\mathsf{L}^{p}
boundedness of
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,i\mathcal{T})
by using lifting and projection arguments when
m
satisfies
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}
regularity conditions.
Using the same approach, they also proved the
\mathsf{L}^{p}
boundedness of certain singular integrals that arise in the
\bar{\partial}
-Neumann problem (see
[
50
,
54
]
).
The new multiparameter structure, called a
flag structure
,
is implicit, that is, it cannot be written in terms of explicit dilations, and this leads to completely new difficulties that do not appear in the one-parameter or product settings.
The implicit structure is obtained by taking the product space
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}
, and identifying points
((z,t),s)
and
((z^{\prime},t^{\prime}),s^{\prime})
when
z=z^{\prime}
and
t+s=t^{\prime}+s^{\prime}
.
Under this identification, a product of balls
B_{1}\times B_{2}
in the product space
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}
becomes a group theoretic product
B_{1}B_{2}
of sets in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
The identification gives rise to a projection from functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}
to functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
: let
F=F\bigl((z,t),s\bigr)
be a function on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}
, with
(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and define the projected function
f
by

f(z,t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}F\bigl((z,t-s),s\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s.

See the survey paper by Stein
[
54
]
.
More recently, Nagel, Ricci and Stein
[
46
]
studied a class of operators on nilpotent Lie groups
G
given by convolution with flag kernels and applied this theory to study the
\Box_{b}
-complex on certain CR submanifolds of
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
.
Further, Nagel, Ricci, Stein and Wainger
[
47
,
48
]
developed the theory of singular integrals with flag kernels in the more general setting of homogeneous groups.
They proved that singular integral operators on these groups with flag kernels are bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}
when
1<p<\infty
, and form an algebra.
See also the recent results of Street
[
56
]
.

Motivated by Stein’s 1999 question, and by
[
43
,
54
,
46
,
47
,
48
]
, the following questions arise.

Question 1.

Is there a flag Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
on the Heisenberg group that may be characterised in terms of square functions, maximal functions, atomic decompositions, and Riesz transforms?

Question 2.

What is the relationship of the space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to the one-parameter Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
introduced by Folland and Stein
[
24
]
and then studied by Christ and Geller
[
10
]
?

Question 3.

Various singular integral operators on the Heisenberg group appear in connection with boundary value problems in complex analysis in several variables and are known to be bounded on the spaces
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p<\infty
.
These include the Cauchy–Szegõ projection, which has a homogeneous kernel, and the operators introduced in
[
50
]
, which have nonhomogeneous kernels.
Are these operators also bounded from
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
?

Question 4.

Suppose that the Marcinkiewicz multiplier function
\mathrm{M}
satisfies the sharp regularity conditions
\alpha>\nu
and
\beta>1/2
identified by Müller, Ricci and Stein
[
44
]
, who showed that the operator
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
is bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p<\infty
.
Is
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
?

Question 5.

Does the dual space
\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
of
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
have a decomposition like that of the one-parameter space
\mathrm{BMO}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
established in
[
24
]
?

Answers.

We answer Question 1 by describing a Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
that may be characterised by atomic decompositions, square functions, area functions, maximal functions and flag Riesz transforms.

Our results imply that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
We show that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is a proper subspace of
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, addressing Question 2.

Next, we give a positive answer to Question 3, by verifying that
\left\|\mathcal{K}a\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
is uniformly bounded when
\mathcal{K}
is a singular integral operator and
a
is a flag atom, and hence confirm the boundedness of the Cauchy–Szegõ operator on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

We also show that, when
\mathrm{M}
satisfies the sharp regularity condition above, the Marcinkiewicz multiplier operator
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
is bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, thereby answering Question 4.
In particular, we see that the one-parameter Hörmander multiplier operator
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)})
is bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and hence also on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
; apparently this was not known before.
By interpolation we also find another proof of the results of Müller and Stein
[
45
]
and of Hebisch
[
31
]
.

Finally, we note that the characterisation of
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
by flag Riesz transforms and the duality of
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
with
\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
imply that functions in
\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
may be written as sums of singular integral operators applied to bounded functions.

Remarks.

Han, Lu and Sawyer
[
30
]
defined a flag Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{HLS}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
using a discrete Littlewood–Paley square function, with convolutions of the form
\psi_{m,n}*f
, and described the interpolation spaces between
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{HLS}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
There is a simple isomorphism between their space and ours, namely the reflection operator; this enables us to appeal to their work to deal with square function Hardy spaces, and to use their interpolation theorem.
They dealt with
\mathsf{H}^{p}
spaces when
p<1
, so their work is more general than ours; however, the heat and Poisson semigroups and Riesz transforms are not given by left convolutions, so their methods do not link immediately to these standard operators, and they do not obtain characterisations by maximal functions, singular integrals, or atomic decompositions.

Han, Lu and Sawyer proved the boundedness from
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{HLS}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
of singular integral operators whose kernel satisfies a cancellation condition.
However, their methods cannot handle the radial and nontangential maximal functions or the operators
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)})
of Müller–Stein
[
45
]
and Hebisch
[
31
]
and
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
of Müller–Ricci–Stein
[
43
]
, which do not even have an explicit kernel.
(Even for the classical one-parameter Mihlin–Hörmander multiplier, when we consider the sharp index of differentiation, there is no pointwise estimate for the kernel, see
[
53
]
.)
Our atomic decomposition shows that all these operators are bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Recently, Han, Lee, Li and Wick

[
28
]
gave a complete description of the flag Hardy space on the simplified model space
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
; their main tools include Fourier transforms, the Cauchy–Riemann equations and the geometrical fact that a flag rectangle
R
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
may be written as a product
R=I\times J
, where
I
and
J
are cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
These tools and geometry do not apply in the flag setting on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.

Further progress in the flag setting includes (but is not limited to)
[
3
,
55
]
.

In studying Hardy spaces, and in other areas of analysis on euclidean and more general spaces, “dyadic decompositions” play an important role.
In the context of the Heisenberg group, there are two possible strategies: one may use the fractal dyadic decompositions of Strichartz
[
57
]
and of Tyson
[
58
]
, which have symmetries under translation and certain dilations, or the less regular decompositions of Christ
[
9
]
and of Hytönen and Kairema
[
36
]
, which do not have these symmetries.
We have chosen to use the former, as it enables us to appeal to results of Han, Lu and Sawyer
[
30
]
, where this approach is used, and enables us to make simplifying assumptions in many proofs.
However, fractal dyadic decompositions do not exist on all nilpotent Lie groups, let alone in more general spaces.
Our results may also be couched in terms of the decompositions of Christ and of Hytönen and Kairema, and that this is essential for some further generalisations.

Statement of main results

In this section, we state our main results in more detail.

We begin with a little notation; the details and further notation are given in the next section.
The Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is a Lie group, with underlying manifold
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}
; a typical element is written
g
, or in coordinates as
(z,t)
where
z\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
We may also write
(z,t)
as
(z_{1},\dots,z_{\nu},t)
, or as
(x,y,t)
, or as
(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu},y_{1},\dots,y_{\nu},t)
.
The multiplication law is

(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})\cdot(z,t)=(z^{\prime}+z,t^{\prime}+t+S(z^{\prime},z)),

where
S
is the (slightly nonstandard) symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
given by

S(z^{\prime},z)=4\nu\operatorname{Im}\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}z^{\prime}_{j}\bar{z}_{j}\Bigr)=4\nu\left(\langle y^{\prime},x\rangle-\langle x^{\prime},y\rangle\right)=4\nu\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}y^{\prime}_{j}x_{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}x^{\prime}_{j}y_{j}\Bigr).

The identity of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is written
o
or
(0,0)
; inversion is given by
(z,t)^{-1}=(-z,-t)
.
The dilations
D_{r}
, where
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, given by
D_{r}(z,t):=(rz,r^{2}t)
, are automorphisms of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.

The Haar measure on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is the Lebesgue measure, which we write
\,\mathrm{d}g
or
\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}t
.
The standard Heisenberg group convolution is given by the formula

f_{1}\ast_{(1)}f_{2}(g)=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}f_{1}(g_{1})f_{2}(g_{1}^{-1}g)\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}f_{1}(gg_{1})f_{2}(g_{1}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

The flag structure on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
involves the subgroup
\{(0,t):t\in\mathbb{R}\}
, which we identify with
\mathbb{R}
in the obvious way (unfortunately this may be a little confusing).
We convolve a function
f_{1}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
with a function
f_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}
as follows:

f_{1}\ast_{(2)}f_{2}(g)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(gg_{2}^{-1})f_{2}(g_{2})\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

Thus we think of functions on
\mathbb{R}
as distributions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
which are supported in the centre of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
; by distributions, we mean elements of the dual of the usual Schwartz space
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Generally we write the group operation in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
as juxtaposition when we use the symbol
g
for a group element, and as
\cdot
when we use coordinates, while we write the group product in
\mathbb{R}
multiplicatively when we use the symbol
g
for a group element, and additively when we are working in coordinates.

The ball with centre
g_{1}
and radius
r
in the gauge metric on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
(see Section
1.1
for the definition) is denoted by
B^{(1)}(g_{1},r)
; it coincides with
g_{1}B^{(1)}(o,r)
.
The interval
(t-s,t+s)
is denoted by
B^{(2)}(t,s)
, and identified with a subset of the centre of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
As mentioned above, the basic geometric object in the flag structure, analogous to the ball in classical analysis and to the direct product of balls in analysis on product spaces, is the (group) product of balls
B^{(1)}(g_{1},r)B^{(2)}(g_{2},s)
.
Given an open subset
E
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, we write
\mathfrak{M}(E)
for the set of all maximal “subshards” of
E
(see the next section for detailed notation and definitions).

We write
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
for the usual sub-Laplacian on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\mathcal{L}_{(2)}
for the Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}
, both normalised to be positive operators, and
\mathcal{T}
for the central invariant vector field on
\mathbb{R}
.
These operators, which are interpreted distributionally, and the associated geometry are key to our results.
We write
\operatorname{Dom}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N})
for the subspace of
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
of functions

b
such that
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b
also lies in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Now we give seven different alternative definitions of flag Hardy spaces.

Definition
.

Fix
M
and
N
in
\mathbb{N}^{+}
and a real number
\kappa\in(1+1/(2\nu),\infty)
.
An
atom
is a function
a
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that there exists an open subset
E
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure
|E|
and functions
a_{R}
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, called
particles
, and
b_{R}
in
\operatorname{Dom}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N})
for all
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
such that

(A1)

a_{R}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b_{R}
and
\operatorname{supp}b_{R}\subseteq R^{*}
, where
R^{*}
is a
\kappa
-enlargement of
R
;

(A2)

for all sign sequences
\sigma:\mathfrak{M}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
, the sum
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\sigma_{R}a_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
a_{\sigma}
say, and
\left\|a_{\sigma}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\left|E\right|^{-1/2}
;

(A3)

a=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}a_{R}
.

We say that
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
has an
atomic decomposition
if we may write
f
as a sum
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
, converging in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, where
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\lambda_{j}|<\infty
and each
a_{j}
is an atom; we write
f\sim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
to indicate that
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
is an atomic decomposition of
f
.
The
atomic Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is defined to be the linear space of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
that have atomic decompositions, with norm

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\inf\biggl\{\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\lambda_{j}|:f\sim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}\biggr\}.

We provide a more complete definition at the beginning of Section
2
, and in particular make the notion of a
\kappa
-enlargement
R^{*}
of a shard
R
precise.
We also discuss alternative definitions of particles.

Several characterisations of classical Hardy spaces involve integrals or maximal functions over cones.
Given
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we define the cone
\Gamma_{\beta}(g)
as follows:

(0.1)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\beta}(g):=\{(g^{\prime},r,s)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}:g^{\prime}\in gB^{(1)}(o,\beta r)B^{(2)}(0,\beta^{2}s)\}.

For simplicity, we write
\Gamma_{1}(g)
as
\Gamma(g)
.
In Section
3
we shall see that changing the parameter
\beta
does not change the Hardy space, though it changes the norm to an equivalent norm.

For our next versions of the flag Hardy space, we take
normalised dilates
of functions
\varphi^{(1)}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
on
\mathbb{R}
, satisfying various smoothness, decay, moment and “invertibility” conditions that we specify later:

(0.2)

\displaystyle\varphi^{(1)}_{r}(z,t):=r^{-\delta}\varphi^{(1)}(z/r,t/r^{2})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(t):=s^{-1}\varphi^{(2)}(t/s),

where
z\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
We also define the
normalised characteristic functions

(0.3)

\displaystyle\chi^{(1)}_{r}:=\frac{1}{|B^{(1)}(o,r)|}\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(1)}(o,r)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\chi^{(2)}_{s}:=\frac{1}{|B^{(2)}(0,s)|}\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(1)}(0,s)}\,,

where
\mathbbm{1}
denotes a characteristic function.
We shorten many formulae by writing

(0.4)

\displaystyle\varphi_{r,s}:=\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\chi_{r,s}:=\chi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\chi^{(2)}_{2}.

The Poisson kernel is an important special case.
Let
p^{(1)}_{r}
and
p^{(2)}_{s}
be the convolution kernels of the operators
\mathrm{e}^{-r\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\mathrm{e}^{-s\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}_{(2)}}
on
\mathbb{R}
; then when we take
\varphi^{(1)}
to be
p^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
to be
p^{(2)}
, we obtain the
flag Poisson kernel

p_{r,s}
, given by
p^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}
.
The flag heat kernel
h_{r,s}
arises similarly.

Definition
.

Take
\varphi^{(1)}
,
\varphi^{(2)}
and
\varphi_{r,s}
satisfying various smoothness, decay, moment and “invertibility” conditions that we specify later, and
\chi^{(1)}
,
\chi^{(2)}
, and
\chi_{r,s}
as above.
For
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, we define the
Lusin–Littlewood–Paley area function
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)
associated to
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g):=\biggl(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\bigl|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\bigr|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}(g)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\biggr)^{1/2}

for all
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, and we define the Lusin–Littlewood–Paley Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to be the set of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
for which
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, with norm

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

In Section
3
, we shall make the conditions on
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
precise, and see that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is largely independent of the choice of
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
, so may be abbreviated to
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
In particular, we may take
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
to be derivatives of the heat or Poisson kernels associated to
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
and
\mathcal{L}_{(2)}
.

Definition
.

Suppose that
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
satisfy appropriate smoothness, decay, moment and “invertibility” conditions.
For
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, we define the
continuous and discrete Littlewood–Paley square functions
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)
and
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{dis},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)
associated to
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g):=\biggl(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\bigl|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g)\bigr|^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{dis},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g):=\biggl(\sum_{(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}\bigl|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{2^{m},2^{n}}(g)\bigr|^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

for all
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
We define the
square function Hardy spaces
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to be the sets of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
for which
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
or
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{dis},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, with norms

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

and

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{dis},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}..

Later we shall see that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
are essentially independent of the choice of
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
, and so may be abbreviated to
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
In particular, we may take
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
to be gradients of the heat or Poisson kernels associated to
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
and
\mathcal{L}_{(2)}
.
The reflection operator identifies our space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
with the Hardy space of Han, Lu and Sawyer
[
30
]
.

Our next definitions involve pairs
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
of functions
\varphi^{(1)}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
on
\mathbb{R}
that are Poisson bounded, that is, together with all their derivatives, they decay at least as fast as Poisson kernels and their corresponding derivatives at infinity.
The precise conditions are stated in Definition
1.26
.

Definition
.

The
radial maximal function
of
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g):=\sup_{r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g)|.

The space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{radial},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is the set of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that
\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, with norm

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{radial}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\left\|\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
.

Definition
.

Fix
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
The
nontangential maximal function
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi},\beta}(f)
of
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is defined by

(0.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi},\beta}(f)(g):=\sup_{(g^{\prime},r,s)\in\Gamma_{\beta}(g)}|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime})|\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

By Lemma
1.35
below, the space of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi},\beta}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is independent of
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, so to simplify notation we take
\beta
equal to
1
and write
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)
rather than
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi},\beta}(f)
.
The Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{nontan},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
consisting of all such
f
is well-defined.
We equip this space with the norm

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{nontan},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\left\|\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

An important special case of the space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{nontan},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
arises when we deal with the flag Poisson kernel
p_{r,s}
.
In this case, properties of harmonic functions may be exploited.

Definition
.

Let
\mathsf{F}
be a Poisson bounded family of pairs of functions, as in Definition
1.26
below.
The
grand maximal function

\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)
of
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g):=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathsf{F}}\sup_{r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g)\right|\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

The space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is defined to be the set of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that
\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)
is in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, with norm

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\left\|\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
.

One of our key results, which generalises a result of Fefferman and Stein
[
16
]
in the classical case, is that these Hardy spaces do not depend on
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
or the family
\mathsf{F}
.
More precisely, we get the same radial and nontangential spaces for all pairs
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
as long as
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
are suitably normalised and satisfy Poisson decay conditions, though the norms do depend on
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
.
Similarly, we get the same space if
\mathsf{F}
is any nontrivial Poisson bounded family.

Our final definition brings in singular integral operators.

Definition
.

The (tensor-valued)
flag Riesz transformations
are defined by

\mathcal{R}_{(1)}=\nabla_{(1)}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-1/2},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{(2)}=\nabla_{(2)}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-1/2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{R}_{F}=\mathcal{R}_{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{R}_{(2)},

and the space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{Riesz}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is the set of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that
\mathcal{R}_{(1)}(f)
,
\mathcal{R}_{(2)}(f)
and
\mathcal{R}_{F}(f)
all lie in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, with norm
\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{Riesz}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
given by

\left\|\mathcal{R}_{F}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|\nabla_{(1)}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-1/2}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|\nabla_{(2)}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-1/2}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

The main result of this paper is that the definitions above all agree.

Theorem A
.

The spaces defined above all coincide, that is,

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{nontan}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\\
=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{radial}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{gmax}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{Riesz}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}).\end{gathered}

Moreover, these spaces have equivalent norms, and the constants in the equivalences depend only on the parameters involved in defining the Hardy spaces.

By the parameters involved in defining the Hardy spaces, we mean, for instance, the integers
M
and
N
in the definition of the atomic spaces, or the angle
\beta
of a cone, or the functions
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(1)}
involved in the Lusin area function, the square function and the maximal function definitions.
In light of this theorem, we may use the notation
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to denote any of these spaces.

Applications

First, to connect our results with previous work, we relate our space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
with the Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{HLS}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
of Han, Lu and Sawyer
[
30
]
.
We use the reflection operator
\mathcal{R}
, which acts on functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
by composing with the inversion:
\mathcal{R}f(g)=f(g^{-1})
for all
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.

Theorem B
.

The reflection
\mathcal{R}
is a bicontinuous linear isomorphism from
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{HLS}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Hence the complex interpolation space
[\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}),\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})]_{\theta}
may be identified with
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1/p=1-\theta/2
, and the dual space of
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
may be identified with
\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

The space
\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
will be defined, and this theorem will be proved, in Section 8.2.

Next, Folland and Stein
[
24
]
defined a one-parameter Hardy space on stratified Lie groups, and characterised it by Littlewood–Paley theory, atomic and molecular decompositions, and tangential and nontangential maximal functions.
Later Christ and Geller
[
10
]
characterised this Hardy space by singular integrals, and in particular by Riesz transforms.
We denote this one-parameter Hardy space by
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Theorem C
.

The space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is a proper subspace of
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

This will be proved in Section 8.3.

Third, the Phong–Stein singular integral operator
\mathcal{K}
arises in solving the
\bar{\partial}
-Neumann problem in a bounded smooth domain in
\mathbb{C}^{\nu+1}
(see
[
50
]
,
[
43
, Section 5]
or
[
54
]
).
It is a convolution operator, that is,
\mathcal{K}f=f*k_{PS}
, whose convolution kernel
k_{PS}
is given by

(0.6)

\displaystyle k_{PS}(z,t)=\frac{\omega(z)}{(|z|^{2}+t^{2})^{\nu}}\frac{1}{|z|^{2}+\mathrm{i}t}\qquad\forall(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu},

where
\omega
is homogeneous of degree
0
on
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
, smooth away from the origin and with mean-value
0
on the unit sphere.
Stein
[
54
]
proved that
\mathcal{K}
is bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p<\infty
by a lifting and projection argument.
The cancellation of
\omega
is only used to show the
\mathsf{L}^{2}
boundedness of
\mathcal{K}
(using the Cotlar–Stein almost orthogonality estimate
[
50
, Theorem 4]
).

Another important singular integral operator on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is the Cauchy–Szegõ projection
\mathcal{C}
, which gives an analytic function in the Siegel upper half space in terms of its boundary value.
Its restriction to the boundary is a convolution operator, that is,
\mathcal{C}(f)=f*k_{CS}
, and the convolution kernel
k_{CS}
is given by

(0.7)

\displaystyle k_{CS}(z,t)=\frac{c}{(|z|^{2}+\mathrm{i}t)^{\nu+1}}\qquad\forall(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}

(see
[
53
, Chapter 12, Section 2.4]
).
These two operators are examples of general flag singular integral operators, which were discussed in much great generality by Nagel, Ricci, Stein and Wainger
[
47
,
48
]
.

In Section 8.4, we use the atomic decomposition to show that general flag singular integral operators, which we define later, are bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Theorem D
.

General flag singular integral operators are bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Fourth, we define the two-parameter Sobolev space
\mathsf{L}_{\alpha,\beta}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
to be the collection of tempered distributions
\mathrm{M}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
for which the following norm is finite:

(0.8)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{M}\right\|_{L_{\alpha,\beta}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}:=\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(1+|\xi_{1}|)^{\alpha}(1+|\xi_{1}|+|\xi_{2}|)^{\beta}|\hat{\mathrm{M}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\right)^{1/2};

here
\hat{\mathrm{M}}
denotes the usual Fourier transform of
\mathrm{M}
.

Define
\mathrm{M}^{r,s}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\mathrm{M}(r\xi_{1},s\xi_{2})
.
Choose
\mathrm{H}
in
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
with support in
(1/2,2)

such that
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{H}(\cdot/{2^{j}})=1
, and set
\mathrm{H}_{1,1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\mathrm{H}(\xi_{1})\mathrm{H}(|\xi_{2}|)
.
Then the following result on the endpoint boundedness of Marcinkiewicz multipliers on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is sharp.

Theorem E
.

If
\alpha>\nu
and
\beta>1/2
, and the function
\mathrm{M}
satisfies the condition

\sup_{r,s>0}\left\|\mathrm{H}_{1,1}\mathrm{M}^{r,s}\right\|_{L_{\alpha,\beta}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<\infty,

then the joint spectral multiplier
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
is bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

The
\mathsf{L}^{p}
boundedness of
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
when
1<p<\infty
was proved in
[
44
]
.
The proof of this theorem will be given in Section 8.5.

Fix a nontrivial, smooth, nonnegative-real-valued function
\mathrm{H}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
with support in
[1/2,2]
, and an index
\alpha>(2\nu+1)/2
.
Write
\mathsf{L}^{2}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
for the usual Sobolev space on
\mathbb{R}
, whose norm is defined by

\left\|\mathrm{M}\right\|_{L_{\alpha}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}:=\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|\xi|)^{\alpha}\hat{\mathrm{M}}(\xi_{1})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\xi\right)^{1/2}.

Müller and Stein
[
45
]
and Hebisch
[
31
]
showed that the Hörmander multiplier
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)})
is bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
for
1<p<\infty
, if
\sup_{t>0}\|\mathrm{H}(\cdot)\mathrm{M}(t\cdot)\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty
.

An interesting corollary of Theorem E is that the one-parameter Hörmander multiplier
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)})
is also bounded on our multiparameter flag Hardy space and on the Folland–Stein–Christ–Geller Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Corollary F
.

Suppose that
\alpha>(2\nu+1)/2
, and the multiplier
\mathrm{M}
satisfies the condition
\sup_{t>0}\|\mathrm{H}(\cdot)\mathrm{M}(t\cdot)\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty
; then
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)})
is bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

By interpolation and duality,
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)})
is bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p<\infty
.
This gives another proof of a result of Müller and Stein
[
45
]
and of Hebisch
[
31
]
.

Next, from
[
30
, Definition 6 and Theorem 7]
and the isomorphism of our space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
with
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{HLS}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, it follows the dual space of
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is the space
\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, which is defined using a “flag Carleson measure condition” (see Section
7.2
).
From Theorems A and B, we obtain a decomposition of
\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Theorem G
.

A function
b\in\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
if and only if there exist (vector-valued) functions
h_{0}
,
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
and
h_{3}
in
\mathsf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that

b=h_{0}+\nabla_{(1)}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-1/2}(h_{1})+\nabla_{(2)}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-1/2}(h_{2})+\mathcal{R}_{F}(h_{3}).

Finally, we note that there are examples of convolution operators with kernels of the form (
0.6
) that are not of weak type
(1,1)
.
It is therefore natural to explore other weak type end-point estimates.

R. Fefferman
[
19
]
showed that Calderón–Zygmund operators in the product setting are bounded from
\mathsf{L}\log^{+}\mathsf{L}
to the weak
\mathsf{L}^{1}
space
\mathsf{L}^{1,\infty}
.
This is related to Zygmund’s conjecture for maximal operators in the general multiparameter setting
[
8
]
.
Our result on the domination of area function by the non-tangential maximal function (Theorem
5.9
) and our new techniques for establishing a flag atomic decomposition (Theorem
3.6
) enable us to prove a similar result in the flag context.

Theorem
.

The singular integral operators of Theorem D and the Marcinkiewicz
multiplier operators of Theorem E are bounded from
\mathsf{L}\log^{+}\mathsf{L}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{L}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

For reasons of space, we shall treat this case in another paper.

New tools and techniques

The Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is noncommutative, with a more complicated geometry and Fourier transformation than the product Euclidean setting
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
of previous results
[
28
]
on flag Hardy spaces.
To overcome these obstacles, we have developed some new tools and techniques, which may be helpful in solving related problems on the Heisenberg groups or more general stratified groups.

First, in the classical theory of Hardy spaces, the Cauchy–Riemann equations are often used.
However, it is not clear whether such systems of equations are available in the context of the Heisenberg group, or more general contexts.
We find two different ways to avoid the use of these equations.
In the classical case of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, Fefferman and Stein
[
16
]
used the Cauchy–Riemann equations to show the nontangential maximal function dominates the Lusin area function, while in the product setting
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the usual tool is Merryfield’s lemma
[
42
]
, which states that for every even
\varphi\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\int\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1
, the function
\psi\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, given by
\psi(x)=x\varphi(x)
, has the same support as
\varphi
, and further
\int\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0
and
\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}(x)=\partial_{x}\psi_{t}(x)
, where
\varphi_{t}(x)=t^{-1}\varphi(x/t)
and
\psi_{t}(x)=t^{-1}\psi(x/t)
.
It is unclear whether this lemma extends to the Heisenberg group or more general contexts.
Our approach bypasses the use of this construction and of the Fourier transformation, and hence it may be used in more general settings such as stratified Lie groups.

Similarly, the standard proof of the characterisation of Hardy space by Riesz transforms uses the radial maximal function and a Cauchy–Riemann type equation.
As before, the availability of such an equation is unclear in our setting.
Our new method dominates the flag Littlewood–Paley square function by the flag Riesz transform, by combining the singular integral characterisation of Christ and Geller with a randomisation argument, based on the Khinchin inequality.
We expect that our method may be applied to singular integral characterisations of Hardy spaces in various settings.
Even in the classical setting of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, these methods are new.

Again in the euclidean context, it is known that various maximal functions characterise the classical Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
These include maximal functions
\sup_{t}|\varphi_{t}*f|
, where
\varphi
is normalised and has decay properties that are too restrictive to apply to the Poisson kernel, or when
\varphi
is the Poisson kernel.
To show that the maximal function defined using the Poisson kernel
p
is connected to the maximal function using, say, a Schwartz function
\varphi
involves some special properties of the Poisson kernel, namely the analyticity of the Fourier transform
\hat{p}
(see
[
16
, p. 187]
or the existence of an asymptotic expansion at infinity for
p
(see
[
53
, p. 99]
).
Alternatively, a more brutal approach due to Uchiyama
[
59
]
may be used.
We use a new method of showing that “approximate identities are more or less alike” (quoted from
[
16
, p. 184]
) that allows us to do this with more general approximate identities that decay like the Poisson kernel, but do not have its special properties.
Since our approach does not involve the Fourier transform in an essential way, it should be applicable in other situations.

Second, in classical harmonic analysis on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, dyadic decompositions play an important role; one key feature of these is that each dyadic cube may be written as a disjoint union of
2^{n}
congruent cubes, each similar to the parent cube.
In product harmonic analysis on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, an analogous role is played by dyadic rectangles.
In particular, in product Hardy space, maximal dyadic subrectangles of open sets are used to index the particles that make up an atom.
There is a similar structure on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, as observed by Strichartz
[
57
]
, but the sets involved are rather irregular; further, there are nilpotent Lie groups where such a structure cannot exist.
To emphasize the analogy with the product space case, we defined atoms which are sums of particles associated to maximal “subshards” of an open set, but we also show that it would suffice to consider particles supported in arbitrary tubes and indexed arbitrarily.
This means that our approach is applicable in more general situations where “maximal subshards” are not at our disposition.

As part of our investigation into the definition of atoms, we clarify the sense in which an atom is a sum of particles, by requiring
unconditional convergence in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Some previous works on Hardy spaces in the product or flag setting required cumbersome estimates on expressions such as

\left\|\left(\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{R}}\left|r^{2m-2M}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}h^{2n-2N}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{n}b_{R}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})},

whenever
0\leq m\leq M
and
0\leq n\leq N
, but our approach enables us to avoid this.
In particular, we are able to give a very straightforward criterion (Proposition
2.16
) for the boundedness of a linear (or sublinear) operator from
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
that involves the action of the operator on particles, not on atoms.

Third, we define the Heisenberg group in an unusual way.
In dealing with classical Hardy spaces, the basic geometry is determined by cubes rather than by euclidean balls, and in our situation a similar geometry is appropriate.
We use an
\ell^{\infty}
rather than an
\ell^{2}
distance to achieve this.
To obtain a true distance, rather than a quasidistance, we need an unusual parametrisation of the group structure.

Further, to handle the geometry of the flag structure, we have to deal simultaneously with several metrics.
For example, we deal with two heat kernels: one on the Heisenberg group, which may be estimated in terms of the Korányi distance, and another in just the central variable, which involves a Euclidean distance.
This leads to various complications, such as decompositions into sets defined using two metrics.
To characterise the boundedness of singular integral on an individual atom, we use the translation and dilation on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
to simply and reduce the estimate to the case of a particle
a_{R}
which is supported in a shard
R
centred at the origin of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and of width
1
.
Then the decomposition of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
into annuli is straightforward.
However, a direct decomposition of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
into annuli for an arbitrary shard
R
is also feasible by combining the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and the Korányi metric on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
This allows us to handle singular integrals that are not convolutions and further development on flag Hardy spaces associated with more general operators.
We discuss this in more depth in Section 8.1.

The fundamental tools to prove our results on Marcinkiewicz type multipliers, are the atomic decomposition and the auxiliary weight
w_{j,\ell}^{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
introduced by Müller, Ricci and Stein
[
44
]
:

w_{j,\ell}^{\varepsilon}(z,t):=2^{-\nu(j+\ell)}(1+2^{j+\ell}|z|^{2})^{\nu(1+\varepsilon)}2^{-\ell}(1+2^{\ell}|t|)^{1+\varepsilon}\qquad\forall(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

We need to see the interplay of
w_{j,\ell}^{\varepsilon}
with a particle
a_{R}
associated to a shard
R
.
The difficulty here is that the decomposition of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
into shards is adapted to the Korányi metric of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
while the weight
w_{j,\ell}^{\varepsilon}
is described in terms of the Euclidean metrics in
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
\mathbb{R}
(i.e.,
|z|
and
|t|
).
In
[
44
]
, this was handled by using iterated one-dimensional maximal functions; these are bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
but not on our Hardy space.
We use refined decompositions of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
to overcome this difficulty.

Plan of the paper

In Section
1
, we discuss the Heisenberg group, and prove or summarise some preliminary results.
In Section
2
, we examine the definition of the atomic Hardy space in detail, and show that the (usually sublinear) operators that define the other Hardy spaces are all bounded on the atomic space, thereby proving one half of many of the equivalences of Theorem A.
In Section
3
, we consider the definition of the area function Hardy space and conclude that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
In Section
4
, we study the square function Hardy spaces in detail and complete the proof that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{cts}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
In Section
5
, we examine the maximal function Hardy spaces.
At this point, we know that

\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{gmax}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{nontan}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{radial}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu});

we show that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{nontan}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and outline the proof that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{nontan}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{radial}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
coincide; the proof of this last fact is similar to the corresponding proof in the product space setting of
[
28
, Subsection 3.2]
.
We complete the proof of Theorem A by showing that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{Riesz}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and briefly discuss possible extensions in Section
6
.
Applications and their proofs appear in Section
7
.

Acknowledgements:

It is a pleasure to thank Alessio Martini, Jill Pipher, Fulvio Ricci and an anonymous referee for many helpful comments.

1.
Preliminaries on the Heisenberg group

In this section, we summarise relevant facts on the Heisenberg group and its geometry.
The center of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is
\{(0,t):t\in\mathbb{R}\}
, and the canonical projection of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
onto
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
“forgets” the variable
t
.

1.1.
Notation

Recall that the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is parametrised by
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}
; a typical element is written
g
, or in coordinates as
(z,t)
where
z\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
We may also write
(z,t)
as
(z_{1},\dots,z_{\nu},t)
, or as
(x,y,t)
, or as
(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu},y_{1},\dots,y_{\nu},t)
.
The multiplication law is

(1.1)

(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})\cdot(z,t)=(z^{\prime}+z,t^{\prime}+t+S(z^{\prime},z)),

where

S(z^{\prime},z)=4\nu\operatorname{Im}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}z^{\prime}_{j}\bar{z}_{j}\biggr)=4\nu\left(\langle y^{\prime},x\rangle-\langle x^{\prime},y\rangle\right)=4\nu\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}y^{\prime}_{j}x_{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}x^{\prime}_{j}y_{j}\biggr).

The identity of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is written
o
or
(0,0)
; inversion is given by
(z,t)^{-1}=(-z,-t)
.
The reflection operator
\mathcal{R}
is defined on functions, and we may write
(\mathcal{R}f)(g)=f(g^{-1})
or
(\mathcal{R}f)(z,t)=f(-z,-t)
.
We say that
f
is
even
if
\mathcal{R}f=f
and
odd
if
\mathcal{R}f=-f
.

The Haar measure on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is the Lebesgue measure, which we write
\,\mathrm{d}g
or
\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}t
.
The standard Heisenberg group convolution is given by the formula

f_{1}\ast_{(1)}f_{2}(g)=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}f_{1}(g_{1})f_{2}(g_{1}^{-1}g)\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}f_{1}(gg_{1})f_{2}(g_{1}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

For reasonable input functions, convolution is associative but not necessarily commutative.
However, it is commutative on the space of
radial functions
, by which we mean the subspace of functions that are radial in the
z
variable (see
[
35
]
).
If
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
both have compact support, then
\operatorname{supp}(f_{1}\ast_{(1)}f_{2})\subseteq\operatorname{supp}(f_{1})\operatorname{supp}(f_{2})
.
We will also say that a function
f
on
\mathbb{R}
is radial if it is even.

We also convolve a function
f_{1}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
with a function
f_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}
as follows:

f_{1}\ast_{(2)}f_{2}(g)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(gg_{2})f_{2}(g_{2}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

Observe that, for suitable functions
f
and
f_{1}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
f_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle f\ast_{(1)}f_{1}\ast_{(2)}f_{2}(g)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(gg_{1})f_{1}(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2})f_{2}(g_{2}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(gg_{2}g_{1})f_{1}(g_{1}^{-1})f_{2}(g_{2}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(gg_{1}g_{2})f_{1}(g_{1}^{-1})f_{2}(g_{2}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1},

since
g_{2}
is central.
If
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are even functions, then we may write

(1.2)

f\ast_{(1)}f_{1}\ast_{(2)}f_{2}(g)=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(gg_{1}g_{2})f_{1}(g_{1})f_{2}(g_{2})\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

For
f_{1}\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
f_{2}\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we define the adjoints
f_{1}^{*}
and
f_{2}^{*}
by
f_{1}^{*}(g_{1})=\bar{f}_{1}(g_{1}^{-1})
and
f_{2}^{*}(g_{2})=\bar{f}_{2}(g_{2}^{-1})
.
Equipped with their usual norms, convolution, and adjunction,
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, are Banach
∗
-algebras; in particular, the
\mathsf{L}^{1}
convolution inequality
\left\|f\ast f^{\prime}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}}\left\|f^{\prime}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}}
holds when the convolution is defined (for instance, if
f_{1}\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
,
f_{2}\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and the convolution is
\ast_{(2)}
).

We recall that the dilations
D_{r}
, where
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, given by
D_{r}(z,t):=(rz,r^{2}t)
, are automorphisms of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
So are the rotations in
\mathrm{U}(n)
, acting on the right in the
z
variable and leaving
t
fixed.
Rotations are measure preserving, while
|D_{r}(E)|=r^{\delta}|E|
for every measurable subset
E
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and every
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
; here,
|E|
denotes the measure of a subset
E
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\delta
the
homogeneous dimension

2\nu+2
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
The normalised dilates
\varphi^{(1)}_{r}
and
\varphi^{(2)}_{s}
of functions
\varphi^{(1)}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
on
\mathbb{R}
are given by

\varphi^{(1)}_{r}(z,t):=r^{-\delta}\varphi^{(1)}(z/r,t/r^{2})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varphi^{(2)}_{2}(t):=s^{-1}\varphi^{(2)}(t/s),

for all
r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, all
(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then

[\varphi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)}]_{r}=\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)}_{r}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varphi^{(2)}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(2)}]_{s}=\varphi^{(2)}_{s}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(2)}_{s}

whenever the convolutions are well defined.

We identify vector fields with the associated first order differential operators.
The Lie algebra of left-invariant vector fields on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is spanned by the fields

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}+4\nu y_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\qquad\mathcal{Y}_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}-4\nu x_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t},

where
j=1,\dots,\nu
.
To unify some of the formulae, when
\nu+1\leq j\leq 2\nu
, we write
x_{j}
for
y_{j-\nu}
,
y_{j}
for
-x_{j-\nu}
, and
\mathcal{X}_{j}
for
\mathcal{Y}_{j-\nu}
when
j=\nu+1,\dots,2\nu
.
The vector fields
\mathcal{X}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{X}_{2\nu}
are called horizontal, while
\mathcal{T}
is called vertical.

We note that
\mathcal{X}_{j}f
may be considered as the convolution
f\ast_{(1)}\Upsilon_{j}
, where the distribution
\Upsilon_{j}
is given by
\Upsilon_{j}(f)=\partial_{j}f(0)
.
The right invariant vector field
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}
that coincides with
\mathcal{X}_{j}
at the identity
o
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is given by (
1.3
), but with
4\nu
and
-4\nu
exchanged.
Alternatively,
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}f=-\mathcal{R}\mathcal{X}_{j}\mathcal{R}f
.
Note that
\mathcal{X}(\varphi*\psi)=\varphi*\mathcal{X}\psi
and

(\mathcal{X}\varphi)*\psi=\varphi*\Upsilon_{j}*\psi=\varphi*\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{X}}\psi.

The vector field
\mathcal{T}
is both left and right invariant, and we may write

\mathcal{T}f
as a convolution with a distribution on either side.

We define the sub-Laplacian
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
to be
-\sum_{j=1}^{2\nu}\mathcal{X}_{j}^{2}
and the Laplacian
\mathcal{L}_{(2)}
to be
-\mathcal{T}^{2}
; the latter only involves the central variable.
Then
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
and
\mathcal{L}_{(2)}
are radial distributions.
We write
\nabla_{(1)}
,
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}
and
\nabla_{(2)}
for the left and right invariant horizontal and vertical gradients, that is,
(\mathcal{X}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{X}_{2\nu})
,
(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\dots,\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{X}}_{2\nu})
and
\mathcal{T}
.
We use higher gradients, such as
\nabla_{(1)}^{q}
and
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{q}
, where
q\in\mathbb{N}
, which are tensors;
\nabla_{(1)}^{0}
means the identity operator.

Note that
\nabla_{(1)}
and
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}
are homogeneous of degree
1
with respect to the dilations
D_{r}
, while
\nabla_{(2)}
and
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
are homogeneous of degree
2
, and
\mathcal{L}_{(2)}
is homogeneous of degree
4
, in the sense that

\displaystyle\nabla_{(1)}\left(f\circ D_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=r\,(\nabla_{(1)}f)\circ D_{r},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\left(f\circ D_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{2}\,(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}f)\circ D_{r},

\displaystyle\nabla_{(2)}\left(f\circ D_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{2}\,(\nabla_{(2)}f)\circ D_{r},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{(2)}\left(f\circ D_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{4}\,(\mathcal{L}_{(2)}f)\circ D_{r},

when
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
f\in\mathsf{C}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

The space of Schwartz functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, written
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, is the space of functions
f
such that
p\mathcal{D}f
vanishes at infinity for all polynomials
p
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and all left-invariant differential operators
\mathcal{D}
.

In the context of classical Hardy spaces, it is natural to focus on cubes rather than balls, and an analogous comment applies in our setting.
There are various left-invariant distances in use on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
; the most common are the
control (or Carnot–Carathéodory) distance

d_{c}
, and the
Korányi distance

d_{K}
.
We shall use the
gauge distance

d_{(1)}
, which is defined by setting

(1.4)

\displaystyle d_{(1)}(g,g^{\prime}):=\left\|g^{\prime-1}g\right\|=\left\|g^{-1}g^{\prime}\right\|\qquad\forall g,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu},

where
\left\|{}\cdot{}\right\|
is given by

(1.5)

\displaystyle\left\|(z,t)\right\|:=\max\left\{|x_{1}|,|y_{1}|,\dots,|x_{\nu}|,|y_{\nu}|,|t|^{1/2}\right\}\qquad\forall(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

See
[
58
, Section 2.2]
for a discussion, and note that we have defined the group multiplication in an unusual way to ensure that our norm satisfies the triangle inequality; our norm also behaves like the usual
\ell^{\infty}
norm in
\mathbb{R}^{2\nu+1}
.
We write
B^{(1)}(g,r)
for the ball in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
with centre
g
and radius
r
constructed using the distance
d
.
We also use balls in the centre of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, which may be identified with
\mathbb{R}
: we define
B^{(2)}(t,s):=\{t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}:|t-t^{\prime}|<s\}
.
Sets of the form
gB^{(1)}(o,r)B^{(2)}(0,s)
occur often in our work: these are images of products of balls in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}
under the identification mentioned in the introduction.
We call them
tubes
, and define
T(g,r,s)
by the formula

(1.6)

T(g,r,s):=gB^{(1)}(o,r)B^{(2)}(0,s)=B^{(1)}(g,r)B^{(2)}(0,s).

Thus
T(o,r,s)
may be identified with the set
\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}:|z|_{\infty}<r\}\times(-r^{2}-s,r^{2}+s)
.

By definition, the following conditions are equivalent: first,
g^{\prime}\in T(g,r,s)
; second,
g^{\prime}\in gB^{(1)}(o,r)B^{(2)}(0,s)
; third,
g\in g^{\prime}B^{(1)}(o,r)B^{(2)}(0,s)
; and fourth,
g\in T(g^{\prime},r,s)
.

Remark 1.1
.

Consider the question of finding the smallest closed tube
\bar{T}(o,r,s)
centred at
o
that contains a point
g
in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
In general, if
g=(z,t)
, then we require that
r\geq|z|_{\infty}
,
s\geq 0
and
s+r^{2}\geq|t|
; when
|t|>|z|_{\infty}
, there are infinitely many minimal tubes with this property, but the smallest by volume is well-determined: if
g=(z,t)
, then
r=|z|_{\infty}
and
s=\max\{|t|-r^{2},0\}
.

A smooth curve
\gamma
in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is said to be
horizontal
if its tangent vector is a linear combination of the
\mathcal{X}_{j}
at each point, and the
control (or Carnot–Carathéodory) distance

d_{c}(g,g^{\prime})
between points
g
and
g^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is defined to be the infimum of the lengths of horizontal curves joining
g
and
g^{\prime}
.
We define the
control norm

\left\|{}\cdot{}\right\|_{c}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
by

\left\|g\right\|_{c}:=d_{c}(e,g)\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

The distance
d_{c}
is left-invariant, that is,
d_{c}(gg^{\prime},gg^{\prime\prime})=d_{c}(g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime})
for all
g,g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, whence

\displaystyle d_{c}((z,t),(z^{\prime},t^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\left\|(z,t)^{-1}\cdot(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right\|_{c}.

Working with the control norm and distance is not easy, and so we often replace it with equivalent norms and distances that are computationally easier.
One such is the
Korányi distance

d_{K}
, given by

(1.7)

\displaystyle d_{K}(g,g^{\prime})=\left\|g^{\prime-1}g\right\|_{K}=\left\|g^{-1}g^{\prime}\right\|_{K}\qquad\forall g,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu},

where the
Korányi norm

\left\|{}\cdot{}\right\|_{K}
(with our definitions) is given by

(1.8)

\displaystyle\left\|(z,t)\right\|_{K}:=(\|z\|^{4}+4\nu^{2}t^{2})^{1/4}\qquad\forall(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

We do not need much about all these distances on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, other than their equivalence.

Because our vector fields and distances are left-invariant, it is necessary to use right convolutions with, for example, the flag Poisson kernel in the definition of the nontangential and radial maximal functions.
This creates a small but important difference between our work and that of Han, Lu and Sawyer
[
30
]
, who used left convolutions.
Reflecting functions (that is, composing with the inversion) exchanges left and right convolutions, so that in the end, the differences are minor, and we may relate our Hardy spaces to theirs by a reflection.

1.2.
Tilings and shards

Following
[
30
]
, we use the work of
[
57
,
58
]
on self-similar tilings to find a “nice” decomposition of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, analogous to the decomposition of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
into dyadic cubes in classical harmonic analysis.
We identify
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2\nu}
, and
|z|_{\infty}
denotes
\max\{|x_{1}|,|y_{1}|,\dots|x_{\nu}|,|y_{\nu}|\}
,
Q_{0}
denotes the cube
[-1/2,1/2)^{2\nu}
, while
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}_{\mathbb{Z}}
denotes the subgroup
\{(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2\nu},t\in(2\nu)^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\}
.
In this subsection, we write
P
for the canonical projection of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
onto
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
57
,
58
]
)
.

There is a Borel measurable function
f:Q_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f(0)=1/4\nu
and

\frac{1}{4\nu(\nu+1)}\leq f(z)\leq\frac{2\nu+1}{4\nu(\nu+1)}\qquad\forall z\in Q_{0},

such that the set
T_{o}
, defined by

T_{o}:=\left\{(z,t):z\in Q_{0},f(z)-\frac{1}{2\nu}\leq t<f(z)\right\},

has the property that

D_{2\nu+1}(T_{o})=\bigsqcup_{g\in\Delta}gT_{o},

where
\Delta:=\{(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}_{\mathbb{Z}}:|z|_{\infty}\leq\nu:|t|\leq\nu+1\}
.

The proof of this is essentially the content of
[
58
, Lemma 3.3]
.

The definitions of
T_{o}
and the metrics that we use show that

(1.9)

\begin{gathered}T_{o}\subset\left\{(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:|z|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2},|t|\leq\frac{2\nu+1}{4\nu(\nu+1)}\right\}\subset\left\{(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:|z|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2},|t|\leq\frac{3}{8}\right\}\\
\subseteq\bar{B}^{(1)}\left(o,\frac{1}{2}\right)\bar{B}^{(2)}\left(0,\frac{1}{8}\right)=\bar{T}\left(o,\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{8}\right)\,,\end{gathered}

where the bars indicate closures.
We may improve this to
T_{o}\subset\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,1/2)
, which is optimal, when
\nu\geq 2
.
Similarly,

(1.10)

T_{o}\supset\left\{(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:|z|_{\infty}<\frac{1}{2},|t|<\frac{1}{4\nu(\nu+1)}\right\}\supset B^{(1)}\left(o,\frac{1}{2(\nu+1)}\right);

the size of the largest open ball inside
T_{o}
cannot be controlled independently of
\nu
.

We observe that
(0,t)\in T_{0}
if and only if
t\in[-1/4\nu,1/4\nu)
, and in some respects we may think of
T_{o}
as a modified version of
Q_{0}\times[1/4\nu,1/4\nu)
.

Definition 1.3
.

We define

\mathfrak{T}_{0}:=\{gT_{o}:g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}_{\mathbb{Z}}\},\qquad\mathfrak{T}_{j}:=D_{(2\nu+1)^{j}}\mathfrak{T}_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{T}:=\bigsqcup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{T}_{j}.

We call the sets
T\in\mathfrak{T}

tiles
.
Then
T=D_{(2\nu+1)^{j}}(g)D_{(2\nu+1)^{j}}(T_{o})
if
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
T=D_{(2\nu+1)^{j}}(gT_{o})
.
We further define

\operatorname{c}(T):=D_{(2\nu+1)^{j}}(g),\qquad\operatorname{w}(T):=(2\nu+1)^{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{h}(T):=\frac{(2\nu+1)^{2j}}{2\nu}\,.

Recall that
\{0\}\times[-1/4\nu,1/4\nu)\subset T_{o}
.
If
g=(m,n/2\nu)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}
and
n=(2\nu)^{-1}l
, where
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

g(\{0\}\times[-1/4\nu,1/4\nu))=\{m\}\times[(2l-1)/4\nu,(2l+1)/4\nu).

We may view
gT_{0}
as a modified version of
(m+Q_{0})\times[(2l-1)/4\nu,(2l+1)/4\nu)
, at least in some respects; for instance, the measure of
gT_{0}
is equal to
1/2\nu
.
However, the projection of
gT_{0}
onto the vertical axis is an interval whose length may be much larger than
1/2\nu
, due to the term
S(z,m)
, where
z
varies over
[-1/2,1/2)^{2\nu}
, in the Heisenberg group multiplication (see (
1.1
)).

Theorem 1.4

(
[
30
,
58
,
57
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{T}
be defined as above.
Then the following hold:

(1)

for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathfrak{T}_{j}
is a partition of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, that is,
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}=\bigsqcup_{T\in\mathfrak{T}_{j}}T
;

(2)

\mathfrak{T}
is nested, that is, if
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{T}
, then either
T
and
T^{\prime}
are disjoint or one is a subset of the other;

(3)

B^{(1)}(g,C_{1}q)\subseteq T\subseteq B^{(1)}(g,C_{2}q)
, where
g=\operatorname{c}(T)
and
q=\operatorname{w}(T)
for each
T\in\mathfrak{T}
; the constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
depend only on
\nu
;

(4)

if
T\in\mathfrak{T}_{j}
, then
gT\in\mathfrak{T}_{j}
for all
g\in D_{(2\nu+1)^{j}}\mathbb{H}^{\nu}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
D_{(2\nu+1)^{k}}T\in\mathfrak{T}_{j+k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Every tile is a dilate and translate of the basic tile
T_{o}
, so all tiles are similar geometrically.
Hence each tile in
\mathfrak{T}_{j}
is a fractal set—its boundary is a set of Lebesgue measure
0
and (euclidean Hausdorff) dimension
2\nu
—and is “approximately” a Heisenberg ball of radius
(2\nu+1)^{j}
.
The decompositions are
product-like
in the sense that the tiles project
onto cubes in the factor
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
, and their centers form a product set.
They are like the usual dyadic decomposition into cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the sense that each tile may be written as the disjoint union of
(2\nu+1)^{\delta}
congruent tiles.
If two tiles in
\mathfrak{T}_{j}
are “horizontal neighbours”, then the distance between their centres is
(2\nu+1)^{j}
, while if they are “vertical neighbours”, then the (Heisenberg) distance is
(2\nu+1)^{2j}/2\nu
.

We say that a tile
T
is the
j
th ancestor of a tile
T^{\prime}
, or that
T^{\prime}
is a
j
th descendant of
T
, when
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
and
|T|/|T^{\prime}|=(2\nu+1)^{j\delta}
,

Han, Lu and Sawyer
[
30
]
used unions of tiles to pursue the analogy with rectangles in the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We follow them, but with different nomenclature to reflect the fact that our objects are fractal, and are not based on powers of
2
, but of
2\nu+1
.
Given tiles
T
and
T^{\prime}
, such that
T\subset T^{\prime}
, the projection
P(T)
of
T
onto
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
is a cube,
Q
say; let
R=P^{-1}(Q)\cap T^{\prime}
.
Then
R
is the (finite) union of the tiles
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{T}_{j}
such that
T^{\prime\prime}\subset T^{\prime}
and
P(T^{\prime\prime})=Q
.

Definition 1.5
.

The
shard

R
determined by tiles
T
and
T^{\prime}
, where
T\subseteq T^{\prime}
, is defined to be the set
P^{-1}(P(T))\cap T^{\prime}
.
Then
R
consists of
\operatorname{h}(T^{\prime})/\operatorname{h}(T)
copies of
T
stacked vertically.
The centre of
R
, written
\operatorname{c}(R)
, is the centre of the middle tile in the stack; the width of
R
, written
\operatorname{w}(R)
, is
\operatorname{w}(T)
and the height of
R
, written
\operatorname{h}(R)
, is
\operatorname{h}(T^{\prime})
.
The collection of all shards is denoted
\mathfrak{R}
.

We note that the collection of all shards is countable.
If two shards are not disjoint, their intersection is also a shard.
Shards are called vertical dyadic rectangles by Han, Lu and Sawyer
[
30
]
; they also define horizonal dyadic rectangles, but we do not use these.

Recall that the tube
T(g,r,s)
is the set
gB^{(1)}(o,r)B^{(2)}(0,s)
; evidently,

(1.11)

|T(g,r,s)|=2^{2\nu+1}r^{2\nu}(r^{2}+s).

Tubes are easier to handle than shards in some respects; for instance, the product of two tubes is a tube, but the product of two shards is not a shard.
However, there are no simple nested decompositions of space into disjoint tubes.
So we need to be able to compare tubes and shards.

Lemma 1.6
.

If
R\in\mathfrak{R}
and
\operatorname{c}(R)=g
,
\operatorname{w}(R)=q
and
\operatorname{h}(R)=h
, then

(1.12)

R\subset\bar{T}(g,q/2,(4h+q^{2})/8).

Proof.

By part (4) of Theorem
1.4
, (
1.12
) is invariant under certain dilations and translations.
Hence we may assume that
\operatorname{c}(R)=o
,
\operatorname{w}(R)=1
, and
\operatorname{h}(R)=(2\nu+1)^{2j}/2\nu
for some
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.
In this case,
R
is made up of
(2\nu+1)^{j}
copies of
T_{o}
stacked vertically on each other, each of height
1/2\nu
; more precisely,

R=T_{o}\left[-\frac{(2\nu+1)^{j}-1}{4\nu},\frac{(2\nu+1)^{j}-1}{4\nu}\right).

Now (
1.12
) follows easily from (
1.9
).
∎

In the case where
R
is not a tile, the result of Lemma
1.6
may be sharpened to

T(g,q/2,h/4)\subset R\subset\bar{T}(g,q/2,h/2).

When we consider atomic Hardy spaces, we will want to consider functions supported in open sets, which may be written as sum of functions supported in enlargements of maximal shards of the open set.

Definition 1.7
.

Fix
\kappa\in(1+1/(2\nu),\infty)
.
The
enlargement

R^{*,\kappa}
, often written
R^{*}
, of a shard
R
is the tube
T(g,\kappa q/2,\kappa^{2}(4h+q^{2})/8)
, where
g=\operatorname{c}(R)
,
q=\operatorname{w}(R)
, and
h=\operatorname{h}(R)
.

Lemma 1.8
.

For all
R\in\mathfrak{R}
and
\kappa\in(1+1/(2\nu),\infty)
,

(1.13)

R\subset R^{*,\kappa}.

Further, when
\kappa\geq(2\nu+1)^{2}
, given any tube
T
, there exists
R\in\mathfrak{R}
such that

(1.14)

R\subseteq T\subseteq R^{*,\kappa}.

Proof.

Lemma
1.6
and the definition of an enlargement imply (
1.13
) immediately.

The assertion (
1.14
) is the
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
version of the fact that every interval
[a,b)
in
\mathbb{R}
contains an interval with centre
(2\nu+1)^{j}k
and width
(2\nu+1)^{j}
such that
[a,b)
is contained in the interval with the same centre and width
(2\nu+1)^{j+2}
; it suffices to take
j=\lfloor\log_{2\nu+1}(b-a)\rfloor-1
and
k=\lceil\log_{2\nu+1}a\rceil+1
.
∎

For future reference, we note that, if
R
is a shard,
\operatorname{c}(R)=g
,
\operatorname{w}(R)=q
and
\operatorname{h}(R)=h
, and
R^{\ddagger}
is the shard with
\operatorname{c}(R^{\ddagger})=g
,
\operatorname{w}(R^{\ddagger})=q
and
\operatorname{h}(R^{\ddagger})=(2\nu+1)^{2}h
, then

(1.15)

\begin{gathered}R\subset\bar{T}(g,q/2,(4h+q^{2})/8)\subset R^{\ddagger},\\
R^{*}=T(g,\kappa q/2,\kappa^{2}(4h+q^{2})/8),\\
|R|=q^{2\nu}h,\quad|R^{\ddagger}|=(2\nu+1)^{2}|R|\\
\left|R^{*}\right|=\kappa^{\delta}q^{2\nu}(h+q^{2}/4)\leq\kappa^{\delta}(1+\nu/2)|R|,\end{gathered}

(the last inequality holds since
h\geq q^{2}/2\nu
).

We need one more geometric result to help us pass from tubes to shards.

Lemma 1.9
.

Suppose that
\kappa\in(1+1/(2\nu),\infty)
, and that
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
(2\nu+1)^{\ell-1}\geq\kappa
.
For all
R\in\mathfrak{R}
, let
R^{\mathrm{\dagger}}
denote the unique shard that contains
R
and is a translate of
D_{(2\nu+1)^{\ell}}R
.
Then

R\subset R^{*}\subset R^{\dagger}.

Proof.

By translating and dilating if necessary, we may suppose that
\operatorname{c}(R)=o
,
\operatorname{w}(R)=1
and
\operatorname{h}(R)=(2\nu+1)^{2k}
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
In this case,

R^{*}=T(o,\kappa/2,\kappa^{2}((2\nu+1)^{2k}+1)/8),

and it is easy to check that
D_{(2\nu+1)^{-\ell}}R^{*}\subset R
, whence
R^{*}\subset R^{\dagger}
.
∎

1.3.
Maximal functions and Journé’s lemma

In light of the results in the previous subsection, controlling averages over shards is essentially the same as controlling averages over tubes, and we use a maximal operator to do this.
Our “flag maximal operator” is “bigger” than the usual Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator, but “smaller” than the “strong maximal operator” used in
[
43
,
44
]
.
Recall that
\chi^{(1)}_{r}
and
\chi^{(2)}_{s}
denote the normalised characteristic functions of the unit balls in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and in
\mathbb{R}
, and that
\chi_{r,s}
is short for
\chi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\chi^{(2)}_{s}
.

Definition 1.10
.

We define the
flag maximal operator

\mathcal{M}_{F}
, the
shard maximal operator

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}
, and the
iterated maximal operator

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{it}}
by

(1.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{F}(f)(g)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{1}{|T(g,r,s)|}\int_{T(g,r,s)}|f(g^{\prime})|\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}

(1.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}(f)(g)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{g\in R\in\mathfrak{R}}\frac{1}{|R|}\int_{R}|f(g^{\prime})|\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}

(1.18)

\displaystyle\phantom{\bigg|}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{it}}(f)(g)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\right|\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}(g)\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

Lemma 1.11
.

Suppose that
f
is a Lebesgue measurable function on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
Then

\mathcal{M}_{F}(f)\eqsim\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}(f)\eqsim\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{it}}(f)
.
All three maximal operators are bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p\leq\infty
.

Proof.

We write
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
as
(z,t)
.
By definition,

(1.19)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|T(g,r,s)|}\int_{T(g,r,s)}\left|f(g^{\prime})\right|\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{C}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}}\left|f((z,t)\cdot(z^{\prime},t^{\prime}))\right|\,v_{(r,s)}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime},

where

v_{(r,s)}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{|B^{(1)}(o,r)B^{(2)}(0,s)|}\,\mathbbm{1}_{Q(0,r)}(z^{\prime})\,\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(2)}(0,r^{2}+s)}(t^{\prime}),

and similarly

(1.20)

\displaystyle|f|\ast_{(1)}\chi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\chi^{(2)}_{s})(g)

\displaystyle=\int_{B^{(1)}(o,r)}\int_{B^{(2)}(0,s)}\frac{1}{\left|B^{(1)}(o,r)\right|\left|B^{(2)}(0,s)\right|}\left|f(gg^{\prime}g^{\prime\prime})\right|\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime\prime}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{C}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}}\left|f((z,t)\cdot(z^{\prime},t^{\prime}))\right|\,w_{(r,s)}(t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime},

and

w_{(r,s)}(t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\left|B^{(1)}(o,r)\right|\left|B^{(2)}(0,s)\right|}\,\mathbbm{1}_{Q(0,r)}(z^{\prime})\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(2)}(0,r^{2})}(t^{\prime}+t^{\prime\prime})\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(2)}(0,s)}(t^{\prime\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime\prime}.

Now

\displaystyle\min\{r^{2},s\}\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(2)}(0,(r^{2}+s)/2)}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(2)}(0,r^{2})}(t^{\prime}+t^{\prime\prime})\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(2)}(0,s)}(t^{\prime\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 2\min\{r^{2},s\}\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(2)}(0,r^{2}+s)}(t^{\prime})

and

\displaystyle\frac{\min\{r^{2},s\}}{\left|B^{(1)}(o,r)\right|\left|B^{(2)}(0,s)\right|}

\displaystyle\eqsim\frac{\min\{r^{2},s\}}{r^{\delta}s}=\frac{1}{\max\{r^{2},s\}r^{2\nu}}

\displaystyle\eqsim\frac{1}{(r^{2}+s)r^{2\nu}}\eqsim\frac{1}{\left|B^{(1)}(o,r)B^{(2)}(0,s)\right|},

whence

v_{(r,s/2)}\lesssim w_{(r,s)}\lesssim v_{(r,s)},

and we complete the proof of the equivalence of
\mathcal{M}_{F}(f)
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{it}}(f)
by substituting these inequalities into (
1.19
) and (
1.20
), and then taking suprema.

The equivalence of
\mathcal{M}_{F}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}
follows from the second part of Lemma
1.8
.

Since
\left|f\right|\ast_{(1)}\chi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\chi^{(2)}_{s}=\left|f\right|\ast_{(2)}\chi^{(2)}_{s}\ast_{(1)}\chi^{(1)}_{r}
, the maximal operator
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{it}}
may be dominated by the composition of a Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator in the central variable with a Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, in either order.
It is now evident that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{it}}
and hence also
\mathcal{M}_{F}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}
are bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p\leq\infty
, and all are unbounded on
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
∎

Lemma 1.12
.

Let
R
be a shard.
Then
\mathcal{M}_{F}\mathbbm{1}_{R}(g^{\prime})\geq 2(5\nu+2)^{-1}\kappa^{-\delta}
for all
g^{\prime}\in R^{*,\kappa}
.

Proof.

Write
g=\operatorname{c}(R)
,
q=\operatorname{w}(R)
, and
h=\operatorname{h}(R)
.
Take
g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}\in R^{*}
.
By definition,

g^{\prime}\in gB^{(1)}(o,\kappa q/2)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/8),

whence

g\in g^{\prime}B^{(1)}(o,\kappa q/2)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/8),

and

\displaystyle g^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\in gB^{(1)}(o,\kappa q/2)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/8)

\displaystyle\subseteq g^{\prime}B^{(1)}(o,\kappa q/2)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h/8))B^{(1)}(o,\kappa q/2)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/8)

\displaystyle=g^{\prime}B^{(1)}(o,\kappa q)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/4).

Hence
R\subseteq R^{*}\subseteq g^{\prime}B^{(1)}(o,\kappa q)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/4)
, whence

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{F}\mathbbm{1}_{R}(g^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{|B^{(1)}(o,\kappa q)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/4)|}\int_{B^{(1)}(g^{\prime},\kappa q)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/4)}\mathbbm{1}_{R}(g^{\prime\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{|R|}{|B^{(1)}(o,\kappa q)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/4)|}=\frac{q^{2\nu}h}{2^{2\nu+1}(\kappa q)^{2\nu}\kappa^{2}(5q^{2}+4h)/4}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2^{2\nu}\kappa^{\delta}(5\nu+2)},

since
h\geq q^{2}/2\nu
.
∎

Lemma 1.13
.

Suppose that
E
is an open subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure
|E|
, and fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
Define

\tilde{E}_{\alpha}:=\bigl\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:\mathcal{M}_{F}(\mathbbm{1}_{E})(g)>\alpha\bigr\}.

Then

\left|\tilde{E}_{\alpha}\right|\lesssim\frac{\left|E\right|}{\alpha^{2}}\,.

Proof.

Since the flag maximal function
\mathcal{M}_{F}
is bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
,
