\left|\tilde{E}_{\alpha}\right|\leq\frac{\left\|\mathcal{M}_{F}\mathbbm{1}_{E}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}}{\alpha^{2}}\lesssim\frac{\left\|\mathbbm{1}_{E}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}=\frac{\left|E\right|}{\alpha^{2}}\,,

where the implicit constant depends only on
\nu
.
∎

We use this inequality to control the sizes of various supersets of a given set.

Definition 1.14
.

Suppose that
E
is an open subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure.
We write
\mathfrak{R}(E)
for the collection of all
R
in
\mathfrak{R}
whose interior is a subset of
E
, and
\mathfrak{M}(E)
for the collection of all maximal such
R
in
\mathfrak{R}(E)
.

We abuse language a little and call shards
R\in\mathfrak{R}(E)
subshards of
E
.
Each such subshard
R
has an enlargement
R^{*}
, and we can control the measure of
\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{R}(E)}R^{*}
.

Corollary 1.15
.

Suppose that
E
is an open subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure.
Then

(1.21)

\displaystyle\Bigl|\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{R}(E)}R^{*}\Bigr|\lesssim|E|.

The implicit constant depends only on the enlargement parameter
\kappa
and
\nu
.

Proof.

We take
\alpha
to be
2/(5\nu+2)
in Lemma
1.13
,
By the definitions and Lemma
1.12
,
\mathcal{M}_{F}\mathbbm{1}_{E}(g^{\prime})\geq\mathcal{M}_{F}\mathbbm{1}_{R}(g^{\prime})\geq 2(5\nu+2)^{-1}\kappa^{-\delta}
;
now (
1.21
) follows.
∎

For a shard
R
, there is a unique shard,
R^{\dagger}
say, that contains
R
and is a translate of
D_{2\nu+1}R
.
It is easy to check that, if the enlargement parameter
\kappa
in the definition of
R^{*}
is large enough, then
R^{\dagger}\subseteq R^{*}
.

Corollary 1.16
.

Suppose that
E
is an open subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure.
Then
\bigl|\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{R}(E)}R^{\dagger}\bigr|\lesssim|E|
.
The implicit constant depends only on
\nu
.

For
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there may be several
R^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}(\tilde{E}_{\alpha})
that contain
R
.
However, for a given width, there is a unique such shard; indeed, if
R\subseteq R^{\prime}\subseteq\tilde{E}_{\alpha}
and
R\subseteq R^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\tilde{E}_{\alpha}
, and
\operatorname{w}(R^{\prime})=\operatorname{w}(R^{\prime\prime})
, then the projections
PR^{\prime}
and
PR^{\prime\prime}
of
R^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime\prime}
onto
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
are
(2\nu+1)
-adic cubes of the same size that contain
PR
, so they must coincide, and then
R\subseteq R^{\prime}\cup R^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\tilde{E}
; if
R^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime\prime}
are both maximal, then
R^{\prime}=R^{\prime\prime}
.
Similarly, there is a unique maximal shard of maximal height; indeed, if there were two, one would be wider than the other, and since the heights coincide, the narrower shard would be properly contained in the wider shard.

We now recall Journé’s covering lemma, which was first proved by Journé
[
37
]
in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
, and later by Fefferman
[
18
,
19
]
and Pipher
[
49
]
in higher dimensions and with more factors.
It has been extended to products of spaces of homogeneous type; see, for example
[
29
]
.
The preliminary version of
[
30
]
stated a flag version of this covering lemma.
We give some more notation to state the version.

Definition 1.17
.

Let
E
be an open subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure, and let
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
be constants in
(0,1)
.
We define sets
E^{(1)}
and
E^{(2)}
(which also depend on
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
) as follows:

(1.22)

\displaystyle E^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left\{g\in G:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}\mathbbm{1}_{E}(g)>{\alpha_{1}}\right\}

\displaystyle E^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\left\{g\in G:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}\mathbbm{1}_{E^{(1)}}(g)>{\alpha_{2}}\right\}.

Given a shard
R
in
\mathfrak{M}(E)
, we define
R^{(1)}
to be the widest shard
S\in\mathfrak{M}(E^{(1)})
such that
R\subseteq S
, and
R^{(2)}
to be the highest shard
S\in\mathfrak{M}(E^{(2)})
such that
R^{(1)}\subseteq S
.
We write
\mathfrak{M}^{(1)}(E^{(1)})
for the collection of
R^{(1)}
that arise as
R
varies over
\mathfrak{M}(E)
.

If
R
and
S
are shards and
R\subseteq S
, and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, then we define

(1.23)

\displaystyle\rho_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(R,S):=\left(\frac{\operatorname{w}(R)}{\operatorname{w}(S)}\right)^{\varepsilon_{1}}+\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(R)}{\operatorname{h}(S)}\right)^{\varepsilon_{2}}.

Lemma 1.18
.

Suppose that
E
is an open subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure, and that
C_{1},C_{2}>1
.
Then it is possible to choose the constants
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
in Definition
1.17
small enough that

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{w}(R^{(1)})}{\operatorname{w}(R)}\geq C_{1}

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\frac{\operatorname{h}(R^{(1)})}{\operatorname{h}(R)}\geq 1

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{h}(R^{(2)})}{\operatorname{h}(R^{(1)})}\geq C_{2}

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\frac{\operatorname{h}(R^{(2)})}{\operatorname{h}(R^{(1)})}\geq 1

for all
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
.
Further, for all
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

(1.24)

\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\rho_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(R,R^{(2)})|R|\leq c|E|,

where
c
does not depend on
E
, but may depend on
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\varepsilon_{2}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
.

Proof.

First, take
\alpha_{1}
to be at most
(C_{1}+2)^{-\delta}
and
\alpha_{2}
to be at most
(C_{2}+2)^{-1}
.
Then it is clear that

\operatorname{w}(R^{(2)})\geq\operatorname{w}(R^{(1)})\geq C_{1}\operatorname{w}(R)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\operatorname{h}(R^{(2)})\geq C_{2}\operatorname{h}(R^{(1)})\geq\operatorname{h}(R).

Next, there is no loss of generality in assuming that
E
is a finite union
\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{F}}R
of shards, each of which is maximal in
E
(provided that our estimate is independent of the cardinality of the collection
\mathfrak{F}
of shards).
Then the finitely many projected sets
P(R)
, as
R
varies over
\mathfrak{F}
, are cubes in
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
, and by the translation and dilation invariance of (
1.24
), we may and shall suppose that all these cubes are subcubes of the cube
Q_{0}:=[-1/2,1/2)^{2\nu}
.

Next, given such a set
E
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, let
\tilde{E}_{\alpha}
be
\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}\mathbbm{1}_{E}(g)>\alpha\}
, let
R^{(w)}
be the widest shard
S\in\mathfrak{M}(\tilde{E}_{\alpha})
such that
R\subseteq S
and
R^{(h)}
be the highest shard
S\in\mathfrak{M}(\tilde{E}_{\alpha})
such that
R\subseteq S
.
We claim that, for all
\varepsilon
in
\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

(1.25)

\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\left(\frac{\operatorname{w}(R)}{\operatorname{w}(R^{(w)})}\right)^{\varepsilon}|R|\lesssim_{\varepsilon}|E|\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(R)}{\operatorname{h}(R^{(h)})}\right)^{\varepsilon}|R|\lesssim_{\varepsilon}|E|.

One the one hand,
R^{(w)}\subseteq R^{(2)}
(as defined in Definition
1.17
), and so the first inequality of our claim (
1.25
) implies that

\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\left(\frac{\operatorname{w}(R)}{\operatorname{w}(R^{(2)})}\right)^{\varepsilon_{1}}|R|\lesssim_{\varepsilon_{1}}|E|.

On the other hand, since shards in
\mathfrak{M}(E)
of the same height are either disjoint or coincide, the second inequality of our claim, applied to
E^{(1)}
rather than to
E
, shows that

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(R)}{\operatorname{h}(R^{(2)})}\right)^{\varepsilon_{2}}|R|

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathfrak{M}(E^{(1)})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)\\
R^{(1)}=S\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(S)}{\operatorname{h}(R^{(2)})}\right)^{\varepsilon_{2}}\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(R)}{\operatorname{h}(S)}\right)^{\varepsilon_{2}}|R|

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathfrak{M}(E^{(1)})}\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(S)}{\operatorname{h}(R^{(2)})}\right)^{\varepsilon_{2}}\sum_{j_{2}\in\mathbb{N}}(2\nu+1)^{-\varepsilon_{2}j_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)\\
R^{(1)}=S\\
\operatorname{h}(R)=(2\nu+1)^{-j_{2}}\operatorname{h}(S)\end{subarray}}|R|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{S\in\mathfrak{M}(E^{(1)})}\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(S)}{\operatorname{h}(S^{(h)})}\right)^{\varepsilon_{2}}\sum_{j_{2}\in\mathbb{N}}(2\nu+1)^{-\varepsilon_{2}j_{2}}|S|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon_{2}}\sum_{S\in\mathfrak{M}(E^{(1)})}\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(S)}{\operatorname{h}(S^{(h)})}\right)^{\varepsilon_{2}}\left|S\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon_{2}}\left|E^{(1)}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha_{1}}\left|E\right|,

Hence it suffices to prove (
1.25
), and we shall do this shortly.

We define a
(2\nu+1)
-adic flag rectangle in
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}
to be a set of the form
Q\times I
, where
Q
is a
(2\nu+1)
-adic cube
in
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
, which we identify with
\mathbb{R}^{2\nu}
; more precisely,

Q=\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\left[(m_{i}-\tfrac{1}{2})(2\nu+1)^{j},(m_{i}+\tfrac{1}{2})(2\nu+1)^{j}\right)

for some
m_{i}
and
j
in
\mathbb{Z}
, while
I
is a
(2\nu+1)^{2}
-adic interval
in
\mathbb{R}
, that is,

I=[(m-\tfrac{1}{2})(2\nu+1)^{2k},(m+\frac{1}{2})(2\nu+1)^{2k})

for some
k
and
n
in
\mathbb{Z}
, where
k\geq j
.
We write
\mathfrak{Q}
and
\mathfrak{I}
for the collections of all
(2\nu+1)
-adic cubes in
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and all
(2\nu+1)^{2}
-adic intervals in
\mathbb{R}
.

Recall the function
f:Q_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
of Theorem
1.2
, and let
F:P^{-1}(Q_{0})\to P^{-1}(Q_{0})
be the invertible Borel measurable function

F(z,t)=(z,2\nu(t-f(z)))\qquad\forall z\in Q_{0}\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

Then
R
is a shard if and only if
F(R)
is a
(2\nu+1)
-adic flag rectangle in
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}
.
The base of this rectangle in
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
is exactly the cube
P(R)
, while the projection of
F(R)
onto
\mathbb{R}
is a
(2\nu+1)^{2}
-adic interval, which we label
P_{\mathbb{R}}(R)
.

We first prove that

(1.26)

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(R)}{\operatorname{h}(R^{(h)})}\right)^{\varepsilon}|R|\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\left|E\right|.

If
R\in\mathfrak{R}
, then there exist a unique tile
T
and a unique cube
Q
such that
R=T\cap P^{-1}Q
.
We define the
j
th vertical ancestor
(R)_{j}
of
R
to be the shard
(T)_{j}\cap P^{-1}Q
, where
(T)_{j}
is the
j
th ancestor of
T
, and
\sigma:\mathfrak{M}(E)\to\mathbb{N}^{+}
as follows:

\sigma(R)=\max\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\left|(R)_{k}\cap E\right|>{\alpha}\left|(R)_{k}\right|\right\}+1.

Now we set, for each
I\in\mathfrak{I}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{I,k}=\Bigl\{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E):P_{\mathbb{R}}(R)=I,\ \sigma(R)=k\Bigr\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad A_{I,k}=\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{A}_{I,k}}R.

Note that
\mathfrak{A}_{I,k}
and hence also
A_{I,k}
may be empty.
Clearly, if
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{A}_{I,k}
, then
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
and
\operatorname{h}(R)=\operatorname{h}(R^{\prime})
, so
R
and
R^{\prime}
coincide or are disjoint, whence

(1.27)

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(R)}{\operatorname{h}(R^{(w)})}\right)^{\varepsilon}|R|

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathfrak{I}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{A}_{I,k}}(2\nu+1)^{-2k\varepsilon}\left|R\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathfrak{I}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(2\nu+1)^{-2k\varepsilon}\ |A_{I,k}|.

We now estimate
\left|A_{I,k}\right|
.
For all
R
in
\mathfrak{M}(E)
, all
I\in\mathfrak{I}
, and all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{R,j}=\{S\in\mathfrak{M}(E):P(S)\subset P(R),\ P_{\mathbb{R}}(S)\supseteq(P_{\mathbb{R}}(R))_{j}\},

\displaystyle B_{R,j}=\bigcup_{S\in\mathfrak{B}_{R,j}}S,\qquad\mathfrak{B}_{I,j}=\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{A}_{I,j}}\mathfrak{B}_{R,j}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{I,j}=\bigcup_{S\in\mathfrak{B}_{I,j}}S.

It is evident that
B_{R,j+1}\subseteq B_{R,j}
so
B_{I,j+1}\subseteq B_{I,j}
, and
B_{I,j}\subseteq E
for all
j
; furthermore,
\mathfrak{B}_{R,j+l}=\mathfrak{B}_{(R)_{j},l}
for all
j
and
l
in
\mathbb{N}
.

For all
I\in\mathfrak{I}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, every
R\in\mathfrak{A}_{I,k}
lies in
\mathfrak{M}(E)
, and
by definition,

\displaystyle|(R)_{k}\cap B_{R,k}|\leq|(R)_{k}\cap B_{I,k}|\leq|(R)_{k}\cap E|\leq{\alpha}\left|(R)_{k}\right|,

which implies that

\left|R\cap B_{I,k}\right|\leq\alpha\left|R\right|
,
and hence, since
R=(R\cap B_{I,0})
, that

\displaystyle\left|R\cap(B_{I,0}\setminus B_{I,k})\right|>(1-{\alpha})\left|R\right|,

that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}(\mathbbm{1}_{B_{I,0}\setminus B_{I,k}})(g)>1-{\alpha}\qquad\forall g\in R.

Since
A_{I,k}=\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{A}_{I,k}}R
, the inequality above holds for all
g\in A_{I,k}
, that is,

\displaystyle A_{I,k}\subseteq\Bigl\{g\in G:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}(\mathbbm{1}_{B_{I,0}\setminus B_{I,k}})(g)>1-{\alpha}\Bigr\}.

The
L^{2}(G)
-boundedness of the maximal function
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}
now implies that

\displaystyle|A_{I,k}|\leq\Big|\Big\{g\in G:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}(\mathbbm{1}_{B_{I,0}\setminus B_{I,k}})(g)>1-{\alpha}\Big\}\Big|\lesssim_{\alpha}|B_{I,0}\setminus B_{I,k}|,

and hence

(1.28)

\displaystyle|A_{I,k}|\lesssim_{\alpha}|B_{I,0}\setminus B_{I,1}|+\cdots+|B_{I,{k-1}}\setminus B_{I,k}|.

Finally, we substitute the estimate (
1.28
) into the right-hand side of (
1.27
), change the order of summation, and deduce that

(1.29)

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(R)}{\operatorname{h}(R^{(h)})}\right)^{\varepsilon}|R|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}2^{-2k\varepsilon}\sum_{I\in\mathfrak{I}}\left|B_{I,j}\setminus B_{I,j+1}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}2^{-2k\varepsilon}\sum_{I\in\mathfrak{I}}\left|B_{(I)_{j},0}\setminus B_{(I)_{j},1}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}2^{-2k\varepsilon}\sum_{I\in\mathfrak{I}}\left|B_{I,0}\setminus B_{I,1}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-2k\varepsilon}k\left|E\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}|E|,

and thus (
1.26
) holds.

To conclude, we prove that

(1.30)

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\left(\frac{\operatorname{w}(R)}{\operatorname{w}(R^{(w)})}\right)^{\varepsilon}|R|\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\left|E\right|.

We start by changing notation, and define the horizontal ancestors of a rectangle
R
.
If
R\in\mathfrak{R}
, then there exist a unique tile
T
and a unique cube
Q
such that
R=T\cap P^{-1}Q
.
If
R\subset T
, then the horizontal parent
(R)_{1}
of
R
is the rectangle
T\cap P^{-1}((Q)_{1})
, where
(Q)_{1}
is the parent cube of
Q
, while if
R=T
, then
(R)_{1}
is the tile
(T)_{1}
.
The
j
th ancestor
(R)_{j}
of
R
is then defined to be the horizontal parent of the
(j-1)
st ancestor
(R)_{j-1}
.
By definition, the width of
(R)_{j}
is
(2\nu+1)^{j}
times the width of
R
, while the height of
(R)_{j}
need not be the same as the height of
R
.

We now define, for each
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
,

\sigma(R)=\max\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\left|(R)_{k}\cap E\right|\geq{\alpha}\left|(R)_{k}\right|\right\}+1,

and for each
R\in\mathfrak{R}
,

\tau(R)=\tfrac{1}{2}\log_{2\nu+1}\operatorname{h}(R)-\log_{2\nu+1}\operatorname{w}(R).

Then
\tau((R)_{1})=\tau(R)-1
if
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
is not a tile.
The horizontal parent
(R)_{1}
is defined differently when
\tau(R)>0
and when
\tau(R)=0
, so we shall treat these cases separately.

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{<}(E):=\{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E):\tau(R)<\sigma(R)\}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{\geq}(E):=\{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E):\tau(R)\geq\sigma(R)\}
.
Let
\Sigma(E):=\{(R)_{\sigma(R)}:R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)\}
and
\mathrm{T}(E):=\{(R)_{\tau(R)}:R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)\}
.
If
R\in\Sigma(E)
and
g\in R
, then
\left|(R)_{\sigma(R)-1}\cap E\right|\geq\alpha\left|(R)_{\sigma(R)-1}\right|
, which implies that

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}(\mathbbm{1}_{E})(g)>\frac{\left|(R)_{\sigma(R)-1}\cap E\right|}{\left|(R)_{\sigma(R)-1}\right|}\geq\frac{\alpha}{(2\nu+1)^{\delta}}\,,

so that

\bigcup_{R\in\Sigma(E)}\subseteq E^{*}_{(2\nu+1)^{-\delta}\alpha}:=\{g\in G:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}(\mathbbm{1}_{E})(g)>(2\nu+1)^{-\nu}\alpha\},

and the measure of
\left|E^{*}_{(2\nu+1)^{-\delta}\alpha}\right|\lesssim_{\alpha}\left|E\right|
because
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}
is
\mathsf{L}^{2}
-bounded.
It is easy to see that the tiles in
\Sigma(E)
are pairwise disjoint, from the definition of
\sigma
, whence

(1.31)

\sum_{R\in\Sigma(E)}\left|R\right|\lesssim_{\alpha}\left|E\right|.

Thus, on the one hand,

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}_{<}(E)}\left(\frac{\operatorname{w}(R)}{\operatorname{w}(R^{(w)})}\right)^{\varepsilon}\left|R\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}_{<}(E)}(2\nu+1)^{-\varepsilon\sigma(R)}\left|R\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\Sigma(E)}\sum_{T\in\mathrm{T}(E)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathfrak{M}_{<}(E)\\
(R)_{\tau(R)}=T\\
(R)_{\sigma(R)}=S\end{subarray}}(2\nu+1)^{-\varepsilon(\sigma(R)-\tau(R))}(2\nu+1)^{-\varepsilon\tau(R)}\left|R\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(2\nu+1)^{-\varepsilon i}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}(2\nu+1))^{-\varepsilon j}\sum_{S\in\Sigma(E)}\sum_{T\in\mathrm{T}(E)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathfrak{M}_{<}(E)\\
(R)_{i+j}=S\\
(R)_{i}=T\end{subarray}}\left|R\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}(2\nu+1)^{-\varepsilon j}\sum_{S\in\Sigma(E)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in\mathrm{T}(E)\\
(T)_{j}=S\end{subarray}}\left|T\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\sum_{S\in\Sigma(E)}\left|S\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|E\right|.

The first inequality holds because when
i
is fixed, the distinct shards
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
such that
(R)_{i}=T
have the same width, and are therefore disjoint, while the second inequality holds because the distinct tiles
T
such that
(T)_{j}=S
have fixed width and are therefore disjoint.
The last inequality is the estimate (
1.31
).

On the other hand, the sum

(1.32)

\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}_{\geq}(E)}\left(\frac{\operatorname{w}(R)}{\operatorname{w}(R^{(w)})}\right)^{\varepsilon}\left|R\right|

may be treated by an argument like that used to prove (
1.26
).
The key definitions are that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{Q,k}=\Bigl\{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)_{\geq}:P(R)=Q,\ \sigma(R)=k\Bigr\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad A_{Q,k}=\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{A}_{Q,k}}R.

for each
Q\in\mathfrak{I}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, and,
for all
R
in
\mathfrak{M}(E)
, all
Q\in\mathfrak{Q}
, and all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{R,j}=\{S\in\mathfrak{M}(E):P(S)\supseteq(P(R))_{j},\ P_{\mathbb{R}}(S)\subset P_{\mathbb{R}}(R)\},

\displaystyle B_{R,j}=\bigcup_{S\in\mathfrak{B}_{R,j}}S,\qquad\mathfrak{B}_{Q,j}=\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{A}_{Q,j}}\mathfrak{B}_{R,j}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{Q,j}=\bigcup_{S\in\mathfrak{B}_{Q,j}}S,

and the key steps of the proof are estimating the sum (
1.32
) in terms of the measures of the
A_{Q,k}
, much as in (
1.27
), estimating the measures of the
A_{Q,k}
in terms of the measures of the
A_{Q,k}
, much as in (
1.28
), and arguing with sums as in (
1.29
).
∎

We say that a constant is
geometric
if it depends on inherent properties of the Heisenberg group and its geometry (including its decompositions into tiles and shards), the apertures of the cones that appear in the various definitions, and the enlargement factors that connect shards and supports of particles; a
geometric multiple
is defined similarly.
The constants mentioned above are geometric, except that some depend on
p
, others on
M
and
N
, and others on positive parameters
\alpha
and
\varepsilon
.
We use the notation
A\lesssim B
to mean that there is a constant
C
such that
A\leq CB
, and
A\eqsim B
to mean that
A\lesssim B
and
A\lesssim B
.
If the constant is geometric, we do not necessarily point this out explicitly.
However, when the implicit constant in one of these inequalities depends on a nongeometric constant, we often indicate this explicitly, to make the proof more transparent, for example, we might write
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-\varepsilon k}\eqsim_{\varepsilon}1
.
Many of the constants that we use in our proofs are geometric.

1.4.
Flag Sobolev inequalities

Because we are dealing with two Laplacians, there are various Sobolev-type inequalities possible.
A comprehensive account of many of these is due to Folland
[
23
]
, to which the reader should refer for unexplained estimates below.
We present several that will be useful for us.

Lemma 1.19
.

Suppose that
Q
is a cube in
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
of side-length
q
, with sides parallel to the axes, and that
f:Q\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable.
Suppose also that
b\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\operatorname{supp}(b)\subseteq\{(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:z\in Q,f(z)\leq t\leq f(z)+h\}
.

(a)

If also
\mathcal{T}b\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, then

\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|b(g)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g\right)^{1/2}\lesssim h\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|\mathcal{T}b(g)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g\right)^{1/2}.

(1)

If also
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}b\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, then

\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|b(g)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g\right)^{1/2}\lesssim q^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}b(g)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g\right)^{1/2}.

Proof.

By calculus in
\mathbb{R}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|c(t)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim h^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Bigl|\frac{d}{dt}c(t)\Bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

for a function
c
on
\mathbb{R}
supported in an interval of length
h
.
The first estimate is proved by integrating the inequality above in
z
.

To prove the second estimate, by translating and dilating, it suffices to suppose that
Q
is the cube with centre
0
in
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and side-length
1
.
Again we denote by
P
the canonical projection from
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
to
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
.
The fundamental solution
k
of the sub-Laplacian
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is well-known to be a power of the Korányi norm
[
22
]
, and

\displaystyle b(g^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}b(g)\,k(g^{-1}g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}b(g)\,\mathbbm{1}_{Q-Q}(P(g^{-1}g^{\prime}))\,k(g^{-1}g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g\qquad\forall g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu},

because of the support restrictions on
b
and hence on
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}b
.
Hence

\left\|b\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}b\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\left\|(\mathbbm{1}_{Q-Q}\circ P)k\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq C_{\nu}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}b\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})},

by an easy calculation.
∎

Lemma 1.20
.

Suppose that
b\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is supported in
T(g,r,s)
.
If
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}b
,
\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b
,
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b
are also in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, where
M>\delta/4
, then
b\in\mathsf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and
\left\|b\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{\infty}}
is controlled by

\frac{1}{|T(g,r,s)|^{1/2}}\left(\left\|b\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}b\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\right).

Proof.

By translation and dilation, it suffices to suppose that
g=o
and
r=1/2
.
Let
h
be the height of
T(o,r,s)
.

There are smooth nonnegative-valued functions
\eta_{j}
and
\tilde{\eta}_{j}
on
\mathbb{R}
such that
\operatorname{supp}\eta_{j}\subseteq[j-1,j+1]
,
\eta_{j}=\eta_{0}(\cdot-j)
, and
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\eta_{j}=1
while
\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{\eta}_{j})\subseteq[j-2,j+2]
,
\tilde{\eta}_{j}=\tilde{\eta}_{0}(\cdot-j)
, and
\tilde{\eta}_{j}=1
on
\operatorname{supp}(\eta_{j})
.
Abusing notation, we consider these as functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
that depend on
t
but not
z
.

Given
b
as in the enunciation, we write
b_{j}
for
\eta_{j}b
.
Then

\displaystyle\left\|b\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\|b_{j}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigl\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}b_{j}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\bigl\|\tilde{\eta}_{j}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}b\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\bigl\|\tilde{\eta}_{j}b\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\right),

by an argument similar to that used to prove the second part of the previous lemma.

From calculus in
\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\tilde{\eta}_{j}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}b(z,t)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim h^{-1/2}\left(\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathcal{L}_{(2)}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}b(z,t)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}+\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}b(z,t)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\right)

for
m
equal to
0
or
M
.
We integrate in
z
to deduce that

\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigl\|\tilde{\eta}_{j}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}b\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim h^{-1/2}\left(\bigl\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\bigl\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}b\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\right).

The desired result follows easily.
∎

1.5.
Vanishing moments on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}

We say that a function
f
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is homogeneous of degree
d
if
f\circ D_{r}=r^{d}f
and that a differential operator
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is homogeneous of degree
e
if
\mathcal{D}(f\circ D_{r})=r^{e}\mathcal{D}(f)\circ D_{r}
.
If
f
is homogeneous of degree
d
and
\mathcal{D}
is homogeneous of degree
e
, then the function
\mathcal{D}f
is homogeneous of degree
d-e
.
The coordinate functions
z_{j}
(and their real and imaginary parts
x_{j}
and
y_{j}
) are homogeneous of degree one, while the coordinate
t
is homogeneous of degree
2
, and each homogeneous polynomial
p
has a positive degree, which is the product of the degrees of the coordinate functions involved in each of the monomial terms of
p
.
A monomial is an expression
p(x,t)=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots x_{2n}^{\alpha_{2n}}t^{k}
, where each
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Our next results are the outcome of reflections on a remark of Fulvio Ricci, who pointed out to us that if all moments of homogeneous order up to
k
of a radial Schwartz function
f
vanish, then there are radial Schwartz functions
g_{a,b}
, where
2a+b=k+1
, such that
f=\sum_{a,b}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{a}\mathcal{T}^{b}g_{a,b}
.
See
[
21
]
(especially Lemma 5.2) for more on this type of result.
These authors use a higher order extension of Hadamard’s lemma to prove this.
We are interested in functions with compact support, and for us a different version of these ideas, based on a lemma of de Rham
[
51
, Lemme II]
, is useful.

Lemma 1.21
.

Suppose that
\varphi\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, that
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)\subseteq[-1,1]^{n}
and that
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
If

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi(x)\,p(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0

for all homogeneous polynomials
p
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of homogeneous degree at most
m
, then there are
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
functions
f_{\alpha}
supported in
[-1,1]^{n}
, for
\alpha\in I^{n}_{m+1}
, such that

(1.33)

\varphi=\sum_{\alpha\in I^{n}_{m+1}}\partial^{\alpha}f_{\alpha},

where
I^{n}_{m+1}
is the set of multi-indices
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
such that
|\alpha|=m+1
.
The
f_{\alpha}
in (
1.33
) may be chosen to depend smoothly and linearly on
\varphi
.

Proof.

In this proof,
J^{n}_{m+1}
denotes the set of multi-indices
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
such that
|\alpha|\leq m+1
.

We apply induction on the dimension
n
of the ambient space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
If
n=1
, we take

f_{m+1}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\frac{(x-t)^{m}}{m!}\,\varphi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,

and the result is a routine verification.

Suppose that the result holds in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, and take
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that satisfies the hypotheses of the lemma.
We write
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
(\hat{x},x_{n})
, where
\hat{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.

Take smooth functions
\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, supported in
[-1,1]^{n-1}
, such that, for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}
, of order at most
m
,

(1.34)

\int_{[-1,1]^{n-1}}\hat{x}^{\alpha}\,\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}(\hat{x})\,\mathrm{d}\hat{x}=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}

(this is the Kronecker delta).
For such
\beta
, define

g_{\beta}(x_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}x^{\beta}\,\varphi(\hat{x},x_{n})\,\mathrm{d}\hat{x}\qquad\forall x_{n}\in\mathbb{R},

so that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}x^{\alpha}\Bigl[\varphi(\hat{x},x_{n})-\sum_{\beta\in J^{n-1}_{m}}\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}(\hat{x})\,g_{\beta}(x_{n})\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\hat{x}=0

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and all
\alpha\in J^{n-1}_{m}
.
By the inductive hypothesis, we may write

\varphi(\hat{x},x)-\sum_{\beta\in J^{n-1}_{m}}\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}(\hat{x})\,g_{\beta}(x_{n})=\sum_{\alpha\in I^{n-1}_{m+1}}\partial^{\alpha}\psi_{\alpha}(\hat{x},x_{n}),

where each
\psi_{\alpha}
is smooth and supported in
[-1,1]^{n}
.
So it suffices to consider the terms
\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}(\hat{x})\,g_{\beta}(x_{n})
.

By definition,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}x_{n}^{j}\,g_{\beta}(x_{n})\,\mathrm{d}x_{n}=0

when
0\leq j\leq m-|\beta|
, so that

g_{\beta}(x_{n})=\frac{d^{m+1-|\beta|}}{dx_{n}^{m+1-|\beta|}}G_{\beta}(x_{n}),

where

G_{\beta}(x_{n})=\int_{-\infty}^{x_{n}}\frac{(x_{n}-t)^{m-|\beta|}}{(m-|\beta|)!}g_{\beta}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Moreover, from (
1.34
) and the inductive hypothesis, there are functions
\psi_{\beta,\alpha}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
such that

\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}=\sum_{\alpha\in I^{n-1}_{m-|\beta|+1}}\partial^{\alpha}\psi_{\beta,\alpha}.

The lemma follows.
∎

Definition 1.22
.

Fix
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\bar{T}
be a closed tube.
We write
\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
for the space of all linear combinations of expressions
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{T}^{n}f
, where
\mathcal{D}
is a product of
m
vector fields, each chosen from
\{\mathcal{X}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{X}_{2\nu}\}
, while
f\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\operatorname{supp}(f)\subseteq\bar{T}
.
We define
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{m}\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
analogously, with right invariant vector fields.

In the next proposition, we identify
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2\nu}\times\mathbb{R}
, and write elements of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
as
(x,t)
, where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2\nu}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
We write
p_{\alpha,k}
for the monomial
x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots x_{2n}^{\alpha_{2n}}t^{k}
.

Proposition 1.23
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\varphi\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
.
Then

(1.35)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\alpha,k}(x,t)\,\varphi(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t=0\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2\nu}

for all monomials
p_{\alpha,k}
when
k<n
if and only if

\varphi\in\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
.
Further,

(1.36)

\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}p_{\alpha,k}(g)\,\varphi(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=0

for all monomials
p_{\alpha,k}
when
|\alpha|+2k<m

if and only if
\varphi\in\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
.

Consequently,
\varphi\in\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
if and only if (
1.35
) holds for all monomials
p_{\alpha,k}
when
k<n
, and

\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}p_{\alpha,k}(g)\,\psi(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=0

for all monomials
p_{\alpha,k}
when
k\geq n
and
|\alpha|+2(k-n)<m
,
where
\psi\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})
is such that
\varphi=\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\psi
.

Proof.

The proposition is invariant under translations and dilations, so without loss of generality we take
\bar{T}
to be
\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,1)\bar{B}^{(2)}(0,h)
.
It is evident that (
1.35
) implies (
1.36
) (for the same
p_{\alpha,k}
).

Suppose that
\varphi=\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\psi
.
Integration by parts shows that, if
n>k
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}}p(x,t)\,\varphi(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t=(-1)^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\nabla_{(2)}^{n}p(x,t)\,\psi(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t=0\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2\nu}.

Conversely, if (
1.35
) holds when
n<k
, then we may define

\psi(x,t)=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\frac{(t-s)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\,\varphi(x,s)\,\mathrm{d}s\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2\nu},

and it is clear that
\psi\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})
and
\varphi=\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\psi
.

Similarly, if
\varphi=\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\cdot\psi
(here
\psi
is tensor valued and we apply the summation convention), then

\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}p(g)\,(\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\cdot\psi)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=(-1)^{m}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}(\nabla_{(1)}^{m}p)(g)\cdot\psi(g)\,\mathrm{d}g.

When
|\alpha|+2k<m
, the homogeneous degree of the polynomial
\nabla_{(1)}^{m}p
is

|\alpha|+2k-m
, which is negative, so
\nabla_{(1)}^{m}p=0
and (
1.36
) holds.

Conversely, suppose that (
1.36
) holds.
Much as in the proof of the previous lemma, we take smooth functions
\hat{\varphi}_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2\nu}
, supported in
[-1,1]^{2\nu}
, such that, for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{2\nu}
, of order at most
m-1
,

\int_{[-1,1]^{2\nu}}x^{\alpha}\,\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}

(this is the Kronecker delta).
For such
\beta
, define

g_{\beta}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2\nu}}x^{\beta}\,\varphi(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R},

so that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2\nu}}x^{\alpha}\Bigl[\varphi(x,t)-\sum_{\beta\in J^{2\nu}_{m-1}}\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}(x)\,g_{\beta}(t)\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d}x=0

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and all
\alpha\in J^{2\nu}_{m-1}
.
By the previous lemma, we may write

\varphi(x,t)-\sum_{\beta\in J^{2\nu}_{m-1}}\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}(x)\,g_{\beta}(t)=\sum_{\alpha\in I^{2\nu}_{m}}\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{\alpha}(x,t),

where each
\varphi_{\alpha}
is smooth and supported in
\bar{T}
.
Now we consider the
\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}(x)\,g_{\beta}(t)
.

By definition,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}t^{j}\,g_{\beta}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=0

when
0\leq 2j\leq m-|\beta|
, so that

g_{\beta}(t)=\frac{d^{\bar{m}+1}}{dt^{\bar{m}+1}}G_{\beta}(t),

where
\bar{m}=\lfloor(m-|\beta|)/2\rfloor
and

G_{\beta}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\frac{(t-s)^{\bar{m}}}{\bar{m}!}g_{\beta}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Moreover, from (
1.34
) and the previous lemma, there are functions
\varphi_{\beta,\alpha}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2\nu}
such that

\hat{\varphi}_{\beta}=\sum_{\alpha\in I^{2\nu}_{|\beta|+1}}\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{\beta,\alpha}.

Hence we may write

\varphi=\sum_{(\alpha,k)\in I}\partial^{\alpha,k}\varphi_{\alpha,k},

where
I
is the collection of multi-indices
(\alpha,k)
, where
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2\nu}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
k=\lfloor(m+2-|\alpha|)/2\rfloor
.

To conclude, we show that each summand belongs to
\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
.
We write the partial derivatives on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
in terms of the left invariant vector fields; then

\partial^{\alpha,k}=(\mathcal{X}_{\alpha_{1}}+cy_{\alpha_{1}}\mathcal{T})\dots(\mathcal{X}_{\alpha_{j}}+cy_{\alpha_{j}}\mathcal{T})\mathcal{T}^{k},

where
j=|\alpha|
and
k=\lfloor(m+2-j)/2\rfloor
.
We write
\mathcal{T}
as the commutator
[\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{\nu+1}]
, and expand the expression, obtaining a weighted sum of (not necessarily commuting) products of vector fields
\mathcal{X}_{i}
and monomials
y_{j}
.
Each summand is homogeneous, of degree
j+2k
, and so the number of vector fields that occur, minus the number of monomials, is equal to
j+2k
.

Observe that
y_{i}\mathcal{X}_{j}=\mathcal{X}_{j}y_{i}-\delta_{i,j}
.
By iterating this, we may move all the monomials to the right, and end with a weighted sum of terms of the form
\mathcal{X}_{i_{1}}\dots\mathcal{X}_{i_{a}}p_{\mathbf{i}}
, where the number of vector fields that occur, minus the homogeneous degree of the polynomial, is equal to
j+2k
.
Hence we realise
\partial^{\alpha,k}\varphi_{\alpha,k}
as a sum of terms of the required form

\mathcal{X}_{j_{1}}\dots\mathcal{X}_{j_{b}}q_{\mathbf{j}}\varphi_{\alpha,k},

where
b\geq j+2k\geq m+1
.
We group together all the terms that begin with the same invariant differential operator
\mathcal{X}_{j_{1}}\dots\mathcal{X}_{j_{m+1}}
, which shows that
\varphi\in\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
, as claimed.

The last part of the lemma is proved by combining the first two results.
∎

Corollary 1.24
.

Suppose that
\varphi\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})
and
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
\varphi\in\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
if and only if
\varphi\in\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{m}\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(2)}^{n}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
.

Proof.

The following are equivalent: first,
\varphi\in\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{q}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
; second,
\mathcal{R}\varphi\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})
(as
\mathcal{R}
exchanges left invariance with right invariance); third, certain moments of
\mathcal{R}\varphi
vanish as in Proposition
1.23
; fourth, certain moments of
\varphi
vanish as in Proposition
1.23
(because
\mathcal{R}
preserves homogeneity); and finally,
\varphi\in\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{q}\cdot\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})^{\otimes}
by the proposition.
∎

Remark 1.25
.

It is not clear whether
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{n}\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})=\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{n}\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\bar{T})
.

1.6.
Estimates for the heat and Poisson kernels

Let
h^{(1)}_{r}
(where
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
) be the heat kernel for
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
, that is, the convolution kernel of
\mathrm{e}^{-r\mathcal{L}_{(1)}}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
By homogeneity,

(1.37)

\displaystyle h^{(1)}_{r}(z,t)=r^{-\delta/2}h^{(1)}_{1}\circ D_{1/{\sqrt{r}}}\qquad\forall r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

The following Gaussian upper bound for the heat kernel in terms of the control norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{c}
holds:

(1.38)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}h^{(1)}_{r}(z,t)|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{q,\varepsilon}r^{-q/2-\delta/2}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\left\|(z,t)\right\|_{c}^{2}}{4(1+\varepsilon)r}\Bigr)

for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
This is proved in
[
60
, p. 48]
.
There is a similar lower bound (see
[
60
, p. 61]
) for the heat kernel (but not its derivatives), namely,

h^{(1)}_{r}(z,t)\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}r^{-\delta/2}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\left\|(z,t)\right\|_{c}^{2}}{4(1-\varepsilon)r}\Bigr).

The subordination formula

\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-v}}{\sqrt{v}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{2}/4v}\,\mathrm{d}v\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

leads us to corresponding estimates for the Poisson kernel
p^{(1)}
.
By functional calculus, for all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-r\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-v}}{\sqrt{v}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-r^{2}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/4v}\,\mathrm{d}v

and

\displaystyle\nabla_{(1)}^{q}\mathrm{e}^{-r\sqrt{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-v}}{\sqrt{v}}\nabla_{(1)}^{q}\mathrm{e}^{-r^{2}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/4v}\,\mathrm{d}v

so from (
1.38
),

\displaystyle\left|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}p^{(1)}_{r}(g)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-v}}{\sqrt{v}}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{4v}\right)^{-(q+\delta)/2}\mathrm{e}^{-v\left\|g\right\|_{c}^{2}/(1+\varepsilon)r^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=2^{q+\delta}r^{-q-\delta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}v^{(q+\delta-1)/2}\mathrm{e}^{-v-v\left\|g\right\|_{c}^{2}/(1+\varepsilon)r^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=2^{q+\delta}r^{-q-\delta}\frac{\Gamma((q+\delta+1)/2)}{(1+\left\|g\right\|_{c}^{2}/(1+\varepsilon)r^{2})^{(q+\delta+1)/2}}

\displaystyle\eqsim\frac{r}{(r^{2}+\left\|g\right\|_{c}^{2})^{(q+\delta+1)/2}}

for all
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
; the implicit constants depend on
q
,
\nu
and
\varepsilon
.
Analogously,

p^{(1)}_{r}(g)\gtrsim\frac{r}{(r^{2}+\left\|g\right\|_{c}^{2})^{\delta/2+1/2}}\qquad\forall g\in G.

For computation, we may and shall replace the control norm by the gauge norm, and we shall use the estimates

(1.39)

\left|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}p^{(1)}_{r}(g)\right|\lesssim_{m,n}{\frac{r}{\max\{r,\left\|g\right\|\}^{q+\delta+1}}}\qquad\forall g\in G.

Similar estimates hold for the usual Poisson kernel
p^{(2)}_{s}
on
\mathbb{R}
, namely,

(1.40)

\left|\nabla_{(2)}^{q}p^{(2)}_{s}(t)\right|\lesssim_{q}\frac{s}{\max\{s,|t|\}^{q+2}}

for all
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence

\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}p^{(1)}_{r}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g\lesssim_{q}r^{-q}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\nabla_{(2)}^{q}p^{(2)}_{s}(t)\right|\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim_{q}s^{-q}.

1.7.
Poisson boundedness

We are going to be interested in smooth functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
that are supported in
B^{(1)}(o,1)
, and in smooth functions that behave much like the Poisson kernel or its derivatives.
The following family of norms works naturally in both situations.

Definition 1.26
.

Fix
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
The
Poisson norms
on the space of smooth (possibly vector-valued) functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
are given by

(1.41)

\bigl\|\varphi^{(1)}\bigr\|_{(Q)}=\sup\left\{\max\{1,\left\|g\right\|\}^{q+\delta+\theta}\bigl|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}\varphi^{(1)}(g)\bigr|:g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu},0\leq q\leq Q\right\}.

We write
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
for the Fréchet space of functions
\varphi^{(1)}
such that
\|\varphi^{(1)}\|_{(Q)}
is finite for all
Q\in\mathbb{N}
.
We say that a family
\mathsf{F}^{(1)}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
of functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is
\theta
-Poisson-bounded
if
\|\varphi^{(1)}\|_{(Q)}
is bounded for all
\varphi^{(1)}\in\mathsf{F}^{(1)}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, for sufficiently many
Q\in\mathbb{N}
.

Similarly, the
Poisson norms
on the space of smooth (possibly vector-valued) functions on
\mathbb{R}
are given by

(1.42)

\bigl\|\varphi^{(2)}\bigr\|_{(Q)}=\sup\left\{\max\{1,|t|\}^{q+1+\theta}\bigl|\nabla_{(2)}^{q}\varphi^{(2)}(t)\bigr|:t\in\mathbb{R},0\leq q\leq Q\right\};

we write
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{R})
for the Fréchet space of functions
\varphi^{(2)}
on
\mathbb{R}
such that
\|\varphi^{(2)}\|_{(Q)}
is finite for all
Q\in\mathbb{N}
.
We say that a family
\mathsf{F}^{(2)}(\mathbb{R})
of functions on
\mathbb{R}
is
\theta
-Poisson-bounded
if
\|\varphi^{(2)}\|_{(Q)}
is bounded for all
\varphi^{(2)}\in\mathsf{F}^{(2)}(\mathbb{R})
, for sufficiently many
Q\in\mathbb{N}
.

We often write
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
for a pair
(\varphi^{(1)},\varphi^{(2)})
of functions, where
\varphi^{(1)}
is defined on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
is defined on
\mathbb{R}
.
A family
\mathsf{F}
of such pairs is said to be
\theta
-Poisson bounded if the families of first and second components are
\theta
-Poisson-bounded.

Finally,
\theta
-Poisson bounded functions
\varphi^{(1)}:\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\to\mathbb{C}
or
\varphi^{(2)}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
are said to be
normalised
if their total integral is
1
.

Mostly we deal with the case where
\theta=1
, and omit
\theta
from the notation.

In this definition, “sufficiently many” is left unspecified; in any particular calculation involving Poisson bounded families of functions, it is only necessary to control finitely many derivatives, but the number of derivatives varies from one calculation to another, and depends on
\nu
; we do not bother keeping tabs on these dependencies.

By (
1.39
) and (
1.40
), the Poisson kernels
p^{(1)}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
p^{(2)}
on
\mathbb{R}
lie in
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{R})
, and evidently
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
while
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{R})\subseteq\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

The closed subspaces of
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and of
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{R})
of functions supported in the closed balls
\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,1)
and
\bar{B}^{(2)}(0,1)
coincide with the closed subspaces of the Fréchet spaces
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
supported in
\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,1)
and
\bar{B}^{(2)}(0,1)
.

Remark 1.27
.

There is another family of Poisson norms on the space of smooth functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, namely,

(1.43)

\bigl\|\varphi^{(1)}\bigr\|_{(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{Q})}=\sup\left\{\max\{1,\left\|g\right\|\}^{q+\delta+\theta}\bigl|\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{q}\varphi^{(1)}(g)\bigr|:g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu},0\leq q\leq Q\right\},

where we consider a right translation invariant gradient rather than a left invariant gradient.
These norms give rise to the same Fréchet space as the norms used in the definition of
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Indeed,
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}=\nabla_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}+8\nu y\mathcal{T}
(here
y
is a vector), so that

\displaystyle\max\{1,\left\|g\right\|\}^{2+\delta}\bigl|\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}(g)\bigr|

\displaystyle\lesssim\max\{1,\left\|g\right\|\}^{2+\delta}\left(\bigl|\nabla_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}(g)\bigr|+\left\|g\right\|\bigl|\mathcal{T}\varphi^{(1)}(g)\bigr|\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\varphi^{(1)}\right\|_{(1)}+\left\|\varphi^{(1)}\right\|_{(2)},

so that
\|\varphi^{(1)}\|_{(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{1})}\lesssim\|\varphi^{(1)}\|_{(2)}
.
Similarly,
\|\varphi^{(1)}\|_{(1)}\lesssim\|\varphi^{(1)}\|_{(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{2})}
.
Inductively we may estimate all the norms from one family by sums of norms from the other family.

Definition 1.28
.

Given a Poisson bounded family
\mathsf{F}
of pairs of functions, the associated
grand maximal function

\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)
of
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g):=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathsf{F}}\sup_{r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g)\right|\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu},

where
\varphi_{r,s}
denotes the convolution product
\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}
of normalised dilates.

In the next lemma, we estimate the size of
\varphi_{r,s}
, where
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is Poisson-bounded, and hence compare two maximal functions.
We recall that
\chi_{r,s}=\chi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\chi^{(2)}_{s}
, where
\chi^{(1)}_{r}
is the normalised characteristic function of the ball
B^{(1)}(o,r)
and
\chi^{(2)}_{s}
is the normalised characteristic function of the ball
B^{(2)}(0,s)
.

Lemma 1.29
.

Fix
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and a Poisson bounded family
\mathsf{F}
of pairs of functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and on
\mathbb{R}
.
Then

(1)

p_{r,s}\simeq p_{\beta r,\gamma s}
for all
r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
;

(2)

\chi_{r,s}\lesssim p_{r,s}\lesssim\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-i-j}\chi_{2^{i}r,2^{j}s}
for all
r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
;

(3)

\left|\varphi_{r,s}\right|\lesssim p_{r,s}
for all
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathsf{F}
;

(4)

\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(\left|f\right|)\eqsim\mathcal{M}_{F}(\left|f\right|)
for all measurable functions
f
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.

Proof.

The first and second parts follow immediately from (
1.39
) and (
1.40
), and the third from (
1.39
), (
1.40
) and Definition
1.26
.
The final part follows from the definitions.
∎

In particular, the grand maximal operator is bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p\leq\infty
.
A similar result is shown in
[
16
]
.
The following lemma extends the above computation, and enables us to reduce many calculations involving Poisson bounded functions to calculations involving smooth functions with compact support.

Lemma 1.30
.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\theta\in(0,1)
.
There are continuous linear maps
L^{1,i}
on
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that
\operatorname{supp}L^{1,0}\varphi\subset B^{(1)}(o,1)
and
\operatorname{supp}L^{1,i}\varphi\subset B^{(1)}(o,2^{j})\setminus B^{(1)}(o,2^{j-2})
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, and

\varphi=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}L^{1,i}\varphi\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}).

Moreover, we may choose the
L^{1,i}
such that
\left\|L^{1,i}\varphi\right\|_{(Q)}\lesssim_{N,Q}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{(Q)}
and the sum converges in
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Further, if
\varphi
has mean
0
, then we may choose the
L^{1,i}
such that all moments of
L^{1,i}\varphi
of homogeneous order
m
vanish if
m\leq N
.

Proof.

By composing a suitable smooth partition of unity on
[0,\infty)
with a smooth norm, such as the Kóranyi norm, we may easily find smooth functions
\eta^{i}
such that
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\eta^{i}=1
,
\operatorname{supp}\eta^{0}\subseteq B^{(1)}(o,1)
,
\operatorname{supp}\eta^{1}\subseteq B^{(1)}(o,2)\setminus B^{(1)}(o,1/2)
, and
\eta^{i}=\eta^{1}(2^{-i}\cdot)
when
i\geq 2
.
Clearly
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\eta^{i}\varphi=\varphi
when
\varphi\in\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\left\|\eta^{i}\varphi\right\|_{(Q)}\lesssim\left\|\varphi\right\|_{(Q)}
for all
Q\in\mathbb{N}
.

For the rest of the proof, we suppose that
\varphi
has mean
0
.
In this case, there is no reason for the
\eta^{i}\varphi
to have the required vanishing moments, so we apply a correction.

Take a basis
\{b_{1},\dots,b_{J}\}
for the vector space of polynomials on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of homogeneous degree at most
N
such that
b_{j}
is homogeneous of degree
d_{j}
, and a dual set of
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
functions
\{f_{1},\dots,f_{J}\}
, supported in
B^{(1)}(o,1)\setminus B^{(1)}(o,1/2)
, such that

\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}f_{j}(g)\,b_{k}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=\delta_{j,k}

(
\delta_{j,k}
is the Kronecker delta) when
j,k\in\{1,\dots,J\}
.
Adding a nonzero multiple of a dilate of
f_{j}
to
\eta^{i}\varphi
changes its integral against
b_{j}
but does not change other moments in the range of interest.

We define the coefficient
c_{k}^{I}(\varphi)
, where
K\in\mathbb{N}
, by

c_{k}^{I}(\varphi)=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\sum_{i=0}^{I}(\eta^{i}\varphi)(g)\,b_{k}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g.

Then for
I\in\mathbb{N}
, the correction term
C_{k}^{I}(\varphi)
is defined by

C_{k}^{I}(\varphi)=c_{k}^{I}(\varphi)2^{-Id_{k}}[f_{k}]_{2^{I}}-c_{k}^{I-1}(\varphi)2^{(1-I)d_{k}}[f_{k}]_{2^{I-1}}.

If
d_{k}>0
, then

\left|c_{k}^{I}(\varphi)\right|\lesssim\int_{B^{(1)}(0,2^{I+1})}\left|\varphi(g)\,b_{k}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g\lesssim\int_{0}^{2^{I+1}}\frac{r^{d_{k}}}{1+r^{\theta+\delta}}r^{\delta-1}\,\mathrm{d}r\lesssim 2^{I(d_{k}-\theta)}.

And if
d_{k}=0
, then
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\varphi(g)\,b_{k}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\varphi(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=0
, whence similarly

\displaystyle\left|c_{k}^{I}(\varphi)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|-\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\sum_{i=I+1}^{\infty}(\eta^{i}\varphi)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\right|\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\setminus B^{(1)}(0,2^{I-1})}\left|\varphi(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g\lesssim 2^{I(d_{k}-\theta)}.

Hence

\displaystyle\left|c_{k}^{I}(\varphi)2^{-Id_{k}}[f_{k}]_{2^{I}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{c_{k}^{I}(\varphi)}{2^{I(\delta+d_{k})}}f_{k}\circ D_{2^{-I}}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-I(\theta+\delta)}\,\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(1)}(o,2^{I})\setminus B^{(1)}(o,2^{I-1})},

and so the correction terms all lie in
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
with controlled Poisson norms.
Further, the correction terms form a telescopic series with sum
0
.
Finally,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(\eta^{k}\varphi(g)-C_{i}^{K}(\varphi)(g)\right)b_{i}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(\eta^{j}\varphi(g)-c_{i}^{k}(\varphi)2^{-kd_{i}}[f_{i}]_{2^{k}}+c_{i}^{m-1}(\varphi)2^{(1-m)d_{i}}[f_{i}]_{2^{k-1}}\right)b_{i}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\eta^{j}\varphi(g)b_{i}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g-\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}c_{i}^{j}(\varphi)2^{-jd_{i}}[f_{i}]_{2^{j}}b_{i}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}c_{i}^{j-1}(\varphi)2^{(1-j)d_{i}}[f_{i}]_{2^{j-1}}b_{i}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\eta^{j}\varphi(g)b_{i}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g-c_{i}^{k}(\varphi)+c_{i}^{k-1}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=0.

Clearly, subtracting off all the correction terms
C^{k}_{i}(\varphi)
from
L^{1,i}\varphi
produces the desired effect.
∎

If we had taken
\theta
to be
1
, we would have had an extra logarithmic term.

If
\theta<1
, then
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
may be injected continuously in
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Further, analogous results hold for Poisson bounded functions on
\mathbb{R}
.
This has the following consequence.

Corollary 1.31
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{F}
is a family of pairs of functions
\varphi^{(1)}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
on
\mathbb{R}
, and that
M,N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta\in(0,1)
.

If
\mathsf{F}
is Poisson bounded, then for all
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathsf{F}
, we may write
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
as sums of normalised dilates:

(1.44)

\varphi^{(1)}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}[\varphi^{(1,i)}]_{2^{i}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varphi^{(2)}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}[\varphi^{(2,j)}]_{2^{j}},

where
\varphi^{(1,i)}\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{(1,i)}\subseteq\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,1)
and moreover
\varphi^{(2,j)}\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\operatorname{supp}\varphi^{(2,j)}\subseteq\bar{B}^{(2)}(0,1)
; further,

(1.45)

\bigl\|\varphi^{(1,i)}\bigr\|_{(Q)}\lesssim_{\mathsf{F},Q}2^{-\theta i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigl\|\varphi^{(2,j)}\bigr\|_{(Q)}\lesssim_{\mathsf{F},Q}2^{-\theta j}

for all
i,j,Q\in\mathbb{N}
.
Further, if
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
have mean
0
, then
\varphi^{(1,i)}
and
\varphi^{(2,j)}
have all moments of homogeneous order at most
M
and
N
equal to
0
.

Conversely, if we may write every
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
as sums of normalised dilates as in (
1.44
), where estimates of the form (
1.45
) hold, then
\mathsf{F}
is
\theta
-Poisson bounded.

Proof.

Let
L^{(1,i)}
be the maps constructed in the previous lemma, and
L^{(2,j)}
be the analogous maps on functions on
\mathbb{R}
.
We define
\varphi^{(1,i)}
to be the normalised dilate
[L^{(1,i)}\varphi^{(1)}]_{2^{-i}}
, which is supported in
B^{(1)}(o,1)
,
and
\varphi^{(2,j)}
to be the normalised dilate
[L^{(2,j)}\varphi^{(2)}]_{2^{-j}}
, which is supported in
B^{(2)}(0,1)
.

Conversely, if
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
decompose as in (
1.44
), where estimates of the form (
1.45
) hold, then the
\theta
-Poisson boundedness of
\mathsf{F}
follows by summation.
∎

Many of the results that we are going to prove involve operators that are sublinear in more than one sense.
For example, we consider the maximal function
\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)
, given by

\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g):=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathsf{F}}\sup_{r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(g)\right|\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

The expression
\sup_{r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\bigl|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(g)\bigr|
is evidently sublinear in
f
,
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
.
The point of the next proposition is that is suffices to handle functions
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
with compact support.

Proposition 1.32
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{F}
is a Poisson bounded family of pairs of functions.
There there is a Poisson bounded family
\mathsf{F}_{0}
of pairs of functions, supported in
B^{(1)}(o,1)
and
B^{(2)}(0,1)
, such that

\left\|\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq 4\left\|\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}_{0}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\qquad\forall f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}).

Proof.

By Corollary
1.31
, given
(\varphi^{(1)},\varphi^{(2)})\in\mathsf{F}
, we may decompose
\varphi^{(1)}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-i}[\varphi^{(1,i)}]_{2^{i}}
and
\varphi^{(2)}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-j}[\varphi^{(2,j)}]_{2^{j}}
, where the functions
\varphi^{(1,i)}
and
\varphi^{(2,j)}
are compactly supported and have uniformly bounded Poisson norms.
We take
\mathsf{F}_{0}
to be the set of all such
\varphi^{(1,i)}
and
\varphi^{(2,j)}
.
Then

\displaystyle\sup_{r,s>0}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r}^{(1)}\ast_{(2)}\varphi_{s}^{(2)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{r,s>0}\left|f\ast_{(1)}[\varphi^{(1,j)}]_{2^{j}r}\ast_{(2)}[\varphi^{(2,k)}]_{2^{k}s}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{r,s>0}\left|f\ast_{(1)}[\varphi^{(1,j)}]_{r}\ast_{(2)}[\varphi^{(2,k)}]_{s}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}_{0}}(f),

and the result follows by taking the supremum over all
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathsf{F}
.
∎

Remark 1.33
.

We will appeal several times to the following principle: when we deal with functionals that are sublinear and dilation invariant in Poisson bounded functions
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
, such as maximal functions, square functions and area functions, the estimation may be reduced to the case where
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi^{(1)})\subseteq\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,1)
and
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi^{(2)})\subseteq\bar{B}^{(2)}(0,1)
in a similar manner to that of the proof above.
Further, when we are dealing with square functions and area functions when the hypotheses include that
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
both have mean
0
, we may additionally assume that
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
have moments of homogeneous order at most
M
and
N
equal to
0
.
In light of Corollary
1.24
, this means that we may assume that
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
are derivatives.

An algebra that is also a Fréchet space is called a Fréchet algebra when multiplication is continuous in the Fréchet topology.

Proposition 1.34
.

The spaces
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{R})
are Fréchet algebras for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Proof.

We prove only the first assertion, as the other proof is very similar.
Let
\eta
be a
[0,1]
-valued function on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
that takes the value
1
on
B^{(1)}(o,1/2)
and vanishes off
B^{(1)}(o,1)
.

Take
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\psi^{(1)}
in
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
It is evident that, if
\left\|g\right\|\leq 2
, then

\displaystyle\bigl|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}(\varphi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)})(g)\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|(\varphi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\nabla_{(1)}^{q}\psi^{(1)})(g)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\varphi^{(1)}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\bigl\|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}\psi^{(1)}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim\left\|\varphi^{(1)}\right\|_{(0)}\left\|\psi^{(1)}\right\|_{(q)},

so we only need worry about estimating
\bigl|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}(\varphi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)})(g)\bigr|
when
\left\|g\right\|\geq 2
.
Fix
r\in[2,\infty)
, and set

\varphi^{(1)}_{1}(g):=\eta(D_{1/r}g)\varphi^{(1)}(g)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varphi^{(1)}_{2}(g):=\varphi^{(1)}(g)-\varphi^{(1)}_{1}(g)

for all
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
; define
\psi^{(1)}_{1}(g)
and
\psi^{(1)}_{2}
analogously.
Observe that, when
\left\|g\right\|\geq 2
,

\left\|g\right\|^{q+\delta+\theta}\bigl|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}\psi^{(1)}_{2}(g)\bigr|\lesssim_{q}\left\|\psi^{(1)}\right\|_{(q)},

because
\nabla_{(1)}^{q}\psi^{(1)}_{2}(g)=0
when
\left\|g\right\|\leq r/2
while
\nabla_{(1)}^{q}\psi^{(1)}_{2}(g)=\nabla_{(1)}^{q}\psi^{(1)}(g)
when
\left\|g\right\|\geq r
by our choice of
\eta
, and because differentiating
\eta(D_{1/r}g)

k
times introduces a factor of
r^{-k}
which is comparable in size to
\left\|g\right\|^{-k}
when
r/2<\left\|g\right\|<r
.
The implicit constant does not depend on either
g
or
r
.

We now suppose that
\left\|g\right\|=r
.
Because of our choice of
\eta
,
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{q}\varphi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)}(g)
vanishes.
Much as before, if
i
is either
1
or
2
, then

\displaystyle\bigl|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}(\varphi^{(1)}_{i}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)}_{2})(g)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|\varphi^{(1)}_{i}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\bigl\|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}\psi^{(1)}_{2}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|g\right\|^{-q-\delta-\theta}\left\|\varphi^{(1)}\right\|_{(0)}\left\|\psi^{(1)}\right\|_{(q)}.

Finally, by arguing as in Remark
1.27
, we see that

\displaystyle\bigl|\nabla_{(1)}^{q}(\varphi^{(1)}_{2}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)}_{1})(g)\bigr|\lesssim\left\|g\right\|^{-q-\delta-\theta}\left\|\varphi^{(1)}\right\|_{(2q)}\left\|\psi^{(1)}\right\|_{(0)},

as required.
∎

Similar results were proved in
[
30
]
.

1.8.
Geometry of cones

In this section, we prepare for later results on the nontangential maximal function and on the area function that show that changing the apertures of cones does not affect the corresponding Hardy space, and for the proof that the maximal function controls the area function.
We start by noting that when
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is a Poisson bounded pair, then
(g,r,s)\mapsto f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}
is continuous in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

First, we take a continuous function
F:\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times R^{+}\to[0,\infty)
, and define

(1.46)

F_{\alpha,\beta}(g)=\sup\{F(g^{\prime},r,s):g^{\prime}\in T(g,\alpha r,\beta s)\}.

Lemma 1.35
.

For all
F
as in (
1.46
), all
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta<\beta^{\prime}
, and all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

(1.47)

\displaystyle\|F_{\alpha,\beta}\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\|F_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\max\biggl\{\Bigl(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\Bigr)^{\delta},\Bigl(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\Bigr)^{2\nu}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}\biggr\}^{1+\varepsilon}\|F_{\alpha,\beta}\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

Proof.

The left hand inequality is trivial.
To prove the right hand inequality, we take
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and write
L(\lambda)
for the set
\{(g,r,s)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}:F(g,r,s)\geq\lambda\}
.
Since
g^{\prime}\in T(g,r,s)
if and only if
g\in T(g^{\prime},r,s)
, we see that

\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:F_{\alpha,\beta}(g)\geq\lambda\}=\bigcup_{(g^{\prime},r,s)\in L(\lambda)}T(g^{\prime},\alpha r,\beta s).

Suppose that
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha
and
\beta^{\prime}\geq\beta
.
Then for all
g\in T(g^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}r,\beta^{\prime}s)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{F}\mathbbm{1}_{T(g^{\prime},\alpha r,\beta s)}(g)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\left|T(g^{\prime},\alpha r,\beta s)\right|}{\left|T(g^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}r,\beta^{\prime}s)\right|}=\left(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{2\nu}\frac{(\alpha r)^{2}+\beta s}{(\alpha^{\prime}r)^{2}+\beta^{\prime}s}

\displaystyle\geq\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}\Bigr)^{2\nu}\min\left\{\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}\Bigr)^{2},\frac{\beta}{\beta^{\prime}}\right\},

(the equality follows from (
1.11
)), and so

\mathcal{M}_{F}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{(g^{\prime},r,s)\in L(\lambda)}T(g^{\prime},\alpha r,\beta s)}\geq\left(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{2\nu}\min\biggl\{\left(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{2},\frac{\beta}{\beta^{\prime}}\biggr\}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{(g^{\prime},r,s)\in L(\lambda)}T(g^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}r,\beta^{\prime}s)}.

That is,

\mathcal{M}_{F}\mathbbm{1}_{F_{\alpha,\beta}\geq\lambda}\geq\min\biggl\{\left(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{\delta},\left(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{2\nu}\frac{\beta}{\beta^{\prime}}\biggr\}\mathbbm{1}_{F_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\geq\lambda}.

Since
\mathcal{M}_{F}
is bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{1+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
,

\displaystyle\left|\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:F_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(g)\geq\lambda\}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\max\biggl\{\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\right)^{\delta},\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\right)^{2\nu}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}\biggr\}^{1+\varepsilon}\left|\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:F_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(g)\geq\lambda\}\right|.

Finally, by integrating the distribution functions, we conclude that

\left\|F_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\max\left\{\biggl(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\biggr)^{\delta},\biggl(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\biggr)^{2\nu}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}\right\}^{1+\varepsilon}\left\|F_{\alpha,\beta}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})},

as required.
∎

The next two lemmas will be used to study the area integral.

Lemma 1.36
.

For all
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and all continuous functions
F:\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\to[0,+\infty)
, define

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{\beta,\gamma}(F)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}(F(\cdot,r,s)\ast_{(1)}\chi^{(1)}_{\beta r}\ast_{(2)}\chi^{(2)}_{\gamma s})(g)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}g

(1.48)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{\beta,\gamma}(F)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(\iiint_{\Gamma_{\beta,\gamma}(g)}\frac{F(g^{\prime},r,s)}{\left|T(o,\beta r,\gamma s)\right|}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}g.

Then
\mathrm{I}_{\beta,\gamma}(F)\eqsim_{\beta,\gamma,\beta^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}\mathrm{I}_{\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(F)
and
\mathrm{I}_{\beta,\gamma/2}(F)\lesssim_{\beta,\gamma}\mathrm{J}_{\beta,\gamma}(F)\lesssim_{\beta,\gamma}\mathrm{I}_{\beta,\gamma}(F)
.

Proof.

First,

(1.49)

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}(F(\cdot,r,s)\ast_{(1)}\chi^{(1)}_{\beta r}\ast_{(2)}\chi^{(2)}_{\gamma s})(g)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}F(gg_{1},r,s)(\chi^{(1)}_{\beta r}*_{(2)}\chi^{(2)}_{\gamma s})(g_{1}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{\beta^{\delta}\gamma}\iiiint_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}F(gg_{1}g_{2},r,s)

\displaystyle\times\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(1)}(o,\beta r)}(g_{1})\mathbbm{1}_{B^{(2)}(0,\gamma s)}(g_{2})\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\frac{dr}{r^{\delta+1}}\,\frac{ds}{s^{2}}\,,

where
C
depends on
\nu
.
The last expression may be rewritten as either

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\beta^{\delta}\gamma}\iint_{\Gamma^{(1)}_{\beta}(o)}\left(\iint_{\Gamma^{(2)}_{\gamma}(0)}F(gg_{1}g_{2},r,s)\,\frac{ds}{s^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\right)\,\frac{dr}{r^{D+1}}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}

or

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\beta^{\delta}\gamma}\iint_{\Gamma^{(2)}_{\gamma}(0)}\left(\iint_{\Gamma^{(1)}_{\beta}(o)}F(gg_{1}g_{2},r,s)\,\frac{dr}{r^{D+1}}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\right)\,\frac{ds}{s^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}.

Hence
\mathrm{I}_{\beta,\gamma}
is equivalent to either

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\beta^{\delta}\gamma}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(\iint_{\Gamma^{(1)}_{\beta}(o)}\left(\iint_{\Gamma^{(2)}_{\gamma}(0)}F(gg_{1}g_{2},r,s)\,\frac{ds}{s^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\right)\,\frac{dr}{r^{D+1}}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}g

or

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\beta^{\delta}\gamma}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(\iint_{\Gamma^{(2)}_{\gamma}(0)}\left(\iint_{\Gamma^{(1)}_{\beta}(o)}F(gg_{1}g_{2},r,s)\,\frac{dr}{r^{D+1}}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\right)\,\frac{ds}{s^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}g.

It may be argued, as in the classical case (see
[
53
, pp. 125–126]
) that
changing the parameter
\beta
changes the first of these two expressions to an equivalent expression, and that changing the parameter
\gamma
changes the second to an equivalent expression (in this case, we need to write
g
as
(z,t)
and integrate with respect to first
t
and then
z
).
We conclude that changing
\beta
and
\gamma
changes
\mathrm{I}_{\beta,\gamma}
to an equivalent integral.

Finally, from Lemma
1.11
,

(1.50)

\mathrm{I}_{\beta,\gamma/2}\lesssim\mathrm{J}_{\beta,\gamma}\lesssim\mathrm{I}_{\beta,\gamma},

and the lemma follows.
∎

Before our next lemma, we recall that
J_{\beta,\gamma}(F)
was defined in (
1.48
).

Lemma 1.37
.

For all
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and all continuous functions
F:\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
J_{\beta,\gamma}(F)<\infty
,

\lim_{g^{\prime\prime}\to o}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(\iiint_{\Gamma_{\beta,\gamma}(g)}\frac{\left|F(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime},r,s)-F(g^{\prime},r,s)\right|}{\left|T(o,\beta r,\gamma s)\right|}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}g=0.

Proof.

First, by several applications of Lebesgue’s convergence theorem,

\lim_{b\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(\int_{1/b}^{b}\int_{1/b}^{b}\int_{T(g,\beta r,\gamma s)}\frac{\left|F(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime},r,s)\right|}{\left|T(o,\beta r,\gamma s)\right|}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}g=0,

and then, for each
b\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\lim_{a\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\setminus B^{(1)}(o,a)}\left(\int_{1/b}^{b}\int_{1/b}^{b}\int_{T(g,\beta r,\gamma s)}\frac{\left|F(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime},r,s)\right|}{\left|T(o,\beta r,\gamma s)\right|}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}g=0,

uniformly for
g^{\prime\prime}\in B^{(1)}(o,1)
in both cases.
On the other hand, by continuity and compactness,

\lim_{g^{\prime\prime}\to o}\left|F(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime},r,s)-F(g^{\prime},r,s)\right|=0

uniformly for
g^{\prime\prime}\in\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,1)
,
g\in\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,a)
, and
g^{\prime}\in T(0,r,s)
where
1/b\leq r,s\leq b
.
The lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 1.38
.

There exists a positive geometric constant
C_{1}
such that, if
E
is a subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure, and
\Omega=\bigcup_{g\in E}\Gamma(g)
, then
\mathbbm{1}_{E}\ast_{(1)}p_{r,s}(g^{\prime})\geq C_{1}
for all
(g^{\prime},r,s)\in\Omega
.

Proof.

By Lemma
1.29
,
\mathbbm{1}_{E}\ast_{(1)}p_{r,s}(g^{\prime})\gtrsim_{\beta,\gamma}\mathbbm{1}_{E}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{\beta r,\gamma s}(g^{\prime})
for all
(g^{\prime},r,s)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
and all
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and we are done.
∎

1.9.
Spectral theory

In much of the literature on analysis on the Heisenberg group, such as
[
26
]
or
[
30
]
, the closed subalgebra
\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
of
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
consisting of limits of linear combinations of the heat kernels
h^{(1)}_{r}
plays an important role.
The Hulanicki functional calculus
[
34
]
is used in place of the Fourier transformation.
Unfortunately,
\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is not closed under pointwise multiplication and “cut-off arguments” are problematic.
Fortunately, the functional calculus of
[
2
]
allows us to deal with “radial” functions, and “cut-off arguments” are straightforward in this context.
It turns out that radial functions provide a natural setting for the joint spectral calculus of
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
and
\mathcal{T}
.

On the Heisenberg group, radial functions (and radial distributions), that is, those that are invariant under the action of the rotations in
\mathrm{U}(n)
, form a commutative algebra.
This was noticed in
[
35
]
(though as is often the case with analysis on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, the necessary calculations to show this may be found in
[
25
]
, but the result is not stated explicitly there).
In particular the differential operators
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
and
i\mathcal{T}
are radial and essentially self-adjoint.
Spectral theory, combining the general features of abstract theory, as in, for instance,
[
41
]
, with the additional features available because we are working on a Lie group, has been used to develop a functional calculus for these operators (see
[
2
]
and the references cited there), so that expressions
\Phi(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
and
\Phi(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},\mathcal{L}_{(2)})
are defined for functions
\Phi
in
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and these operators are given by right convolution with kernels
k_{\Phi}
in
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
The functional calculus may be extended to other classes of functions, such as bounded continuous functions, or rational functions, at the cost of using distributional kernels.

Let
\mathbb{F}
denote the Heisenberg fan, that is,

\{((2d+n)|\lambda|,\lambda)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:d\in\mathbb{N},\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\}\cup\{(\xi_{1},0):\xi_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\}.

We shall use the following features of this theory.

Theorem 1.39
.

There exists an isometry
\mathcal{F}:L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\to\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{N}}L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{H}_{d})
, where each
\mathcal{H}_{d}
is a Hilbert space, such that

(1)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}=\left(\sum_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{F}f(d,\lambda)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}^{2}\lambda^{\delta-1}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\right)^{1/2}

for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
;

(2)

\mathcal{F}(\Phi(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})f)(d,\lambda)=\Phi((2d+n)|\lambda|,\lambda)\mathcal{F}(f)(d,\lambda)
for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
, all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, all
f\in\operatorname{Dom}\Phi(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
, and all Schwartz functions or polynomials
\Phi
;

(3)

let
\mathcal{A}
be a continuous linear operator from
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to its dual space
\mathsf{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
that commutes with (left) translations.
Then there exists
\Phi\in\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\mathcal{A}=\Phi(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
if and only if
\mathcal{A}
has a radial convolution kernel
k_{\Phi}
in
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

If
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is even in the second variable, we may interpret the theorem as giving information about
\Psi(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},\mathcal{L}_{(2)})
, where
\Psi(\mu,\lambda)=\Phi(\mu,\lambda^{2})
.

In particular, the space of kernels of convolution operators
\Phi(\mathcal{L}_{(1)})
, where
\Phi\in\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{R})
, is a subalgebra of the convolution algebra of radial Schwartz functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, and the space of kernels of convolution operators
\Phi(\mathcal{L}_{(1)})
, where
\Phi\in\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{R})
, is an algebra that may be identified with the convolution algebra of even Schwartz functions on the centre
\mathbb{R}
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.

For the reader who may be interested in the extension of our results, we mention that this result has been extended to more general contexts, including stratified nilpotent groups, by Martini
[
40
]
.

1.10.
Homogeneous singular integrals and distributions

In the context of euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it is well known that the following are equivalent (see, e.g,
[
53
, Section XIII.5.3]
):

(a)

K
is a distribution that is smooth away from
0
and is homogeneous of degree
-n
;

(b)

K
is a linear combination of the Dirac delta at
0
and a principal value distribution
K_{0}
, where
K_{0}
is homogeneous of degree
-n
, is smooth away from
0
, and has mean
0
on spheres with centre
0
;

(c)

K
may be expressed as an integral
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\omega_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t/t
, where
\omega
is in
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and has mean
0
.

The same holds in the Heisenberg group (and indeed on more general stratified groups).
The following lemma may be extracted from
[
53
, Section II.8.19]
(for one direction of the proof).

Lemma 1.40
.

Suppose that
\omega
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is Poisson bounded and has mean
0
, that
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, that
c,d\in\mathbb{Z}
and that
\alpha>1
.

If
a\to 0
and
b\to\infty
, then

k_{a,b}:=\int_{a}^{b}\omega^{(1)}_{t}\,\frac{dt}{t}

converges in
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\prime}
and in
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\setminus\{o\})
to a distribution
k
that is homogeneous of degree
-\delta
, that is,
(k,\varphi)=(k,\varphi\circ D_{r})
for all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Further, away from
o
,
k
is given by integration against a smooth function that is homogeneous of degree
-\delta
and has mean
0
on the unit sphere of any smooth homogeneous norm on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
The associated convolution operators are uniformly bounded in
a
and
b
on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
p\in(1,\infty)
, with a bound that depends on
p
and on
\left\|\omega\right\|_{(1)}
, and hence converge strongly as
a\to 0
and
b\to\infty
.
If
a
is fixed and
b\to\infty
, then the distributions
k_{a,b}
converge in
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\prime}
to a distribution
k_{a,\infty}
that is given by integration against a smooth function, and the associated convolution operators are uniformly bounded in
a
on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
p\in(1,\infty)
.
If
a\to 0
and
b
is fixed, then the distributions
k_{a,b}
converge in
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\prime}
to a distribution
k_{0,b}
that is given by integration against a smooth function away from
o
, and the associated convolution operators are uniformly bounded in
b
on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
p\in(1,\infty)
.

Likewise, if
c\to-\infty
and
d\to\infty
in
\mathbb{Z}
, then

\tilde{k}_{c,d}:=\sum_{m=c}^{d}\omega^{(1)}_{\alpha^{m}}

converges in
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\prime}
and in

\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\setminus\{o\})
to a distribution
\tilde{k}
that is discretely homogeneous of degree
-\delta
, in the sense that
(\tilde{k},\varphi)=(\tilde{k},\varphi\circ D_{\alpha^{m}})
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, and is given by integration against a smooth function away from
o
.
The associated convolution operators are uniformly bounded in
c
and
d
on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
p\in(1,\infty)
, with a bound that depends on
p
and on
\left\|\omega\right\|_{(1)}
, and hence converge strongly as
c\to-\infty
and
d\to\infty
.

The mappings
\omega\mapsto k
and
\omega\mapsto\tilde{k}
from
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
are continuous.

Conversely, every homogeneous distribution that is smooth away from
o

arises in this way, for some
\omega\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Analogous results hold on
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We sketch the forward direction of the proof, treating only
\tilde{k}_{c,d}
; the other case is similar.

It is easy to check that
\mathcal{D}k_{c,d}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\setminus B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon))
for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and all left-invariant differential operators
\mathcal{D}
.
Consider the sum

\sum_{m=c}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\omega_{\alpha^{m}}(g)\,\psi(g)\,\mathrm{d}g,

where
\psi\in\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
; if
\psi
vanishes at
0
, this converges absolutely, and if
\psi
is constant near
o
, then we may use the cancellation of
\omega
to show convergence.

The Cotlar–Stein lemma
[
53
, p. 280]
and the (Heisenberg group version of) the Hörmander cancellation condition
[
33
]
may be used to show that convolution with
\tilde{k}_{c,d}
is bounded uniformly in
c
and
d
on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p<\infty
; the uniform bound for the operator norms depends on
p
and on
\left\|\omega\right\|_{(1)}
.

The continuity claimed may be established by checking continuity at each step of the argument above.

For the converse, we treat the continuous case only; it is useful to use the Korányi norm (or another smooth norm).
Let
k
be a homogeneous distribution that is smooth away from
0
.
Then necessarily
k=c\delta_{o}+k_{0}
, where
c
is a constant and
k_{0}
is a smooth principal value distribution;
k_{0}
has integral
0
over the unit sphere relative to the Korányi norm.
It is not hard to find a nonnegative-real-valued
\mathsf{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
-function
\eta
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\eta\left(\frac{\cdot}{t}\right)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{t}=1.

Set
\omega=k_{0}\eta(|\cdot|)
; then
\omega\in\mathsf{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\omega_{t}(\cdot)\,\frac{dt}{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}t^{-\delta}\eta\Bigl(\frac{|\cdot|}{t}\Bigr)k_{0}\left(\frac{\cdot}{t}\right)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{t}=k_{0}\left(\cdot\right).

Thus the principal value distribution
k_{0}
arises in this way.

The unit sphere
S
is a smooth submanifold, and every
x\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\setminus\{o\}
may be written uniquely in the form
D_{r}\sigma
, where
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\sigma\in S
(see
[
24
, Proposition 1.15]
for more information about polar coordinates).
For
f\in\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, define the function
F
on
\mathbb{R}\times S
by
F(u,\sigma):=f(D_{e^{u}}\sigma)
.
Take
\Phi\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Phi(u)\,\mathrm{d}u=1
, and define
