\zeta\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
by
s^{\delta}\zeta(s)=\Phi^{\prime}(\log(s))
.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}f(x)\zeta_{t}(|x|)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{S}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}f(D_{s}\sigma)t^{-\delta}\zeta(s/t)s^{\delta-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\,\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{S}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}f(D_{s}\sigma)(s/t)^{\delta}\zeta(s/t)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}s}{s}\,\frac{dt}{t}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{S}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}f(D_{st}\sigma)s^{\delta}\zeta(s)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}s}{s}\,\frac{dt}{t}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{S}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}f(D_{e^{u+v}}\sigma)\mathrm{e}^{\delta u}\zeta(e^{u})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{S}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}F(u+v,\sigma)\Phi^{\prime}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=-\int_{S}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}F^{\prime}(u+v,\sigma)\Phi(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=-\int_{S}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}F^{\prime}(u+v,\sigma)\Phi(u)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=-\int_{S}\int_{\mathbb{R}}[F(\infty,\sigma)-F(-\infty,\sigma)]\Phi(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{S}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(o)\Phi(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma f(o),

that is,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\zeta_{t}(|\cdot|)\,\frac{dt}{t}=\left(\int_{S}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\right)\delta_{o},

and so
\delta_{o}
may also be represented in the required form.
∎

For more on principal value convolution with such kernels, see
[
23
]
,
[
38
]
, or
[
53
]
.

Definition 1.41
.

A distribution
k
as constructed in the previous lemma is called a
simple homogeneous singular integral kernel
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
(of type
0
); a
simple homogeneous singular integral operator
is a convolution (on the right) by such a kernel.
Simple homogeneous singular integrals on
\mathbb{R}
are defined analogously.

A
homogeneous flag singular integral kernel
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is the convolution of a
simple homogeneous singular integral kernel
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
with a
simple homogeneous singular integral kernel
on
\mathbb{R}
.

In this section, for simplicity, we omit the word homogeneous.

Flag singular integral operators may be realised as expressions of the form

f\mapsto\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}r}{r}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}s}{s}\,,

where the convergence of the double integral is much as in Lemma
1.40
.

The simple singular integral operators on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
form an algebra under composition; likewise the simple singular integral operators on
\mathbb{R}
form an algebra.
Since
\mathbb{R}
is central in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, it is easy to see that the flag singular integral operators on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
also form an algebra under composition.
Nagel, Ricci, Stein and Wainger
[
48
]
showed that the flag singular integral operators form an algebra in much more general circumstances.

Simple singular integral operators on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
are bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p<\infty
and on the Folland–Stein–Christ–Geller Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
In Section 8 we consider more general flag singular integrals studied by Phong and Stein
[
50
]
and by Nagel, Ricci, Stein and Wainger
[
47
,
48
]
.

We recall that the (tensor-valued) flag Riesz transformations are defined by

\mathcal{R}_{(1)}=\nabla_{(1)}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-1/2},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{(2)}=\nabla_{(2)}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-1/2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{R}_{F}=\mathcal{R}_{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{R}_{(2)}.

It is well known that
\mathcal{R}_{(2)}
is the Hilbert transformation, which is a convolution with an odd singular kernel.
Likewise
\mathcal{R}_{(1)}
is a convolution with an odd singular kernel.
Indeed,
\mathcal{R}_{(1)}
is
\mathsf{L}^{2}
bounded by spectral theory, and is a convolution with a distribution that is homogeneous of degree
-\delta
.
By the subellipticity of
\nabla_{(1)}
, this distribution may be identified with a smooth kernel away from the identity.
Any additional component of the distribution must be a multiple of the Dirac delta distribution at the group identity, but since
\mathcal{R}_{(1)}
is odd, the multiple must be
0
.
For further information, the reader should consult the cited references.

1.11.
The Calderón reproducing formula

Before our next definitions, we recall that Poisson boundedness is defined in Definition
1.26
.

Definition 1.42
.

Fix
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We write
\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\cdot\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\otimes}
for the space of all linear combinations of expressions
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{T}^{n}f
, where
\mathcal{D}
is a product of
m
vector fields, each chosen from
\{\mathcal{X}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{X}_{2\nu}\}
, while
f\in\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
We define
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{m}\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\cdot\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\otimes}
analogously, with right invariant vector fields.

Definition 1.43
.

A Poisson-bounded, possibly vector-valued, function
\varphi
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
is said to be continuous or discretely w-invertible if there exists a Poisson bounded function
\psi
, called a continuous or discrete w-inverse of
\varphi
, such that

(1.51)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}\psi_{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}=\delta\qquad\text{or}\qquad\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi_{\alpha^{m}}\ast_{(1)}\psi_{\alpha^{m}}=\delta

(here
\delta
is the delta of Dirac and
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
), as in Lemma
1.40
(where
\omega=\varphi\ast_{(1)}\psi
).
If
\varphi
is vector-valued, then
\psi
must be dual-vector-valued and the convolution
\varphi\ast_{(1)}\psi
taken as scalar-valued.

Analogous definitions apply to Poisson bounded functions on
\mathbb{R}
, and to pairs of Poisson bounded functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and on
\mathbb{R}
.

It is advantageous to have w-inverses with rapid decay and lots of cancellation.
We do this by finding w-inverses in
\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\cdot\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\otimes}
, when
m
and
n
are large.

The letter w stands for weak or for wavelet; (
1.51
) (or its analogue on
\mathbb{R}
) is called the Calderón reproducing formula.
It is sometimes said that
\varphi
and
\psi
satisfy the Calderón condition.

Gradients may be dealt with using integrations by parts.
As we have already noted, there are distributions
\Upsilon^{j}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
such that
\mathcal{X}^{j}f=f\ast_{(1)}\Upsilon^{j}
for all differentiable functions
f
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
It follows that

(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\varphi)\ast_{(1)}\psi=\sum_{j=1}^{2\nu}\varphi\ast_{(1)}\Upsilon^{j}\ast_{(1)}\Upsilon^{j}\ast_{(1)}\psi=\nabla_{(1)}\varphi\ast_{(1)}\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}\mathring{\psi},

where
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}
denotes a left gradient rather than a right gradient.
Similar considerations hold for gradients in the central variable, and we conclude that if
(\mathring{\psi}^{(1)},\mathring{\psi}^{(2)})
is a w-inverse for
(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)},\mathcal{L}_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)})
, then
(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}\mathring{\psi}^{(1)},\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(2)}\mathring{\psi}^{(2)})
is a w-inverse for
(\nabla_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)},\nabla_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)})
.

In
\mathbb{R}
, or more generally
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the Fourier transformation is used to understand the Calderón condition.
In
\mathbb{R}
all nonzero Poisson bounded functions are w-invertible; a Poisson bounded function
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is w-invertible if and only if its Fourier transform
\hat{\varphi}
does not vanish on any ray emanating from
0
.

In the literature that we have found on the Calderón condition in the context of the Heisenberg group, such as
[
26
]
or
[
30
]
, the closed subalgebra
\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
of
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
mentioned at the start of Section
1.9
plays an important role.
The functions
\varphi^{(1)}
and their w-inverses belong to
\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, or are gradients of such functions.
However these results extend to more general
\varphi
.
Indeed, the functional calculus of
[
2
]
allows us to deal with the case where
\varphi^{(1)}
is radial.

The set of invertible elements in a Banach algebra is open.
A similar argument applies to the set of w-invertible elements.

Lemma 1.44
.

Suppose that
\varphi^{(1)}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
has continuous or discrete w-inverse
\psi^{(1)}
.
All
\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}
that are sufficiently close to
\varphi^{(1)}
in
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
have a w-inverse
\tilde{\psi}^{(1)}
that is close to
\psi^{(1)}
in
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
An analogous result holds for Poisson bounded functions
\varphi^{(2)}
on
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We consider the discrete case on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
only; the continuous case is similar, and the analysis on
\mathbb{R}
is simpler.
For notational simplicity, we write
\varphi
instead of
\varphi^{(1)}
,
\psi
instead of
\psi^{(1)}
, and so on.

If
\tilde{\varphi}
is close to
\varphi
in
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, then
\tilde{\omega}:=\tilde{\varphi}\ast_{(1)}\psi
is close to
\varphi\ast_{(1)}\psi
in
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Then
\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\omega}_{\alpha^{m}}
is close to
\delta
as a homogeneous distribution, so convolution with
\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\omega}_{\alpha^{m}}
is close to the identity as an operator on
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and hence is invertible as an operator on
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
with an inverse that is close to the identity operator.
The inverse operator is also convolution with a homogeneous distribution by
[
10
, Theorem B]
, and is necessarily close to
\delta
in the space of homogeneous distributions that are smooth away from
o
.
∎

The final results of this section connect w-invertibility to square functions.
Suppose that
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
are Poisson bounded and have mean
0
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\mathbb{R}
respectively.
Recall that the sublinear operator
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
is defined on
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
by

\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)=\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(g)\right|^{2}\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}.

Similar operators that involve only one convolution are more standard.

Corollary 1.45
.

Suppose that
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
are Poisson bounded and have mean
0
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\mathbb{R}
respectively.
Then

(1.52)

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}(\cdot)\right|^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim_{\varphi^{(1)}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

(1.53)

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(\cdot)\right|^{2}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim_{\varphi^{(2)}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

(1.54)

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(\cdot)\right|^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

for all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Moreover,
\varphi^{(1)}
has a w-inverse
\mathring{\varphi}^{(1)}
with mean
0
, or
\varphi^{(2)}
has a w-inverse
\mathring{\varphi}^{(2)}
with mean
0
, if and only if
converse inequalities to (
1.52
), (
1.53
) or (
1.54
) hold, with constants that depend on
\mathring{\varphi}^{(1)}
or
\mathring{\varphi}^{(2)}
.
We may find w-inverses
\mathring{\varphi}^{(1)}
and
\mathring{\varphi}^{(2)}
belonging to the spaces
\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\cdot\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\otimes}
and
\nabla_{(2)}^{n}\cdot\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{R})
for arbitrarily large
m
and
n
.

Proof.

We treat only one of these sublinear operators, as all are similar.
Observe that

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\ast_{(1)}[\varphi_{r,s}]^{*}(g)\bar{f}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}f\ast_{(1)}\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\varphi_{r,s}\ast_{(1)}[\varphi_{r,s}]^{*}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)(g)\bar{f}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{K}f(g)\bar{f}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g,

where
\mathcal{K}
is a convolution with a flag singular integral kernel as in Definition
1.41
(where we take
\omega^{(1)}
to be
\varphi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}[\varphi^{(1)}]^{*}
and define
\omega^{(2)}
similarly), and
\mathcal{K}
is bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Hence

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\leq\left\|\mathcal{K}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2},

as required.
If
\mathcal{K}
is the identity operator, then
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
is isometric.
A polarisation argument shows that the converse also holds.

Suppose that
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
have w-inverses
\mathring{\varphi}^{(1)}
and
\mathring{\varphi}^{(2)}
with mean
0
, and write
\psi^{(1)}
and
\psi^{(2)}
for
[\mathring{\varphi}^{(1)}]^{*}
and
[\mathring{\varphi}^{(2)}]^{*}
.
Then
\psi^{(1)}
and
\psi^{(2)}
are Poisson bounded and have mean
0
, and

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left<f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}\ast_{(1)}\mathring{\varphi}^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\mathring{\varphi}^{(2)}_{s},f\right>\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left<f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s},f\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\psi^{(2)}_{s}\right>\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left\|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\times\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left\|f\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\psi^{(2)}_{s}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\times\left\|\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}f\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\psi^{(2)}_{s}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\mathring{\varphi}}}\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})},

by the first part of the lemma, and
\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
, as claimed.

Conversely, suppose that
\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
.
Then from the first part of the lemma,

(1.55)

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim\left\|\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}(\cdot)\right|^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

and

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim\left\|\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(\cdot)\right|^{2}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

for all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
The second inequality implies that

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\left\|\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(\cdot)\right|^{2}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

for all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

From (
1.52
) and (
1.55
), the singular integral operator

f\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(1)}[\varphi^{(1)}]^{*}_{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}

is invertible as an operator on
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
It was shown by Christ and Geller
[
10
, Theorem B]
that if a simple singular integral operator is invertible as an operator on
\mathsf{L}^{2}(G)
, then the inverse operator is also a simple singular integral operator.
Thus there is a simple singular integral kernel
k
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(1)}[\varphi^{(1)}]^{*}_{r}\ast_{(1)}k\,\frac{dr}{r}=f

for all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Now
[\varphi^{(1)}]^{*}_{r}\ast_{(1)}k=[[\varphi^{(1)}]^{*}\ast_{(1)}k]_{r}
, and we may easily check that
\psi^{(1)}:=[\varphi^{(1)}]^{*}\ast_{(1)}k
has mean
0
, is Poisson bounded, and is a w-inverse for
\varphi^{(1)}
.
We may find a w-inverse for
\varphi^{(2)}
similarly.

To find a w-inverse with rapid decay, we take a w-inverse
\mathring{\varphi}^{(1)}
and modify it.
Fix
\theta\in(0,1)
.
By applying Corollary
1.31
, we may approximate
\mathring{\varphi}^{(1)}
in
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
by a function
\psi^{(1)}
in the space
\nabla_{(1)}^{m}\cdot\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\otimes}
.
Since
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is a Fréchet algebra, by Proposition
1.34
,
\varphi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)}
approximates
\varphi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\mathring{\varphi}^{(1)}
in
\mathsf{P}^{\theta}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and then from Lemma
1.40
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\varphi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)})_{t}\,dt/t
approximates
\delta
in the space of distributions, and if the approximation is good, the associated operator is invertible.
Now we can find a simple singular integral kernel
k
such that
\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\varphi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)})_{t}\,dt/t\bigr)\ast_{(1)}k=\delta
, that is,
\psi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}k
is a w-inverse for
\varphi^{(1)}
.
Since
\psi^{(1)}=\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{m}\rho
for some
\rho\in\mathsf{P}^{\otimes}
,

\psi^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}k=\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{m}\rho\ast_{(1)}k\in\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{m}\mathsf{P}^{\otimes}
.
∎

Lemma 1.46
.

Suppose that
\varphi\in\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\varphi(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=1
.
Then
\varphi
is w-invertible, and for all
M\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
there exists
\psi\in\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\otimes}
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\varphi\ast_{(1)}\nabla_{(1)}^{M}\cdot\psi)_{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}=\delta.

Proof.

This proof is similar to the last part of the proof of Corollary
1.45
, so we shall be brief.

Let
q
be
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}p_{1}
.
Then
q\in\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\nabla}_{(1)}^{2M}\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, so, by the functional calculus of
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}q_{t}\,\frac{dt}{t}=c\delta,

for a suitable nonzero constant
c
.

It is easy to check that when
s
is small,
\varphi_{s}\ast_{(1)}q
is close to
q
in
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, since
(\varphi_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}
is an approximate identity for convolution, whence
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\varphi_{s}\ast_{(1)}q)_{t}\,dt/t
is close to
c\delta
in
\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})^{\prime}
, and so, by
[
10
, Theorem B]
, there exists a simple singular integral kernel
k
such that

\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\varphi\ast_{(1)}q_{1/s})_{t}\,\frac{dt}{t}\Bigr)\ast_{(1)}k=c\delta.

Now
c^{-1}q_{1/s}\ast_{(1)}k
has the required properties.
∎

Finally we show that if
\varphi
is w-invertible continuously, then a related function
\psi
is w-invertible discretely.
This will enable us to connect discrete square functions and continuous square functions.

Lemma 1.47
.

Suppose that
\varphi
is Poisson bounded on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, with w-inverse
\mathring{\varphi}
, as in Definition
1.43
.
For
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
, define

\psi=\int_{1}^{\alpha}\varphi_{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,.

If
\alpha
is close enough to
1
, then
\psi
is w-invertible discretely.
Further,

\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{dis},\boldsymbol{\psi}}(f)\leq\log^{1/2}(\alpha)\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\qquad\forall f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}).

An analogous result holds for functions on
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

By hypothesis,

\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{1}^{\alpha}\varphi_{r\alpha^{m}}\ast_{(1)}\mathring{\varphi}_{r\alpha^{m}}\,\frac{dr}{r}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}\mathring{\varphi}_{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}=\delta.

Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}[\psi\ast_{(1)}\mathring{\varphi}]_{\alpha^{m}}

\displaystyle=\delta-\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{1}^{\alpha}\varphi_{r\alpha^{m}}\ast_{(1)}[\mathring{\varphi}_{r\alpha^{m}}-\mathring{\varphi}_{\alpha^{m}}]\,\frac{dr}{r}

\displaystyle=\delta-\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left[\int_{1}^{\alpha}\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}[\mathring{\varphi}_{r}-\mathring{\varphi}]\,\frac{dr}{r}\right]_{\alpha^{m}}

\displaystyle=\delta-\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}[\omega]_{\alpha^{m}},

say.
If
\alpha
is close to
1
, then
\omega
is small in
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, so
\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}[\omega]_{\alpha^{m}}
is small in the space of homogeneous distributions, and
\delta-\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}[\omega]_{\alpha^{m}}
is invertible.
Let
k
be the kernel of the inverse operator.
Much as in the previous lemma, we may write

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left[\psi\ast_{(1)}\mathring{\varphi}\ast_{(1)}k\right]_{\alpha^{m}}

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}[\psi\ast_{(1)}\mathring{\varphi}]_{\alpha^{m}}\right]\ast_{(1)}k=\delta,

and
\mathring{\varphi}\ast_{(1)}k
is the desired discrete w-inverse of
\psi
.

The second claim of the lemma is true for all positive
\alpha
: by definition and Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{dis},\boldsymbol{\psi}}(f)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\psi_{\alpha^{m}}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\int_{1}^{\alpha}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r\alpha^{m}}\right|\,\frac{dr}{r}\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\log(\alpha)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{1}^{\alpha}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r\alpha^{m}}\right|^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\log(\alpha)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r}\right|^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\log(\alpha)^{1/2}\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f),

as required.
∎

It is evident that if

\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{\alpha^{m}}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\qquad\forall f\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}),

then

\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{\beta^{m}}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\qquad\forall f\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}).

when
\beta
is a positive integral power of
\alpha
.

2.
The atomic Hardy space

In this section we focus on the Hardy space defined using atomic decompositions.
We first examine the definition and properties of the space in more detail than most previous studies, even in the product case, and then show that if
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, then
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
,
\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and
\mathcal{K}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
\mathcal{K}
is a simple singular integral operator.

2.1.
A precise definition of the atomic Hardy space

We recall the definition of the atomic Hardy space, and make it more precise.

Definition 2.1
.

Fix
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and a real number
\kappa\in(1+1/(2\nu),\infty)
.
An
atom
is a function
a\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that there exist an open subset
E
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure
|E|
and functions
a_{R}
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, called
particles
, and
b_{R}
in
\operatorname{Dom}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N})
for all
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
such that

(A1)

a_{R}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b_{R}
and
\operatorname{supp}b_{R}\subseteq R^{*}=T(g,\kappa q,\kappa^{2}h)
, where
g=\operatorname{c}(R)
,
q=\operatorname{w}(R)
and
h=\operatorname{h}(R)
;

(A2)

for all sign sequences
\sigma:\mathfrak{M}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
, the sum
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\sigma_{R}a_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
a_{\sigma}
say, and

(2.1)

\left\|a_{\sigma}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\left|E\right|^{-1/2};

(A3)

a=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}a_{R}
.

We say that
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
has an atomic decomposition if we may write
f
as a sum
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
, converging in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, where
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\lambda_{j}|<\infty
and each
a_{j}
is an atom; we write
f\sim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
to indicate that
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
is an atomic decomposition of
f
.
The space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is defined to be the linear space of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
that have atomic decompositions, with norm

(2.2)

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\inf\biggl\{\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\lambda_{j}|:f\sim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}\biggr\}.

The constant
\kappa
is called the enlargement parameter.

A few comments are in order.

First,
\left\|a\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq 1
for each atom
a
, so the sum representing
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
as a weighted sum of atoms converges in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
; hence
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Next, our norm condition (A2) implies the usual condition that
\left\|a\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq|E|^{-1/2}
, and, by a randomisation argument, that

(2.3)

\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\leq|E|^{-1};

This condition is standard in much of the literature.
Our condition is just as easy to verify, and is more useful; it is a quantitative form of unconditional convergence.
In particular, it shows that the sum converges in any order, and with any regroupings, and implies a similar inequality to (
2.1
) when
\sigma
is just a bounded sequence.
See, for instance,
[
39
, Section 1.c]
for information about unconditional convergence.

In particular, it is known that for all small positive
\varepsilon
, we can find a finite subset
\mathfrak{F}
of
\mathfrak{M}(E)
such that for any subset
\mathfrak{F}_{1}
of
\mathfrak{M}(E)
that is disjoint from
\mathfrak{F}
,
\left\|\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}}a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}<\varepsilon
.
Hence if
\sigma
is any sign function, and
\mathfrak{F}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{-}
are the subsets of
\mathfrak{M}(E)\setminus\mathfrak{F}
where
\sigma
is positive and negative, then

\biggl\|\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)\setminus\mathfrak{F}}\sigma_{R}a_{R}\biggr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\biggl\|\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{F}_{+}}a_{R}\biggr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\biggl\|\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{F}_{-}}a_{R}\biggr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}<2\varepsilon.

Third, the enlargement
R^{*}
of
R
has the same centre as
R
, but is “bigger and smoother” than
R
.

If we need to be more precise, we say that a particle is an
(M,N)
particle, and that an atom
a
and the Hardy space as above are a
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atom and the
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atomic Hardy space.
We will discuss the dependence of the atomic Hardy space on
\kappa
in the following section.

We note that if
a\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and
a=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{M}b
, where
b\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\operatorname{supp}b\subseteq R
for some
R\in\mathfrak{R}
, then
a=\left\|a\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}|R|^{1/2}c
, where
c=\left\|a\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{-1}|R|^{-1/2}a
, and
c
is an atom, so that

(2.4)

\left\|a\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq|R|^{1/2}\left\|a\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

2.2.
Properties of the atomic Hardy space

In this section, we clarify some properties of the atomic Hardy space in the flag setting.
We examine the dependence on the enlargement parameter, we show that there is a dense subspace with atomic decompositions that converge in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
as well as in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and we show that linear or nonnegative-real-valued sublinear operators that are bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and which send particles into
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and satisfy certain decay estimates are bounded from
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
into
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

In the classical theory, Hardy spaces may be defined using atoms which are naturally
\mathsf{L}^{p}
functions, where
p
may be any index greater than
1
, including
\infty
, and the convergence considered was a simple
\ell^{1}
sum.
In later versions of the theory, matters became more complicated, and in particular, in some Hardy spaces associated to rough differential operators, it is not at all clear whether the norm is given by the expression (
2.2
).
One aim of this section is to establish that we do have such a representation.

The first point is that the
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
norm conditions on the particles
a_{R}
imply norm conditions on the functions
b_{R}
and certain of their derivatives.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
R\in\mathfrak{R}
and
R^{*}=T(g,\kappa q/2,\kappa^{2}(4h+q^{2})/8)
, where
g=\operatorname{c}(R)
,
q=\operatorname{w}(R)
and
h=\operatorname{h}(R)
.
Suppose moreover that
a_{R}\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
,
b_{R}\in\operatorname{Dom}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N})
,
a_{R}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b_{R}
and
\operatorname{supp}b_{R}\subseteq R^{*}
, as in Definition
2.1
.
Choose
m
and
n
such that
0\leq m\leq M
and
0\leq n\leq N
, and define
c_{R}
to be
q^{2m-2M}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}h^{2n-2N}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{n}b_{R}
.
Then

(2.5)

\left\|c_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim_{M,N,\kappa}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

Proof.

Iterate the second inequality of Lemma
1.19

M-m
times and
the first
N-n
times, noting that
h\gtrsim q^{2}
since

R\in\mathfrak{R}
, so the height of the tube is controlled by a multiple of
h
.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

This lemma means that it is not necessary for us to be concerned about
\left\|c_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
.
Some previous work on atomic Hardy spaces in the product and flag setting has imposed conditions on
\left\|c_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
(or the equivalent), but this is only sometimes necessary.

Note that subelliptic regularity (see, for instance,
[
23
, Theorem 6.1]
) implies that, for any atom
a
, there exists a function
b
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that
a=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b
.
If
E
is bounded or if
N=0
, then it is evident that
b\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, but if
E
is unbounded and
N=0
, then this is not so.

Our second remark is that in the definition of an atom, we may parametrise particles by general, not necessarily maximal, shards, and we may group together the particles in many ways.

Lemma 2.4
.

Fix
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and a real number
\kappa
in
(1+1/(2\nu),\infty)
.
Let
E
be an open subset of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure and
\mathfrak{R}(E)
be the set of subshards of
E
.
Suppose that there exist functions
a_{R}
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
b_{R}
in
\operatorname{Dom}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N})
for all
R\in\mathfrak{R}(E)
such that

(A1)

a_{R}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b_{R}
and
\operatorname{supp}b_{R}\subseteq R^{*}
;

(A2)

for all sign sequences
\sigma:\mathfrak{R}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
, the sum
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{R}(E)}\sigma_{R}a_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
a_{\sigma}
say, and

\left\|a_{\sigma}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\left|E\right|^{-1/2};

(A3)

a=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{R}(E)}a_{R}
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{S}
is a subcollection of
\mathfrak{R}(E)
and
\dagger:\mathfrak{R}(E)\to\mathfrak{S}
is a mapping such that
R\subseteq R^{\dagger}
.
Then
for each
S\in\mathfrak{S}
, the sum
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{R}(E):R^{\dagger}=S}a_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
\tilde{a}_{S}
, say, and
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{R}(E):R^{\dagger}=S}b_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
\tilde{b}_{S}
, say.
Further,

(B1)

\tilde{a}_{S}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}\tilde{b}_{S}
and
\operatorname{supp}b_{S}\subseteq S^{*}
;

(B2)

for all sign sequences
\sigma:\mathfrak{S}\to\{\pm 1\}
, the sum
\sum_{S\in\mathfrak{S}}\sigma_{S}\tilde{a}_{S}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
\tilde{a}_{\sigma}
say, and

\left\|\tilde{a}_{\sigma}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\left|E\right|^{-1/2};

(B3)

a=\sum_{S\in\mathfrak{S}}\tilde{a}_{S}
.

In particular, any function
a
for which (A1) to (A3) hold is an atom.

Proof.

By unconditional convergence, the sum
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{R}(E):R^{\dagger}=S}a_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, for each
S\in\mathfrak{S}
, to
\tilde{a}_{S}
, say, and by Lemma
2.2
,
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{R}(E):R^{\dagger}=S}b_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
\tilde{b}_{S}
, say, and
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}\tilde{b}_{S}=\tilde{a}_{S}
.
Now
R\subseteq R^{\dagger}
, so
R^{*}\subseteq(R^{\dagger})^{*}
, and
\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{b}_{S})\subseteq S^{*}
.
Moreover, any sign function
\sigma:\mathfrak{S}\to\{\pm 1\}
determines a sign function
\tilde{\sigma}:\mathfrak{R}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
by the rule
\tilde{\sigma}_{R}=\sigma_{R^{\dagger}}
.
Again by unconditional convergence,
\sum_{S\in\mathfrak{S}}\sigma_{S}\tilde{a}_{S}
converges to
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\tilde{\sigma}_{R}a_{R}
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and

\left\|\sum_{S\in\mathfrak{S}}\sigma_{S}\tilde{a}_{S}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\left|E\right|^{-1/2}

for all sign sequences
\sigma:\mathfrak{S}\to\{\pm 1\}
, and
a=\sum_{S\in\mathfrak{M}(E)\mathfrak{S}}\tilde{a}_{S}
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

In particular, for each shard
R\in\mathfrak{R}(E)
, there is a unique widest shard
S
of maximal volume in
\mathfrak{M}(E)
such that
R\subseteq S
.
Indeed, if
R\subseteq S_{1}\subseteq E
and
R\subseteq S_{2}\subseteq E
, where
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are shards and
\operatorname{w}(S_{1})=\operatorname{w}(S_{2})
, then
R\subseteq S_{1}\cup S_{2}\subseteq E
, and
S_{1}\cup S_{2}
is also a shard.
We denote this widest maximal shard
S
by
R^{\uparrow}
.
As the mapping
R\mapsto R^{\uparrow}
has the property
R\subseteq R^{\uparrow}
, we may take
\mathfrak{S}
to be
\mathfrak{M}(E)
, and deduce that
a
is an atom.
∎

Our third remark is that the set
E
involved in the definition of an atom need not be open.

Corollary 2.5
.

Fix
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and a real number
\kappa>1+1/(2\nu)
.
Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a countable set of shards, let
E=\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{S}}R
, and suppose that
E
has finite measure.
Suppose that there exist functions
a_{R}
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
b_{R}
in
\operatorname{Dom}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N})
for all
R\in\mathfrak{S}
such that

(A1)

a_{R}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b_{R}
and
\operatorname{supp}b_{R}\subseteq R^{*}
;

(A2)

for all sign sequences
\sigma:\mathfrak{M}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
, the sum
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\sigma_{R}a_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
a_{\sigma}
say, and

\left\|a_{\sigma}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\left|E\right|^{-1/2};

(A3)

a=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}a_{R}
.

Then
(1+\varepsilon)^{-1}a
is an atom for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Proof.

Since
|E|
is finite, for all positive
\varepsilon
, there is an open subset
E_{\varepsilon}
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
such that
|E_{\varepsilon}|<(1+\varepsilon)|E|
.
The shards
R\in\mathfrak{S}
all lie in
\mathfrak{R}(E_{\varepsilon})
, and the previous lemma implies that
(1+\varepsilon)^{-1}a
is an atom.
∎

Hence we may replace the requirement that
E
be an open set by the assumption that it is a countable union of shards.

Fourth, our atomic Hardy space does not depend on the enlargement parameter
\kappa
(though the norm may well do so).

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose that
\kappa>\kappa_{1}>1+1/(2\nu)
and
\kappa_{1}-1\geq\kappa/(2\nu+1)
.
Then every
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atom is the product of a geometric constant and a
(1,2,M,N,\kappa_{1})
atom.
Consequently, the
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atomic Hardy space and the
(1,2,M,N,\kappa_{1})
atomic Hardy space coincide, with equivalence of norms.

Proof.

In this proof, we include the enlargement parameter
\kappa
in the notation, and write
R^{*,\kappa}
instead of
R^{*}
for
gB^{(1)}(o,\kappa q/2)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/8)
, where
g=\operatorname{c}(R)
,
q=\operatorname{w}(R)
, and
h=\operatorname{h}(R)
.

Suppose that
a
is a
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atom associated to the open set
E
.
Then we may find functions
a_{R}
and
b_{R}
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
for all
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
such that

(A1)

a_{R}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b_{R}
and
\operatorname{supp}b_{R}\subseteq R^{*,\kappa}
;

(A2)

for all sign sequences
\sigma:\mathfrak{M}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
, the sum
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\sigma_{R}a_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
a_{\sigma}
say, and
\left\|a_{\sigma}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\left|E\right|^{-1/2}
;

(A3)

a=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}a_{R}
.

For
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
, write
R^{\dagger}
for the unique shard that contains
R
and is a translate of
D_{2\nu+1}(R)
, and let
E^{\dagger}
be the set
\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}R^{\dagger}
.
Then, as shown in Corollary
1.16
,
\left|E^{\dagger}\right|\lesssim|E|
.
Further, for such
R
and
R^{\dagger}
, write
g=\operatorname{c}(R)
,
g^{\dagger}=\operatorname{c}(R^{\dagger})
,
q=\operatorname{w}(R)
and
h=\operatorname{h}(R)
; then
g
is in the interior of
R^{\dagger}
, and (
1.15
) implies that

\displaystyle R^{*,\kappa}

\displaystyle=gB^{(1)}(o,\kappa q/2)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/8)

\displaystyle\subseteq g^{\dagger}B^{(1)}(o,(2\nu+1)q/2)B^{(2)}(0,(2\nu+1)^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/8)

\displaystyle\qquad B^{(1)}(o,\kappa q/2)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/8)

\displaystyle\subseteq g^{\dagger}B^{(1)}(o,(2\nu+1+\kappa)q/2)B^{(2)}(0,((2\nu+1)^{2}+\kappa^{2})(q^{2}+4h)/8)

\displaystyle\subseteq g^{\dagger}B^{(1)}(o,\kappa_{1}(2\nu+1)q/2)B^{(2)}(0,\kappa_{1}^{2}(2\nu+1)^{2}(q^{2}+4h)/8)

\displaystyle=(R^{\dagger})^{*,\kappa_{1}},

since
\kappa_{1}-1\geq\kappa/(2\nu+1)
.

We take
\mathfrak{S}
to be the collection
\{R^{\dagger}:R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)\}
, and for
S\in\mathfrak{S}
, we write

\tilde{a}_{S}=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E):R^{\dagger}=S}a_{R}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{b}_{S}=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E):R^{\dagger}=S}b_{R},

as in the previous lemma.
Clearly,
\operatorname{supp}b_{R}\subseteq(R^{\dagger})^{*,\kappa_{1}}
, whence
\operatorname{supp}\tilde{b}_{S}\subseteq S^{*,\kappa_{1}}
.
By Lemma
2.4
,
(|E|/|\tilde{E}|)^{1/2}a
is a
(1,2,M,N,\kappa_{1})
atom.

It follows that the
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atomic Hardy space is a subspace of the
(1,2,M,N,\kappa_{1})
atomic Hardy space; since the converse is trivial, these spaces coincide.
∎

Corollary 2.7
.

As a topological vector space, the
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atomic Hardy space is independent of the parameter
\kappa
in
(1+1/(2\nu),\infty)
.

In light of this corollary, when we are only interested in boundedness or convergence in
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, we may abbreviate
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atom to
(1,2,M,N)
atom.
However, when we make precise statements about
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
norms, we need to specify
\kappa
(and
M
and
N
).

Fifth, there is no loss of generality in supposing that atoms are
finite
, by which we mean that they are sums of finitely many particles.

Lemma 2.8
.

For every atom
a
and every
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, there exist finite atoms
a_{j}
and
\lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that

a=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}a_{j}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\leq 1+\varepsilon.

The sum
\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
converges absolutely in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Proof.

Take an atom
a
, and write
a=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}a_{R}
, as in Definition
2.1
.
The unconditional convergence of the sum implies that we can find recursively finite subsets
\mathfrak{F}_{j}
of
\mathfrak{M}(E)
such that
\mathfrak{F}_{j}\subseteq\mathfrak{F}_{j+1}
for all
j
,
\mathfrak{M}(E)=\bigcup_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{F}_{j}
and, for all sign sequences
\sigma:\mathfrak{R}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
, the sum
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)\setminus\mathfrak{F}_{j}}\sigma_{R}a_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
a_{\sigma,j}
say, and

\left\|a_{\sigma,j}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq 2^{-2-j}\varepsilon\left|E\right|^{-1/2}.

Let
\lambda_{0}=1
and
\lambda_{j}=2^{-j}\varepsilon
when
j\geq 1
.
It now follows that
\lambda_{j}^{-1}\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{F}_{j+1}\setminus\mathfrak{F}_{j}}a_{R}
is a finite atom associated to
E
, and clearly
\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}=1+\varepsilon
and
a=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
.
∎

Corollary 2.9
.

Fix
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and a real number
\kappa>1+1/(2\nu)
.
Suppose that
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Then for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we may write
f\sim\lambda_{j}a_{j}
, where
a_{j}
is a finite atom, and
\lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}<(1+\varepsilon)\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
2.8
and the definition of
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
∎

Each finite atom is associated to a bounded set
E
.

Finally, we used the family of shards
\mathfrak{R}
in defining atoms.
Since a dilate or a left translate of a shard need not be a shard, it is possible that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
might not be dilation or (left) translation invariant.
Since the other Hardy spaces defined in the Introduction are evidently dilation and left translation invariant, this would mean that these spaces could not coincide with the atomic space.
Fortunately, our next result implies that this is not the case.

We write
\mathfrak{T}(E)
for the collection of all tubes contained in an open set
E
.

Definition 2.10
.

Fix
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.
A
(1,2,M,N)
tube atom
associated to an open set
E
of finite measure
|E|
is a function
a\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that there exist functions
a_{T}
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, called
tube particles
, and
b_{T}
in
\operatorname{Dom}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N})
for all
T\in\mathfrak{T}(E)
such that

(A1)

a_{T}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b_{T}
and
\operatorname{supp}b_{T}\subseteq T
;

(A2)

for all sign sequences
\sigma:\mathfrak{T}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
, the sum
\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{T}(E)}\sigma_{T}a_{T}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
a_{\sigma}
say, and

\left\|a_{\sigma}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\big|E\big|^{-1/2};

(A3)

a=\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{T}(E)}a_{T}
.

We say that
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
has a decomposition into tube atoms if we may write
f
as a sum
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
, converging in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, where
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\lambda_{j}|<\infty
and each
a_{j}
is a tube atom; we also write
f\sim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
to indicate that
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
is a decomposition of
f
into tube atoms.
The tube atom Hardy space is defined to be the linear space of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
that have decompositions into tube atoms, with norm

\left\|f\right\|:=\inf\biggl\{\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\lambda_{j}|:f\sim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}\biggr\}.

We note that the set
\mathfrak{T}(E)
is uncountable.
Only countably many
a_{T}
can be nonzero in the definition of a tube atom.
We also note that the proof of Lemma
2.8
also works for tube atoms, and hence in the definition of the Hardy space based on tube atoms, we may suppose that all atoms are finite, that is, are finite sums of particles.

Corollary 2.11
.

Fix
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.
Every
(1,2,M,N)
tube atom associated to a set
E
is a
(1,2,M,N,(2\nu+1)^{2}))
atom associated to a set
E_{1}
, where
|E_{1}|\leq|E|
.
Conversely, every
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atom associated to a set
E_{1}
is a geometric multiple of a
(1,2,M,N)
tube atom associated to a set
E
, where
|E_{1}|\eqsim|E|
.
Hence the tube atom Hardy space coincides with the Hardy space based on shards, with equivalence of norms.

Proof.

Let
a
be a tube atom associated to a set
E
.
By Lemma
1.8
, for each
T\in\mathfrak{T}(E)
, there is a shard
R_{T}
such that
R_{T}\subseteq T\subseteq R_{T}^{*}
, where the enlargement parameter
\kappa
is taken to be
(2\nu+1)^{2}
.
We take
E_{1}
to be
\bigcup_{T\in\mathfrak{T}(E)}R_{T}
; then

|E_{1}|\leq|E|\leq\left|\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}R_{k}^{*}\right|\lesssim|E_{1}|.

For all sign functions
\sigma:\mathfrak{T}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
,

\left\|\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{T}(E)}\sigma_{T}a_{T}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\big|E\big|^{-1/2}\leq\big|E_{1}\big|^{-1/2}.

As in the proof of Lemma
2.4
, we group together all
a_{T}
contained in the same maximal subshard
R
of
E_{1}
.
Let
R_{T}^{\dagger}
be the widest maximal subshard of
E_{1}
such that
T\subseteq R_{T}^{\dagger}
.
For
\tilde{\sigma}:\mathfrak{M}(E_{1})\to\{\pm 1\}
, we define
\sigma:\mathfrak{T}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
by
\sigma(T)=\tilde{\sigma}(R_{T}^{\dagger})
, and for
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E_{1})
, we set

a_{R}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in\mathfrak{T}(E)\\
R_{T}^{\dagger}=R\end{subarray}}a_{T}.

It is now immediate that

\biggl\|\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E_{1})}\tilde{\sigma}_{R}\tilde{a}_{R}\biggr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\big|E_{1}\big|^{-1/2},

and
a
is a
(1,2,M,N,(2\nu+1)^{2})
atom associated to
E_{1}
.

The converse statement follows from the definition: if
a
is a
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atom associated to
E_{1}
, then
a=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E_{1})}a_{R}
, and each
a_{R}
is supported in the tube
R^{*}
.
Define
E=\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E_{1})}R^{*}
.
Then

\left|\bigcup_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E_{1})}R^{*}\right|\leq|E_{1}|^{-1/2}\lesssim|E|^{-1/2},

so
a
is a geometric multiple of a
(1,2,M,N)
tube atom.
∎

For a function
f
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, we define the
left translate

{}_{g}f
of
f
by
g
by

{}_{g}f(g^{\prime}):=f(g^{-1}g^{\prime})\qquad\forall g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

Corollary 2.12
.

Suppose that
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
(defined using finite tube atoms).
Then the following hold:

(1)

the translate
{}_{g}f
is in
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and

\left\|{}_{g}f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}=\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu},

(2)

as
g\to o
in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
,
{}_{g}f\to f
in
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
;

(3)

the normalised dilate
f_{r}
is in
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and

\left\|f_{r}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}=\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\qquad\forall r\in\mathbb{R}^{+};

(4)

as
r\to 1
in
\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
f_{r}\to f
in
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider what happens with atoms.

Suppose that
a
is a finite tube atom associated to a bounded open set
E
.
Then
a=\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}a_{T}
, where
\mathfrak{F}
is a finite subset of
\mathfrak{T}(E)
; further,
\operatorname{supp}(a_{T})\subseteq T
and
E=\bigcup_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}T
.
Then
{}_{g}a=\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}{}_{g}a_{T}
; clearly
\operatorname{supp}({}_{g}a_{T})\subseteq gT
and
gE=\bigcup_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}gT
.
If
a_{T}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b_{T}
for some
b_{T}\in\operatorname{Dom}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N})
, then
{}_{g}a_{T}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}{}_{g}b_{T}
.
For any sign function
\sigma:\mathfrak{F}\to\{\pm 1\}
,

\biggl\|\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}\sigma_{T}{}_{g}a_{T}\biggr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}=\biggl\|\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}\sigma_{T}a_{T}\biggr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\left|E\right|^{-1/2}=\left|gE\right|^{-1/2}.

Hence
{}_{g}a
is a tube atom associated to
gE
, and (1) holds.

For all
g
in
B^{(1)}(o,t)
, the function
{}_{g}a_{T}-a_{T}
is supported in
B^{(1)}(o,t)T
, and

\biggl|\bigcup_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}B^{(1)}(o,t)T\biggr|\leq\biggl|\bigcup_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}T\biggr|+\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}\biggl|B^{(1)}(o,t)T\setminus T\biggr|\leq 4\biggl|\bigcup_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}T\biggr|

when
t
is small enough, by the continuity of the group operations.

Indeed, suppose that
T=gB^{(1)}(o,r)B^{(2)}(0,h)
.
For all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, there exists
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
(which depends on
t
and on
g
) such that
B^{(1)}(o,t^{\prime})g\subseteq gB^{(1)}(o,t)
, and so

B^{(1)}(o,t^{\prime})T\subseteq gB^{(1)}(o,t)B^{(1)}(o,r)B^{(2)}(0,h)=gB^{(1)}(o,r+t)B^{(2)}(0,h),

and

\lim_{t\to 0}\left|gB^{(1)}(o,r+t)B^{(2)}(0,h)\setminus gB^{(1)}(o,r)B^{(2)}(0,h)\right|=0.

Further,
\left\|{}_{g}a_{R}-a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\to 0
as
g\to o
, so for any sign function
\sigma:\mathfrak{F}\to\{\pm 1\}
,

\lim_{g\to o}\biggl\|\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}\sigma_{T}({}_{g}a_{T}-a_{T})\biggr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\lim_{g\to o}\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}\left\|{}_{g}a_{T}-a_{T}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}=0,

and (2) holds.

We leave to the reader the task of treating dilations in a similar manner.
∎

Corollary 2.13
.

Suppose that
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Suppose also that
\varphi:\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\to\mathbb{C}
is Poisson bounded and
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\varphi(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=1
, and let
\varphi_{r}
be the normalised dilate of
\varphi
.
Then
\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\bigl\|\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}f\bigr\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
is uniformly bounded for all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}f\to f
in
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
as
r\to 0
.

Proof.

This follows from the previous lemma.
∎

To prove Journé’s lemma (in Section
2.3
), we need a slightly stronger result.

Corollary 2.14
.

Suppose that
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and that
\varphi
is smooth, normalised, that is,
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\varphi(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=1
, and
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)\subset B^{(1)}(o,1)
.
Then there exists a constant
C
such that, for all finite atoms
a
,
we may write
a-\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a
as a finite sum
\sum_{k}\lambda_{k,r}a_{k,r}
,
where
\lambda_{k,r}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\sum_{k}\lambda_{k,r}\leq C
for all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\limsup_{r\to 1}\sum_{k}\lambda_{k,r}=0
, while each
a_{k,r}
is a finite atom.

Proof.

Suppose that
a=\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}a_{T}
, where each
a_{T}
is a particle,
\operatorname{supp}(T)\subseteq T
, and for all sign functions
\sigma:\mathfrak{F}\to\{\pm 1\}
,

\biggl\|\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}\sigma_{T}a_{T}\biggr\|\leq\left|E\right|^{-1/2},

where
E=\bigcup_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}T
.

As argued in the proof of Corollary
2.12
, we may choose
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\left|B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon)E\right|\leq 4\left|E\right|
.
When
g\in B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon)
,
{}_{g}a
is supported in the set
B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon)E
.
Hence if
r\leq\varepsilon
, then
a_{T}-\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a_{T}
is supported in
B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon)T
and
a-\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a
is supported in
B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon)E
.
Further,
\left\|a_{T}-\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a_{T}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}}\to 0
as
r\to 0+
, and hence
for any sign function
\sigma:\mathfrak{F}\to\{\pm 1\}
,

\biggl\|\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}\sigma_{T}(a_{T}-\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a_{T})\biggr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}=\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}\left\|a_{T}-\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a_{T}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\to 0

as
r\to 0+
.
Then
a-\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a
is a multiple of a single atom associated to
B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon)E
, and the multiple tends to
0
as
r
does.

When
r
is large, it suffices to write
\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a
as a finite weighted sum of finite atoms, with control of the sum of the weights.
Take finitely many
g_{j}\in\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,r)
such that the open sets
g_{j}B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon)
cover the compact set
\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,r)
, and then take closed subsets
F_{j}
of
g_{j}B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon)
that are pairwise disjoint (up to null sets) and cover
\bar{B}^{(1)}(o,r)
.
Define
\lambda_{j}=2\int_{F_{j}}\left|\varphi_{r}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g
.
Then

\displaystyle\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a(g^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}(\mathbbm{1}_{F_{j}}\varphi_{r})(g)a(g^{-1}g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}(\mathbbm{1}_{F_{j}}\varphi_{r})(g_{j}g)a(g^{-1}g_{j}^{-1}g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}{}_{g_{j}}(\mathbbm{1}_{F_{j}}\varphi_{r})(g)a(g^{-1}g_{j}^{-1}g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g.

By construction,
{}_{g_{j}}(\mathbbm{1}_{F_{j}}\varphi_{r})
is supported in
g_{j}^{-1}F_{j}\subseteq B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon)
, and the convolution

g^{\prime}\mapsto\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}{}_{g_{j}}(\mathbbm{1}_{F_{j}}\varphi_{r})(g)a(g^{-1}g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g

is a finite atom supported in
B^{(1)}(o,\varepsilon)E
.
Indeed,

{}_{g_{j}}(\mathbbm{1}_{F_{j}}\varphi_{r})\ast_{(1)}a=\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}{}_{g_{j}}(\mathbbm{1}_{F_{j}}\varphi_{r})\ast_{(1)}a_{T}

and, for any sign function
\sigma:\mathfrak{F}\to\{\pm 1\}
,

\displaystyle\biggl\|\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}}\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}\sigma_{T}{}_{g_{j}}(\mathbbm{1}_{F_{j}}\varphi_{r})\ast_{(1)}a_{T}\biggr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\|(\mathbbm{1}_{F_{j}}\varphi_{r})\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}}{\lambda_{j}}\biggl\|\sum_{T\in\mathfrak{F}}\sigma_{T}a_{T}\biggr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|E\right|^{-1/2}\leq\left|B^{(1)}(0,\varepsilon)E\right|^{-1/2}.

Finally, the translate

g^{\prime}\mapsto\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}{}_{g_{j}}(\mathbbm{1}_{F_{j}}\varphi_{r})(g)a(g^{-1}g_{j}^{-1}g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g

is also a finite atom.
∎

2.3.
The moment atom Hardy space

There is a closely related atomic space that we define at this point.

Definition 2.15
.

Fix positive integers
M
and
N
and a real number
\kappa
in
(1+1/(2\nu),\infty)
.
A
moment atom
is a function
a\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that there exists an open subset
E
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure
|E|
and functions
a_{R}
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, called
moment particles
, for all
R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)
such that

(A1)

the moments
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}p(z,t)\,a_{R}(z,t)\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}t
vanish for all monomials
p
of degree
d_{1}
in
z
and degree
d_{2}
in
t
, where
d_{2}<2N
or
d_{1}+2d_{2}<2M+4N
, and
\operatorname{supp}a_{R}\subseteq R^{*}=T(g,\kappa q,\kappa^{2}h)
, where
g=\operatorname{c}(R)
,
q=\operatorname{w}(R)
and
h=\operatorname{h}(R)
;

(A2)

for all sign sequences
\sigma:\mathfrak{M}(E)\to\{\pm 1\}
, the sum
\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\sigma_{R}a_{R}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to
a_{\sigma}
say, and

\left\|a_{\sigma}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq\left|E\right|^{-1/2};

(A3)

a=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}a_{R}
.

We say that
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
has a moment atomic decomposition if we may write
f
as a sum
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
, converging in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, where
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\lambda_{j}|<\infty
and each
a_{j}
is a moment atom; we write
f\sim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
to indicate that
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
is an atomic decomposition of
f
.
The space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{momat}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is defined to be the linear space of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
that have moment atomic decompositions, with norm

(2.6)

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{momat}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\inf\biggl\{\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|\lambda_{j}|:f\sim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{j}a_{j}\biggr\}.

We note that, from Proposition
1.23
, the atomic Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is a subspace of the moment atomic Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{momat}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and there is a corresponding norm inequality.
Later we show that these Hardy spaces coincide.

Next, the results of the previous section about the atomic Hardy space extend to the moment atomic Hardy space, with almost identical proofs.

2.4.
Boundedness on particles and atoms

We recall (
1.15
) and (
1.23
).
First, if
R
is a shard,
\operatorname{c}(R)=g
,
\operatorname{w}(R)=q
and
\operatorname{h}(R)=h
, then
R^{*}
is defined to be
T(g,\kappa q/2,\kappa^{2}(4h+q^{2})/8)
.
Second, if
R
and
S
are shards and
R\subseteq S
, and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, then

\displaystyle\rho_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(R,S):=\left(\frac{\operatorname{w}(R)}{\operatorname{w}(S)}\right)^{\varepsilon_{1}}+\left(\frac{\operatorname{h}(R)}{\operatorname{h}(S)}\right)^{\varepsilon_{2}}.

Proposition 2.16
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a linear operator, or a nonnegative sublinear operator, that satisfies a strong type
(2,2)
bound
\left\|\mathcal{A}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq C_{1}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
or a weak-type
(2,2)
bound

\displaystyle\left|{\left\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:{\left|\mathcal{A}f(g)\right|}>\lambda\right\}}\right|\leq\frac{C_{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

for all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Let
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.

(a) Suppose also that there exist
C
,
\varepsilon_{1}
, and
\varepsilon_{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
I,J\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that

(2.7)

\int_{(S^{*})^{c}}\left|\mathcal{A}a_{R}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g\leq C\rho_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(R,S)\left|R\right|^{1/2}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

for all
R,S\in\mathfrak{R}
such that
R\subseteq S\in\mathfrak{R}
and
\operatorname{w}(S)/\operatorname{w}(R)\geq(2\nu+1)^{I}
and
\operatorname{h}(S)/\operatorname{h}(R)\geq(2\nu+1)^{2J}
, and for all particles
a_{R}\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that
a_{R}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b_{R}
where
b_{R}\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\operatorname{supp}b_{R}\subseteq R^{*}
.
Then there is a constant
C_{0}
such that

(2.8)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{A}a\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq C_{0}

for all
(1,2,M,N)
atoms
a
.

(b) Suppose also that (
2.8
) holds.
Then
\mathcal{A}
maps
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\cap\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
into
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and

(2.9)

\left\|\mathcal{A}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq C_{0}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\qquad\forall f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\cap\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}).

Hence
\mathcal{A}
extends uniquely by continuity to a bounded operator from
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
that satisfies the same inequality for all
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Proof.

Take a
(1,2,M,N,\kappa)
atom
a
associated to an open set
E
of finite measure, and, as in Definition
2.1
, write
a\sim\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}a_{R}
.
As in Definition
1.17
, we define sets
E^{(1)}
and
E^{(2)}
as follows:

\displaystyle E^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left\{g\in G:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}(\mathbbm{1}_{E})(g)>{\alpha_{1}}\right\}

\displaystyle E^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\left\{g\in G:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sh}}(\mathbbm{1}_{E^{(1)}})(g)>{\alpha_{2}}\right\}.

Given a shard
R
in
\mathfrak{M}(E)
, we define
R^{(1)}
to be the widest shard
S
such that
R\subseteq S
and
S\in\mathfrak{M}(E^{(1)})
, and
R^{(2)}
to be the highest shard
S
such that
R^{(1)}\subseteq S
and
S\in\mathfrak{M}(E^{(2)})
.
Then
|E^{(2)}|\lesssim_{I,J}|E|
by two applications of Lemma
1.13
.

Now

\left\|\mathcal{A}(a)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}=\int_{{E^{(2)}}}|\mathcal{A}(a)(g)|\,\mathrm{d}g+\int_{({E^{(2)}})^{c}}|\mathcal{A}(a)(g)|\,\mathrm{d}g.

On the one hand, by the (weak)
\mathsf{L}^{2}
boundedness of
\mathcal{A}
, and Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\int_{{E^{(2)}}}|\mathcal{A}(a)(g)|\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\lesssim|{E^{(2)}}|^{1/2}\left\|\mathcal{A}(a)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim_{I,J}|E|^{1/2}\left\|a\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq 1.

On the other hand,
\mathcal{A}a=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\mathcal{A}a_{R}
, where the sum converges unconditionally in the Lorentz space
\mathsf{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
by the boundedness hypothesis, so

\displaystyle\int_{({E^{(2)}})^{c}}|\mathcal{A}(a)(g)|\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\int_{({E^{(2)}})^{c}}|\mathcal{A}(a_{R})(g)|\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\int_{(R^{(2)})^{c}}\left|\mathcal{A}(a_{R})(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\rho_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(R,R^{(2)})\left|R\right|^{1/2}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, Journé’s lemma (Lemma
1.18
), and (
2.3
),

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{A}(a)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\rho_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(R,R^{(2)})^{2}|R|\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\left\|a_{R}\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim|E|^{1/2}|E|^{-1/2}=1,

as required.

To prove (b), it will suffice to prove (
2.9
).
Take
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\cap\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and a smooth radial function
\varphi
with support in
B^{(1)}(o,1)
such that
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\varphi(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=1
; let
\varphi_{r}
be the normalised dilate of
\varphi
, as in (
0.2
).
Now
f=\lim_{r\to 0+}\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}f
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and so
\mathcal{A}f=\lim_{r\to 0+}\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}f)
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
If we can show that

(2.10)

\limsup_{r\to 0+}\left\|\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq C_{0}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})},

then it will follow that

\int_{E}\left|\mathcal{A}(f)(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g=\lim_{r\to 0+}\int_{E}\left|\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}f)(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g\leq C_{0}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

for all subsets
E
of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
of finite measure, so
\left\|\mathcal{A}f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq C_{0}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
.

Given
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we can write
f\sim\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
, where the
a_{j}
are finite atoms,
\lambda_{j}>0
, and
\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}<\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\varepsilon
.
The sum
\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a_{j}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
since the sum
\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
converges in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, so

\left|\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}f)\right|=\Bigl|\mathcal{A}(\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a_{j})\Bigr|\leq\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\left|\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a_{j})\right|,

whence

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\lambda_{j}\left\|\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a_{j})\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

If we can show that

(2.11)

\left\|\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a_{j})\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq C_{1}\qquad\forall r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

for some constant
C_{1}
, and

\limsup_{r\to 0+}\left\|\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a_{j})\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq C_{0},

then (
2.10
), and hence the proposition, will follow.

By Corollary
2.14
, there exists a constant
C
such that, for all finite atoms
a
and all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
we may write
a-\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a
as a finite sum of finite atoms
\sum_{k}\lambda_{k,r}a_{k,r}
,
where
\lambda_{k,r}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\sum_{k}\lambda_{k,r}\leq C
for all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\lim_{r\to 0}\sum_{k}\lambda_{k,r}=0
.
Then

\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a)\leq\mathcal{A}(a)+\sum_{k}\lambda_{k,r}\mathcal{A}(a_{k,r}),

and so

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{A}(\varphi_{r}\ast_{(1)}a)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathcal{A}(a)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\sum_{k}\lambda_{k,r}\left\|\mathcal{A}(a_{k,r})\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}+\sum_{k}C_{0}\lambda_{k,r},

and the desired result (
2.11
) follows.
∎

A similar result holds for the moment atomic Hardy space of Section
2.3
.

2.5.
The inclusion
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})

In this section, we consider the Lusin–Littlewood–Paley operator
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
, and show that it is bounded from the atomic Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, whence the inclusion of the title follows.
We first recall the definition of this operator.
For the definition of Poisson bounded functions, see Definition
1.26
, and for normalised dilates, see (
0.2
) and (
0.3
).

Definition
.

Suppose that Poisson bounded functions
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
have mean
0
.
We define the Lusin–Littlewood–Paley area function
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)
of
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
associated to
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
by

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g):=\biggl(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\bigl|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\bigr|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}(g)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\biggr)^{1/2}

for all
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, and the Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, usually shortened to
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, to be the set of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
for which
\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}<\infty
, with seminorm

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

Theorem 2.17
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is a Poisson bounded pair of functions, both of which have mean
0
.
Then
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and

\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq C(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\qquad\forall f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}).

where, for some integer
M
greater than
\delta/4
,

C(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\lesssim\bigl\|\mathcal{R}\varphi^{(1)}\bigr\|_{(2M)}\bigl\|\varphi^{(2)}\bigr\|_{(2)}.

Proof.

Since
2M>\delta/2
and
2M
and
\delta/2
are integers,
2M\geq 1+\delta/2
.

We appeal to Proposition
2.16
and the principle enunciated in Remark
1.33
, which show that to prove the theorem, it suffices to consider smooth functions
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
, supported in the unit balls in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and in
\mathbb{R}
and with vanishing means, and show that

(2.13)

\displaystyle\int_{(S^{*})^{c}}\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(a_{R})(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\leq C(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\,\rho_{1,3/2}(R,S)\left|R\right|^{1/2}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

for all particles
a_{R}
associated to all
R\in\mathfrak{R}
, such that
\operatorname{c}(R)=o
,
\operatorname{w}(R)=1
and
\operatorname{h}(R)=h
, and for all
S\in\mathfrak{R}
such that
R\subseteq S
and
1<\operatorname{w}(S)=:r^{*}
and
h<\operatorname{h}(S)=:s^{*}
.

It follows from the definition just before the statement of this theorem that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(a_{R})(g)

\displaystyle\eqsim\sum_{k=1}^{4}\left(\iint_{\Omega_{k}}\left|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g)\right|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}(g)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{4}\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{k}(a_{R})(g),

say, where

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(r,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}:r<1,s>h\}\qquad

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}

\displaystyle=\{(r,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}:r<1,s<h\}

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}

\displaystyle=\{(r,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}:r>1,s<r^{2}+h\}

\displaystyle\Omega_{4}

\displaystyle=\{(r,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}:r>1,s>r^{2}+h\}.

We treat these four summands separately.

The key to our estimation of these terms is the observation that

(2.14)

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\left|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\right|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}

\displaystyle\subseteq T(o,1,h)T(o,r,s)T(o,r,s)

\displaystyle\subseteq T(o,2r+1,2s+h).

First, (
2.14
) implies that

\operatorname{supp}(\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{1}(a_{R}))\cap(S^{*})^{c}=\emptyset
and so no estimation is needed.

Second, to treat
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{2}(a_{R})
and
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{3}(a_{R})
we use a well known argument, which we now sketch.
We first choose an exponentially increasing family of sets
E_{j}
such that, from (
2.14
),

\operatorname{supp}(\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{k}(a_{R}))\subseteq\bigcup_{(r,s)\in\Omega_{k}}T(o,1+2r,h+2s)\subseteq\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}E_{j}.

We then take
j^{*}
to be the smallest
j
such that
E_{j}\cap(S^{*})^{c}\neq\emptyset
and
\Omega_{k,j}
to be a subset of
\Omega_{k}
such that
(r,s)\notin\Omega_{k.j}
implies that
T(o,1+2r,h+2s)\subseteq E_{j}
.
Then, by the definitions and basic results,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\int_{(S^{*})^{c}}|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{k}(a_{R})(g)|\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq j^{*}}\int_{E_{j+1}\setminus E_{j}}|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{k}(a_{R}))(g)|\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq j^{*}}\left|E_{j+1}\setminus E_{j}\right|^{1/2}\left(\int_{E_{j+1}\setminus E_{j}}|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{k}(a_{R}))(g)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq j^{*}}\left|E_{j+1}\right|^{1/2}\left(\iint_{\Omega_{k,j}}\left\|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}.

since
\left|E_{j+1}\setminus E_{j}\right|\leq\left|E_{j+1}\right|
and

\displaystyle\int_{E_{j+1}\setminus E_{j}}|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{k}(a_{R}))(g)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\qquad=\iint_{\Omega_{k}}\int_{E_{j+1}\setminus E_{j}}\left|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\right|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle\qquad=\iint_{\Omega_{k,j}}\int_{E_{j+1}\setminus E_{j}}\left|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\right|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\iint_{\Omega_{k,j}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\right|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle\qquad=\iint_{\Omega_{k,j}}\left\|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}.

We then compute the final integral, and find that the sum converges geometrically, so is of comparable size to the term when
j=j^{*}
.

To treat
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{2}(a_{R})
, we define
E_{j}:=T(o,3,3\times 2^{j}h)
when
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Omega_{2,j}=(0,1)\times(2^{j}h,\infty)
; then
\left|E_{j}\right|\eqsim 2^{j}h
and
j^{*}\eqsim\log_{2}(s^{*}/h)
.
Moreover,

(2.16)

\displaystyle a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}b_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}[\varphi^{(2)}_{s}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{s^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}b_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}[\mathcal{L}_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}]_{s},

so that

\displaystyle\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{s^{2}}\bigl\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}b_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\bigl\|[\mathcal{L}_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}]_{s}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\eqsim_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\frac{1}{s^{2}}\bigl\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}b_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

From these considerations, (
2.15
), Littlewood–Paley theory (as in Section
1.9
) and Lemma
2.2
,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\int_{(S^{*})^{c}}\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{2}(a_{R})(g)\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\sum_{j=j^{*}}^{\infty}|E_{j_{1}}|^{1/2}\left(\iint_{[2^{j-1}h,\infty)\times(0,1]}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}b_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s^{5}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\sum_{j=j^{*}}^{\infty}|E_{j+1}|^{1/2}\left(\int_{[2^{j}h,\infty)}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}b_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\,\frac{ds}{s^{5}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\eqsim 2^{-3j^{*}/2}h^{1/2}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\eqsim\Bigl(\frac{h}{s^{*}}\Bigr)^{3/2}|R|^{1/2}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

To deal with
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{3}(a_{R})
, we define
E_{j}:=T(o,2^{j+1}+1,3h)
and
\Omega_{3,j}:=(2^{j},\infty)\times(0,h)
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
; then
\left|E_{j}\right|\lesssim 2^{\delta j}+2^{2\nu j}h
and
j^{*}\eqsim\log_{2}(r^{*})
.
Further,

(2.18)

\displaystyle a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b_{R}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}\ast_{(1)}\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}^{M}[\varphi^{(1)}_{r}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2M}}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b_{R}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}\ast_{(1)}[\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}^{M}\varphi^{(1)}]_{r},

so that

\displaystyle\bigl\|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{r^{2M}}\bigl\|\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}^{M}\varphi^{(1)}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\bigl\|\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b_{R}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\frac{1}{r^{2M}}\bigl\|\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b_{R}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

The parameter
M
does not matter in this calculation.

From these considerations, (
2.15
), Littlewood–Paley theory (as in Section
1.9
) and Lemma
2.2
,

(2.19)

\displaystyle\int_{(S^{*})^{c}}\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{3}(a_{R})(g)\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\sum_{j=j^{*}}^{\infty}\left|E_{j+1}\right|^{1/2}\left(\int_{2^{j}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{r^{2}+h}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b_{R}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\,\frac{ds}{s}\,\frac{dr}{r^{4M+1}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\sum_{j=j^{*}}^{\infty}\left|E_{j+1}\right|^{1/2}\left(\int_{2^{j}}^{\infty}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r^{4M+1}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\eqsim_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\sum_{j=j^{*}}^{\infty}(2^{\delta j/2}+2^{\nu j}h^{1/2})2^{-2Mj}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(2)}b_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M}(2^{j^{*}(\delta-4M)/2}+2^{j^{*}(\nu-2M)}h^{1/2})\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Bigl(\frac{1}{r^{*}}\Bigr)^{2M-\delta/2}\left|R\right|^{1/2}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

We require that
4M>\delta
, whence
2M-\delta/2\geq 1
, to ensure that the sum converges.

Finally, we use a pointwise estimate to control the term
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{4}(a_{R})
, that is,

(2.20)

\biggl(\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{h}^{\infty}\bigl|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\bigr|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}\,\frac{ds}{s}\,\frac{dr}{r}\biggr)^{1/2}.

From (
2.14
), the definition of
\chi_{r,s}
, and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

(2.21)

\displaystyle\left|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\right|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|\left|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\right|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\mathbbm{1}_{T(o,2r+1,2s+h)}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|\left|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}\right|^{2}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\mathbbm{1}_{T(o,2r+1,2s+h)}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|b_{R}\ast_{(1)}\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}\varphi_{r,s}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{T(o,2r+1,2s+h)}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|b_{R}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\bigl\|\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}\varphi_{r,s}\bigr\|_{\mathsf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{T(o,2r+1,2s+h)}.

From Hölder’s inequality and Lemma
2.2
,
\left\|b_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq|R^{*}|^{1/2}\left\|b_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim h^{5/2}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}\varphi_{r,s}(z,t)

\displaystyle=\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}^{M}[\varphi^{(1)}_{r}]\ast_{(1)}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}[\varphi^{(2)}_{s}](z,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2M}}\frac{1}{s^{2}}[\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{L}}_{(1)}^{M}\varphi^{(1)}]_{r}\ast_{(1)}[\mathcal{L}_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}]_{s}(z,t)
