The key to this lemma is the following calculation, inspired by
[
16
]
.
Take
\boldsymbol{\psi}
in
\mathsf{F}_{00}
.
If we can write

\psi^{(1)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(1)}\omega^{(1,r)}\,\frac{dr}{r}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi^{(2)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}\ast_{(2)}\omega^{(2,s)}\,\frac{ds}{s},

for some
\omega^{(1,r)}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
,
\omega^{(2,s)}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{R})
and
r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, where
C(\boldsymbol{\psi})
, given by

(5.6)

\displaystyle C(\boldsymbol{\psi})

\displaystyle:=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\omega^{(1,r)}(g_{1})\bigr|\Bigl(1+\frac{\left\|g_{1}\right\|}{r}\Bigr)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl|\omega^{(2,s)}(g_{2})\bigr|\Bigl(1+\frac{\left\|g_{2}\right\|}{s}\Bigr)^{1+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right),

is finite, then it will follow that

\displaystyle\sup_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\psi_{r^{\prime}}^{(1)}\ast_{(2)}\varphi_{s^{\prime}}^{(2)}(g)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad=\sup_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\biggl|\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}}(f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{rr^{\prime}}^{(1)}\ast_{(2)}\varphi_{ss^{\prime}}^{(2)})(gg_{2}^{-1}g_{1}^{-1})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\omega_{r^{\prime}}^{(1,r)}(g_{1})\,\omega_{s^{\prime}}^{(2,s)}(g_{2})\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\biggr|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sup_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}}\left|(f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{rr^{\prime}}^{(1)}\ast_{(2)}\varphi_{ss^{\prime}}^{(2)})(gg_{2}^{-1}g_{1}^{-1})\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left|\omega_{r^{\prime}}^{(1,r)}(g_{1})\,\omega_{s^{\prime}}^{(2,s)}(g_{2})\right|\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sup_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{M}^{**}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\Bigl(1+\frac{\left\|g_{1}\right\|}{rr^{\prime}}\Bigr)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\Bigl(1+\frac{\left\|g_{2}\right\|}{ss^{\prime}}\Bigr)^{1+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left|\omega_{r^{\prime}}^{(1,r)}(g_{1})\,\omega_{s^{\prime}}^{(2,s)}(g_{2})\right|\,\mathrm{d}g_{2}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle\qquad=C(\boldsymbol{\psi})\mathcal{M}^{**}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g),

where
C(\boldsymbol{\psi})
is given by (
5.6
).
Loosely speaking, for
C(\boldsymbol{\psi})
to be finite, we need
\omega^{(1,r)}(g)
and
\omega^{(2,s)}(t)
to decay faster than
\left\|g\right\|^{-2\delta}
and
|t|^{-2}
at infinity.
We seek suitable representations of
\psi^{(1)}
and
\psi^{(2)}
.

By hypothesis,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(1)}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}_{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}=\delta,

where
\mathcal{D}
is a left-invariant differential operator of homogeneous degree
2\delta+2
.
We take
\omega^{(1,r)}
to be
\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(1)}\psi
, and then

\psi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(1)}\omega^{(1,r)}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,.

Note that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\omega^{(1,r)}(g_{1})\bigr|\Bigl(1+\frac{\left\|g_{1}\right\|}{r}\Bigr)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(1)}\psi(g_{1})\bigr|\Bigl(1+\frac{\left\|g_{1}\right\|}{r}\Bigr)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi_{1/r}(g_{1})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi_{r}(g_{1})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,.

On the one hand, when
r
is small, we use Proposition
1.23
to write
\psi=\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{E}}\breve{\psi}
, where
\mathcal{E}
is a left invariant differential operator of degree
1
.
Then
\psi_{r}=r\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{E}}(\breve{\psi}_{r})
, and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi_{r}(g_{1})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{E}}(\breve{\psi}_{r})(g_{1})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|\mathcal{E}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\breve{\psi}_{r}(g_{1})\right|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{E}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1}g_{0}^{-1})\,\breve{\psi}_{r}(g_{0})\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\right|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{E}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1}g_{0}^{-1})\bigr|\bigl|\breve{\psi}_{r}(g_{0})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{E}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1})\bigr|\bigl|\breve{\psi}_{r}(g_{0})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}g_{0}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2^{\delta+\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{E}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1})\bigr|\bigl|\breve{\psi}_{r}(g_{0})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\breve{\psi}(g_{0})\bigr|\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{E}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,,

since
1+\left\|g_{1}g_{0}\right\|\leq 1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|+\left\|g_{0}\right\|\leq 2(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)
when
\left\|g_{0}\right\|\leq 1
.
Both integrals are finite; in particular, since
\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}
is Poisson bounded, and
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{D}
is a differential operator of degree
2\delta+3
,

\bigl|\mathcal{E}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1})\bigr|\lesssim\max\{1,\left\|g_{1}\right\|\}^{-3\delta-4}\qquad\forall g_{1}\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

On the other hand, when
r
is large, we may write
\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}
, where
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}
are both left invariant differential operators of degrees
\delta+1
.
Further,
\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\psi_{r})=r^{\delta+1}(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\psi)_{r}
, so

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{D}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\psi_{r}(g_{1})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\psi_{r})(g_{1})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}\ast_{(1)}(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\psi)_{r}(g_{1})\right|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r^{\delta+2}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1}g_{0}^{-1})\,(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\psi)_{r}(g_{0})\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\right|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r^{\delta+2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1}g_{0}^{-1})\bigr|\bigl|(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\psi)_{r}(g_{0})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r^{\delta+2}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1})\bigr|\bigl|(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\psi)_{r}(g_{0})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}g_{0}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,\frac{dr}{r^{\delta+2}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\psi)_{r}(g_{0})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{0}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\,\frac{dr}{r^{\delta+2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{1}\,,

since
1+\left\|g_{1}g_{0}\right\|\leq 1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|+\left\|g_{0}\right\|\leq(1+\left\|g_{1}\right\|)(1+\left\|g_{0}\right\|)
.
Both factors are finite; indeed,

\bigl|\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\varphi}^{(1)}(g_{1})\bigr|\lesssim\max\{1,\left\|g_{1}\right\|\}^{-2\delta-2}\qquad\forall g_{1}\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}

since
\varphi^{(1)}
is Poisson bounded, and

\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|(\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\psi)_{r}(g_{0})\bigr|(1+\left\|g_{0}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\chi^{(1)}_{r}(1+\left\|g_{0}\right\|)^{\delta+\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}g_{0}\leq(1+r)^{\delta+\varepsilon}.

Similar (but easier to prove) formulae hold for the
\mathbb{R}
variable, and it follows that we can control
C(\boldsymbol{\psi})
, uniformly in
\boldsymbol{\psi}
, thereby proving the lemma for the case where
\mathsf{F}=\mathsf{F}_{00}^{(1)}\times\mathsf{F}_{00}^{(2)}
.

The other two cases are similar but easier.
∎

Corollary 5.6
.

Suppose that
\varphi^{(1)}:\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\to\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi^{(2)}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
are Poisson bounded and that
\{\varphi_{r}\}
is an approximate identity for convolution.
Suppose also that
\mathsf{F}
is a Poisson bounded family of pairs
(\psi^{(1)},\psi^{(2)})
.
Then

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g)\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{**}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}

for all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Proof.

We take a general Poisson bounded
\psi^{(1)}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, with mean
c
.
Then
\psi^{(1)}-c\varphi^{(1)}
has mean
0
, whence from Corollary
1.31
we may write
\psi^{(1)}
as a sum:

\psi^{(1)}=c\varphi^{(1)}+\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}[{\psi}^{(1,i)}]_{2^{i}}

where the
{\psi}^{(1,i)}
have mean equal to
0
.
We do the same with
\varphi^{(2)}
.
Then

\displaystyle\left|f\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\psi^{(2)}_{s}(g)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(g)\right|+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\psi^{(2,j)}_{s}(g)\right|

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1,i)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(g)\right|+\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}\psi^{(1,i)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\psi^{(2,j)}_{s}(g)\right|.

We can control the suprema of each of these four terms over
r
and
s
, using the hypothesis for the first and Lemma
5.5
for the others.
∎

5.2.
The inclusion
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{radial}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{nontan}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})

We now conclude our study of the equivalence of the maximal function Hardy spaces.

Theorem 5.7
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is a Poisson bounded pair.
Then

(5.8)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\leq C\|\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

Proof.

We use a “good-
\lambda
inequality”.
Fix
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, to be determined a little later, and define

F_{\lambda}=\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g)\leq\lambda\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\},

where
\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g)
is the grand maximal function as in Definition
1.28
.
We shall prove two inequalities: first,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\leq 2\int_{F_{\lambda}}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g,

and second that

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\leq C\biggl(\mathcal{M}_{F}\Bigl(\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)^{\theta}\Bigr)(g)\biggr)^{1/\theta}\qquad\forall g\in F_{\lambda}.

when
0<\theta<1
.
Once we have proved these two inequalities, the
\mathsf{L}^{1/\theta}
boundedness of
\mathcal{M}_{F}
implies that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{F_{\lambda}}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\leq 2C\int_{F_{\lambda}}\biggl(\mathcal{M}_{F}\Bigl(\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)^{\theta}\Bigr)(g)\biggr)^{1/\theta}\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\leq 2C\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\biggl(\mathcal{M}_{F}\Bigl(\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)^{\theta}\Bigr)(g)\biggr)^{1/\theta}\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\leq 2C\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g,

as required.

To prove (
5.9
), we observe that the definition of
F_{\lambda}^{c}
implies that

\displaystyle\int_{F_{\lambda}^{c}}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\leq\int_{F_{\lambda}^{c}}\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g.

Further, from (
5.7
),

(5.11)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\leq C_{1}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g.

We take
\lambda
to be
2C_{3}
and combine these inequalities; then

\displaystyle\int_{F_{\lambda}^{c}}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g,

as desired.

To prove (
5.10
), take
g\in F_{\lambda}
.
By definition of
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)
, there exists
(g_{1},r,s)\in\Gamma_{\beta}(g)
such that

|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g_{1})|>\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g).

We will write
g
and
g_{1}
as
(z,t)
and
(z_{1},t_{1})
.

Take small
\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Then for all
g^{\prime}=(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in B_{c}^{(1)}(g_{1},\alpha_{1}r)
,

\displaystyle|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g_{1})-f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\alpha_{1}r\sup_{g^{\prime\prime}\in B^{(1)}_{c}(g_{1},\alpha_{1}r)}|\nabla_{(1)}(f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s})(g^{\prime\prime})|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{1}\alpha_{1}\sup_{g^{\prime\prime}\in B^{(1)}_{c}(g_{1},\alpha_{1}r)}|f\ast_{(1)}r\nabla_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(g^{\prime\prime})|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{1}\alpha_{1}\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g),

assuming that the family
\mathsf{F}^{(1)}
is big enough to include translates of components of the horizontal gradient of
\varphi^{(1)}
by elements of
B^{(1)}_{c}(g_{1},\alpha_{1})
.
Similarly, take small
\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Then for all
g^{\prime\prime}=(0,t^{\prime\prime})\in B^{(2)}(t^{\prime},\alpha_{2}s)
,

\displaystyle|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime})-f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime}g^{\prime\prime})|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\alpha_{2}s\sup_{t^{\prime\prime\prime}\in B^{(2)}(t^{\prime},\alpha_{2}s)}|\nabla_{(2)}(f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s})(z^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{2}\alpha_{2}\sup_{t^{\prime\prime\prime}\in B^{(2)}(t^{\prime},\alpha_{2}s)}|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}s\nabla_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{2}\alpha_{2}\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g),

assuming that the family
\mathsf{F}^{(2)}
is big enough to include certain translates of
\mathcal{T}\varphi^{(2)}
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g_{1})|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g_{1})-f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime})|+2|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime})-f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\left|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2C_{1}\alpha_{1}\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g)+2C_{2}\alpha_{2}\mathcal{M}_{F,\mathrm{gmax},\mathsf{F}}(f)(g)+2|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2C_{1}\alpha_{1}\lambda\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)+2C_{2}\alpha_{2}\lambda\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)+2|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime})|.

By choosing
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
so small that
2C_{1}\alpha_{1}\lambda<{1/4}
and
2C_{2}\alpha_{2}\lambda<{1/4}
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)+\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)+|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime})|,

which implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)

\displaystyle<2|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime})|

for all
g^{\prime\prime}=(0,t^{\prime\prime})\in B^{(2)}(t^{\prime},\alpha_{2}s)
and
g^{\prime}=(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in B^{(1)}(g_{1},\alpha_{1}r)
.

Consequently, if
\theta<1
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)^{\theta}

\displaystyle<2^{\theta}\frac{1}{|B^{(1)}(g_{1},\alpha_{1}r)||B^{(2)}(t^{\prime},\alpha_{2}s)|}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int_{B^{(1)}(g_{1},\alpha_{1}r)\cdot B^{(2)}(t^{\prime},\alpha_{2}s)}|f\ast_{(1)}\varphi_{r,s}(g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime})|^{\theta}dg^{\prime\prime}dg^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathcal{M}_{F}\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)^{\theta}\bigr)(g),

and so

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)(g)

\displaystyle\leq C\biggl(\mathcal{M}_{F}\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{+}_{F,\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)^{\theta}\bigr)(g)\biggr)^{1/\theta}.

This completes the proof of (
5.11
) and hence of Theorem
5.7
.
∎

5.3.
The inclusion
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{nontan}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})

We use the nontangential maximal function associated to the Poisson kernel, as we will use properties of harmonic functions.
In view of the results of the previous sections, this is not restrictive.

Recall that
p^{(1)}_{r}
and
p^{(2)}_{s}
are the Poisson kernels on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and on
\mathbb{R}
(here
r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
), and, for
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, the
Poisson integral

u
of
f
is given by

u(g,r,s):=f\ast_{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}(g)\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

The
nontangential maximal function
of
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is defined by

\displaystyle u^{*}(g):=\sup_{(g^{\prime},r,s)\in\Gamma_{\beta}(g)}|u(g^{\prime},r,s)|,

and the
radial maximal function
of
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is defined by

\displaystyle u^{+}(g)=\sup_{r,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}|u(g^{\prime},r,s)|.

It is easy to see that, if
f\in\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, then
v
, given by
v(r,g):=f\ast_{(1)}p_{r}(g)
for all
(r,g)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, is harmonic, in the sense that
\mathcal{L}^{(1)}v=0
, where
\mathcal{L}^{(1)}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}-\partial_{r}^{2}
(recall that the operator
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
was normalised to be positive).
Moreover, if
v
is a function on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\mathcal{L}^{(1)}v=0
, then

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(1)}|v|^{2}(r,g)=2\left|\nabla^{(1)}v(r,g)\right|^{2}

in
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, where

(5.13)

\displaystyle\nabla^{(1)}=(\nabla_{(1)},\partial_{r})=(\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{\nu},\mathcal{Y}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Y}_{\nu},\partial_{r}).

We use coordinates
(t,s)
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and write
\nabla^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}
for the full gradient
(\partial_{t},\partial_{s})
and full Laplacian
-\partial_{t}^{2}-\partial_{s}^{2}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}+
.
As usual, we write
p_{r,s}
for
p^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}
and
\chi_{r,s}=\chi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\chi^{(2)}_{s}
where
\chi^{(1)}_{r}
and
\chi^{(2)}_{s}
are the normalised characteristic functions of the unit balls in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\mathbb{R}
.
We also denote by
\not{\nabla}p_{r,s}
the tensor-valued function
(\nabla^{(1)}p^{(1)})_{r}\ast_{(2)}(\nabla^{(2)}p^{(2)})_{s}
.

Definition 5.8
.

For
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, the
Lusin–Littlewood–Paley area integral

\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)
of
f
associated to the Poisson kernel is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)(g):=\biggl(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}(\not{\nabla}p)_{r,s}\right|^{2}\ast_{(1)}\chi_{r,s}(g)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\biggr)^{1/2}.

Theorem 5.9
.

Suppose that
u^{*}\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Then
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and

(5.14)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim\|u^{*}\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

Proof.

Take
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that
\left\|u^{*}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}<\infty
, and define

\displaystyle E(\alpha):=\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:u^{*}(g)\leq\alpha\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad A(\alpha):=\left\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:\mathcal{M}_{F}(\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)^{c}})(g)<\frac{1}{200}\right\}.

By definition and the
\mathsf{L}^{2}
boundedness of the flag maximal function
\mathcal{M}_{F}
,

E(\alpha)^{c}\subseteq A(\alpha)^{c}\qquad\text{and}\qquad|A(\alpha)^{c}|\lesssim|E(\alpha)^{c}|.

Recall that
\Gamma_{\beta}(g):=\{(g^{\prime},r,s)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}:g^{\prime}\in gT(o,\beta r,\beta^{2}s)\}
and
\Gamma(g)=\Gamma_{1}(g)
.
Define

\displaystyle\Omega:=\bigcup_{g\in A(\alpha)}\Gamma(g)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{\Omega}:=\bigcup\limits_{g\in E(\alpha)}\Gamma_{\beta}(g),

for some large
\beta
, which we shall determine after (
5.19
) below.

In order to prove (
5.14
), it suffices to prove that

(5.15)

\displaystyle\left|\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)(g)>\alpha\}\right|\lesssim\left|E(\alpha)^{c}\right|+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\int_{E(\alpha)}\left|u^{*}(g)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g.

For then, as in
[
16
, p. 163]
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)(g)>\alpha\}\right|\,\mathrm{d}\alpha

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|E(\alpha)^{c}\right|\,\mathrm{d}\alpha+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\int_{E(\alpha)}\left|u^{*}(g)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g\,\mathrm{d}\alpha

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u^{*}\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

The left-hand side of (
5.15
) is equal to

\displaystyle\left|\{g\in A(\alpha)^{c}:\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)(g)>\alpha\}\right|+\left|\{g\in A(\alpha):\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)(g)>\alpha\}\right|.

It follows straight from the definitions that

\left|\{g\in A(\alpha)^{c}:\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)(g)>\alpha\}\right|\leq|A(\alpha)^{c}|\lesssim|E(\alpha)^{c}|=|\{g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}:u^{*}(g)>\alpha\}|,

and hence it suffices to estimate the other summand.
By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\displaystyle\left|\{g\in A(\alpha):\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)(g)>\alpha\}\right|\leq\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\int_{A(\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)(g)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g.

Thus it will suffice to show that

(5.16)

\int_{A(\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)(g)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g\lesssim\alpha^{2}\left|E(\alpha)^{c}\right|+\int_{E(\alpha)}\left|u^{*}(g)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g.

Since
\chi_{r,s}
is even,

(5.17)

\displaystyle\int_{A(\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}(f)(g^{\prime})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{A(\alpha)}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}(\not{\nabla}p)_{r,s}(g^{\prime}g)\right|^{2}\chi_{r,s}(g)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\,\mathrm{d}g\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}{J}(g^{\prime}g,r,s)\left|f\ast_{(1)}(\not{\nabla}p)_{r,s}(g^{\prime}g)\right|^{2}\chi_{r,s}(g)

\displaystyle\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}{J}(g^{\prime},r,s)\left|f\ast_{(1)}(\not{\nabla}p)_{r,s}(g^{\prime})\right|^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime},

where
{J}:\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\to[0,\infty)
is chosen such that
{J}(g^{\prime}g,r,s)=1
if
g^{\prime}\in A(\alpha)
and
g\in\operatorname{supp}(\chi_{r,s})
.

Define

V(g,r,s):=\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(1)}p_{r,s}(g)\qquad\forall(g,r,s)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Recall from Lemma
1.29
that

p_{r,s}\gtrsim\left|T(o,r,s)\right|^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{T(o,r,s)}
; it follows that

\displaystyle V(g^{\prime},r,s)

\displaystyle=\int_{E(\alpha)}p_{r,s}((g^{\prime\prime})^{-1}g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime\prime}=\int_{E(\alpha)}p_{r,s}((g^{\prime})^{-1}g^{\prime\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{|E(\alpha)\cap T(g^{\prime},r,s)|}{|T(g^{\prime},r,s)|}.

When
(g^{\prime},r,s)\in\bigcup_{g\in A(\alpha)}\Gamma(g)
, there exists
g\in A(\alpha)
such that
g\in T(g^{\prime},r,s)
, and so

\displaystyle\frac{|E(\alpha)^{c}\cap T(g^{\prime},r,s)|}{|T(g^{\prime},r,s)|}\leq\mathcal{M}_{F}(\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)^{c}})(g)\leq\frac{1}{200}\,,

which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{|E(\alpha)\cap T(g^{\prime},r,s)|}{|T(g^{\prime},r,s)|}\geq\frac{199}{200}.

Hence there is a geometric constant
C_{1}\in(0,1)
such that

(5.18)

V(g^{\prime},r,s)\geq C_{1}\qquad\forall(g^{\prime},r,s)\in\Omega.

Further, we claim that there is a geometric constant
C_{0}
in
(C_{1}.\infty)
such that

(5.19)

V(g^{\prime},r,s)\leq C_{0}\qquad\forall(g^{\prime},r,s)\in\tilde{\Omega}.

Since
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}p_{r,s}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=1
, this follows similarly if the cone aperture
\beta
is large enough.

Now we choose a smooth function
\mathrm{H}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
such that

\mathrm{H}(t)=0
when
t\leq C_{1}
and
\mathrm{H}(t)=1
when
t\geq C_{0}
.
From (
5.18
),
\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))=1
when
(g^{\prime},r,s)\in\Omega
, and so, from (
5.17
),

(5.20)

\displaystyle\int_{A(\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{p}}^{2}(f)(g)\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|f\ast_{(1)}(\not{\nabla}p)_{r,s}(g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\right|^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|\nabla^{(1)}U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\right|^{2}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime},

where
U(g^{\prime},r,s)=f\ast_{(2)}\nabla^{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}\ast_{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}(g^{\prime})
.
We assume that
f
is real-valued; if not, one treats its real and imaginary parts separately.
As in (
5.12
),

(5.21)

\displaystyle\left|\nabla^{(1)}U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}^{(1)}\left(\left(U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-4U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\nabla^{(1)}U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\,\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))

\displaystyle-\left|U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}^{\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\,\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle-\left|U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\,\mathrm{H}^{\prime\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\,\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)\right|^{2},

and we write
\mathrm{i}_{1}(g^{\prime},r,s)
,
\mathrm{i}_{2}(g^{\prime},r,s)
,
\mathrm{i}_{3}(g^{\prime},r,s)
and
\mathrm{i}_{4}(g^{\prime},r,s)
for these four terms.

The right-hand side of (
5.20
) is bounded by
\sum_{j=1}^{4}\mathrm{I}_{j}
, where, for each
j
,

\mathrm{I}_{j}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\mathrm{i}_{j}(g^{\prime},r,s)\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\right|.

We treat
\mathrm{I}_{1}
using integration and the decay at infinity of the functions involved:

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{1}

\displaystyle=\biggl|\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\sum_{m=1}^{2n}\mathcal{X}_{j}^{2}\left(\left(U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\right)^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,s\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\partial_{r}^{2}\left(\left(U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\right)^{2}\right)\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}s\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(\left(U(g^{\prime},0,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},0,s))\right)^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s,

where
U(g^{\prime},0,s)
and
V(g^{\prime},0,s)
are interpreted as the obvious limits.

For the term
\mathrm{I}_{2},
we use Hölder’s inequality; then

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\biggl(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|\nabla^{(1)}U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\biggl(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2C}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\nabla^{(1)}U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,s\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{H}^{\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\,\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,s\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=:\mathrm{I}_{21}+\mathrm{I}_{22},

say, where
C
is large.
The term
\mathrm{I}_{21}
is a small multiple of the right-hand side of (
5.20
), and may be absorbed there.

It is easy to see that
\mathrm{I}_{3}
is dominated by
\mathrm{I}_{22}
, and
\mathrm{I}_{4}
is not dissimilar to
\mathrm{I}_{3}
.
More precisely, we take the smooth function
\mathrm{Z}
on
\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathrm{Z}(0)=0
and

\mathrm{Z}^{\prime}(t)=\bigl((\mathrm{H}^{\prime}(t))^{4}+(\mathrm{H}(t)\mathrm{H}^{\prime}(t))^{2}\bigr)^{1/4};

clearly
\mathrm{Z}^{\prime}
is supported in
[C_{0},C_{1}]
.

The right-hand side of (
5.20
) is bounded by a geometric multiple of

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(U(g^{\prime},0,s)\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},0,s))\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\mathrm{Z}^{\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\,\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,s\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathrm{J}_{1}+\mathrm{J}_{2},

say.
We treat
\mathrm{J}_{1}
using the argument of (
5.21
) in the central variable, and see that

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\bigl|\nabla^{(2)}f\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}(g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},0,s))\bigr|^{2}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|f(g^{\prime})\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},0,0))\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\bigl|f\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}(g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{H}^{\prime}(V(g^{\prime},0,s))\,\nabla^{(2)}V(g^{\prime},0,s)\bigr|^{2}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\bigl|{f\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}}(g^{\prime})\bigr|^{2}\Bigl|\mathrm{H}(V(g^{\prime},0,s))\,\mathrm{H}^{\prime\prime}(V(g^{\prime},0,s))\Bigr|\Bigl|\nabla^{(2)}V(g^{\prime},0,s)\Bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}.

Now
|f|\leq u^{*}
and
|f\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}|\leq u^{*}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})
by definition of
u^{*}
.
The definitions of
E(\alpha)
and
\mathrm{H}
imply that
u^{*}(g^{\prime})\leq\alpha
for all
g^{\prime}
and
s
such that
\mathrm{H}\bigl(({\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}}(g^{\prime})\bigr)\neq 0
and
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}\bigl(({\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}}(g^{\prime})\bigr)\neq 0
.
Hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{E(\alpha)}\bigl|u^{*}(g^{\prime})\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}+\alpha^{2}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\bigl|s\nabla^{(2)}(\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s})(g^{\prime})\bigr|^{2}\,\frac{ds}{s}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}.

Observe that
s\nabla^{(2)}(1\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s})=0
, and so

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|s\nabla^{(2)}(\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s})(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\,\frac{ds}{s}\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|{s\nabla^{(2)}(1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)})\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\frac{ds}{s}\ dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\|1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}(z^{\prime},\cdot)\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}

by Littlewood–Paley theory.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{E(\alpha)}\bigl|u^{*}(g^{\prime})\bigr|^{2}dg^{\prime}+\alpha^{2}\bigl|E(\alpha)^{c}\bigr|.

We turn to the term
\mathrm{J}_{2}
, which, up to a constant, is equal to

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,s\,\mathrm{d}s,

where
U(g^{\prime},r,s)=f\ast_{(2)}\nabla^{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}\ast_{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}(g^{\prime})
.
Now
|U(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))|^{2}
is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}^{(2)}\biggl(u(g^{\prime},r,s)^{2}\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))^{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\quad-4u(g^{\prime},r,s)\nabla^{(2)}u(g^{\prime},r,s)\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\nabla^{(2)}\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))

\displaystyle\quad-U(g^{\prime},r,s)^{2}\Bigl|\nabla^{(2)}\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\Bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-U(g^{\prime},r,s)^{2}\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\nabla^{(2)}\nabla^{(2)}\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s));

we write these expressions as
\mathrm{j}_{21}(g^{\prime},r,s)
,
\mathrm{j}_{22}(g^{\prime},r,s)
,
\mathrm{j}_{23}(g^{\prime},r,s)
and
\mathrm{j}_{24}(g^{\prime},r,s)
.
It follows straight away that
\mathrm{J}_{2}\leq\mathrm{J}_{21}+\mathrm{J}_{22}+\mathrm{J}_{23}+\mathrm{J}_{24}
, where

\mathrm{J}_{2i}=\biggl|\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\mathrm{j}_{2i}(g^{\prime},r,s)\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\biggr|

when
i=1,2,3,4
.

By definition,
\mathrm{Z}^{\prime}
is bounded, and
|f\ast_{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}(g^{\prime})|\leq u^{*}(g^{\prime})\leq\alpha
.
Further,

\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,0))=\mathrm{Z}^{\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,0))\,\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,0).

We estimate the term
\mathrm{J}_{21}
using integration by parts in
s
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{21}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\bigl|u(g^{\prime},r,0)\,\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,0))\bigr|^{2}\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathrm{Z}}\alpha^{2}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|r\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,0)\Bigr|^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{2}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|{(1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)})\ast_{(1)}r\nabla^{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}}(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\alpha^{2}\|1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\bigl|E(\alpha)^{c}\bigr|.

Hölder’s inequality and then the argument used for
\mathrm{I}_{2}
show that

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{22}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2C}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|\nabla^{(2)}u(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,r\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{C}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl|u(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\nabla^{(2)}\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,r\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad=:\mathrm{J}_{221}+\mathrm{J}_{222},

say; here
C
is large.
We see immediately that
\mathrm{J}_{221}
may be absorbed by
\mathrm{J}_{2}
.
To estimate
\mathrm{J}_{222}
, we note that
\nabla^{(2)}\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))
is equal to

\displaystyle\mathrm{Z}^{\prime\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)\nabla^{(2)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)+\mathrm{Z}^{\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\nabla^{(2)}\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,s).

Hence,
\mathrm{J}_{222}
is bounded by
\mathrm{J}_{2221}+\mathrm{J}_{2222}
, where the integrands of these terms are the two summands above.
By using the support condition on
\mathrm{Z}^{\prime\prime}
we see that

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{2221}

\displaystyle\lesssim\alpha^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\Bigl|\nabla^{(2)}\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(1)}p^{(2)}_{s}\ast_{(2)}p^{(1)}_{r}(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Bigl|{\nabla^{(1)}\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(2)}p^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(1)}p^{(2)}_{s}}(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,r\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\alpha^{2}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(2)}s\nabla^{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}\bigr)(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Bigl|\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(1)}r\nabla^{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}\bigr)(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\,\frac{ds}{s}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime},

where
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{R}}
are the Hardy–Littlewood maximal functions on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\mathbb{R}
.
By Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{2221}

\displaystyle\lesssim\alpha^{2}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl(s\nabla^{(2)}(\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s})\bigr)(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(r\nabla^{(1)}(\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r})\bigr)(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\frac{dr}{r}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{2}\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\bigl({s\nabla^{(2)}(\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s})}\bigr)(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\frac{ds}{s}\biggr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\Biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl({r\nabla^{(1)}(\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r})}\bigr)(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\frac{dr}{r}\biggr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\Biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{2}\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|{s\nabla^{(2)}(1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)})\ast_{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}}(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\frac{ds}{s}\biggr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\Biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|{(1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)})\ast_{(1)}r\nabla^{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}}(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\frac{dr}{r}\ \biggr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\Biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{2}\|1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\|_{\mathsf{L}^{4}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}\times\|1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\|_{\mathsf{L}^{4}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\bigl|E(\alpha)^{c}\bigr|,

where the third inequality follows from the Fefferman–Stein vector-valued inequality
[
15
]
for the Hardy–Littlewood maximal function and then replacement of
\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}
by
1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}
(which is possible since both
s\nabla^{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}
and
r\nabla^{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}
have mean zero); the last inequality follows from Littlewood–Paley theory.
By the support condition on
\mathrm{Z}^{\prime}
and the cancellation properties of
s\nabla^{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}
and
r\nabla^{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{2222}

\displaystyle\lesssim\alpha^{2}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|{\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\ast_{(1)}\nabla^{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\nabla^{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}}(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|{(1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)})\ast_{(1)}r\nabla^{(1)}p^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}s\nabla^{(2)}p^{(2)}_{s}}(g^{\prime})\Bigr|^{2}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{2}\|1-\mathbbm{1}_{E(\alpha)}\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\bigl|E(\alpha)^{c}\bigr|;

the second inequality follows from Littlewood–Paley theory, as in Corollary
1.45
.

The term
\mathrm{J}_{23}
may be handled in the same way as
\mathrm{J}_{2222}
.

Finally, we turn to
\mathrm{J}_{24}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\,\nabla^{(2)}\nabla^{(2)}\nabla^{(1)}\mathrm{Z}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathrm{Z}^{\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\,\mathrm{Z}^{\prime\prime\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))|\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)|^{2}|\nabla^{(2)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\mathrm{Z}^{\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\,\mathrm{Z}^{\prime\prime}(V(g^{\prime},r,s))\,\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\,\nabla^{(2)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)\,\nabla^{(1)}\nabla^{(2)}V(g^{\prime},r,s).

Hence
\mathrm{J}_{24}
is bounded by
\mathrm{J}_{241}+\mathrm{J}_{242}
, where these terms involve the two summands above in the integrands.
The term
\mathrm{J}_{241}
may be handled in the same way as
\mathrm{J}_{2221}
.
Hölder’s inequality implies that
\mathrm{J}_{242}
may be dominated by a multiple of

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\bigl|\nabla^{(2)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)\bigr|^{2}\bigl|\nabla^{(1)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)\bigr|^{2}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad+\alpha^{2}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Bigl|\nabla^{(1)}\nabla^{(2)}V(g^{\prime},r,s)\Bigr|^{2}\,s\,\mathrm{d}s\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime},

which is bounded by
C\alpha^{2}\bigl|E(\alpha)^{c}\bigr|
.

By combining all the estimates above, we prove (
5.16
), and so the theorem.
∎

6.
Singular integrals and the proof of Theorem A

In this section, we complete the proof of Theorem A by first showing that flag Riesz transforms also characterise the flag Hardy space, and then putting everything together.

6.1.
The inclusion
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{Riesz}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\subseteq\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})

In this section, we show that the Hardy space defined by singular integrals is a subspace of the square function Hardy space.
We remind the reader of a definition.

Definition 6.1
.

The (tensor-valued)
flag Riesz transformations
are given by

\mathcal{R}_{(1)}=\nabla_{(1)}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-1/2},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{(2)}=\nabla_{(2)}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-1/2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{R}_{F}=\mathcal{R}_{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{R}_{(2)}.

The space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{Riesz}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is the set of all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
such that
\mathcal{R}_{(1)}(f)
,
\mathcal{R}_{(2)}(f)
and
\mathcal{R}_{F}(f)
lie in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, with norm
\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{Riesz}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
defined to be

(6.1)

\left\|\mathcal{R}_{F}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|\mathcal{R}_{(1)}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|\mathcal{R}_{(2)}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

We are going to prove a slightly more general result, for which we need some more notation.
Let
\{\mathcal{R}_{j}:j=0,\dots,J\}
be a collection of simple singular integral operators on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
that characterise the Folland–Stein–Christ–Geller Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
; that is,
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
if and only if
\mathcal{R}_{j}f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
for each
j
.
In particular, the identity operator together with the Riesz transformations would do.
Then

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\eqsim\sum_{j=0}^{J}\left\|\mathcal{R}_{j}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})},

by the closed graph theorem.
For more information, see
[
10
]
.
We write
\mathcal{H}
for the Hilbert transformation in the central variable on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, that is,

\mathcal{H}f(z,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(z,t-s)\frac{ds}{s}\,.

Definition 6.2
.

The space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{sing}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is the set of all functions
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
for which
\mathcal{R}_{j}f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{R}_{j}f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
j=0,\dots,J
, with norm

(6.2)

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{sing}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}:=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\left\|\mathcal{R}_{j}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|H\mathcal{R}_{j}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{j}
and
\mathcal{H}
be as above.
If
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathcal{R}_{j}f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{R}_{j}f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, where
j=0,\dots,J
, then
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
for some suitable
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
, and
\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{sing}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
.

Proof.

We combine the characterisation of Christ and Geller with a randomisation argument.
We remind the reader that the operators
\mathcal{R}_{j}
and
\mathcal{H}
commute.

We recall from the classical theory of Hardy spaces that
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
if and only if both
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathcal{H}f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, and that the Hörmander–Mihlin multiplier theorem on
\mathbb{R}
shows that if
\mathrm{M}
is a bounded function on
\mathbb{R}
, differentiable except perhaps at
0
, such that
y\mapsto y\mathrm{M}^{\prime}(y)
is also bounded, then the associated Fourier multiplier operator
f\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathrm{M}\hat{f})
is bounded from
\mathsf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
to
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
(here
\mathcal{F}
indicates the Fourier transformation
f\mapsto\hat{f}
on
\mathbb{R}
).
For the details, see, for instance,
[
11
]
.
A similar result holds for the one-parameter Hardy space on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, except that more differentiability of
\mathrm{M}
is required; it suffices that the functions
y\mapsto y^{k}\mathrm{M}^{(k)}(y)
are bounded for
k
between
0
and
\nu+2
, but more derivatives may be used without any changes other than in the value of some unimportant constants.

Take a
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
function
\mathrm{H}
, supported in
[1/2,2]
, such that
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{H}(2^{n}y)=1
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Then by spectral theory, there are smooth functions
\varphi^{(1,m)}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\varphi^{(2,n)}
on
\mathbb{R}
such that
f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}=\mathrm{H}(2^{m}\mathcal{L}_{(1)})f
and
f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}=\mathrm{H}(2^{n}\mathcal{L}_{(2)})f
for all
f\in\mathsf{S}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

In this paragraph, we consider functions on
\mathbb{R}
.
Let
s_{n}:\Omega_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
be a collection of independent Rademacher random variables (that is, each takes the values
\pm 1
with probability one half); then for each
\omega_{2}\in\Omega_{2}
, the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\omega_{2}}
from
\mathsf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
to
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, defined (at least formally) by

\mathcal{A}_{\omega_{2}}f:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)},

is bounded, with norm bounded independently of
\omega_{2}
, by the Mikhlin–Hörmander multiplier theorem.
That is,

\left\|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})}+\left\|\mathcal{H}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})}

for all
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, or equivalently, for all
f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathcal{H}f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

We take a function
f
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
such that
\mathcal{R}_{j}f
and
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{R}_{j}f
lie in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and apply the preceding observation to the functions
\mathcal{R}_{j}f(z,\cdot)
and
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{R}_{j}f(z,\cdot)
.
We then integrate over
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and deduce that

\left\|\mathcal{R}_{j}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim\left\|\mathcal{R}_{j}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|\mathcal{H}\mathcal{R}_{j}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

Now
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
from the Folland–Stein–Christ–Geller characterisation
[
10
, Theorem A]
, and so, similarly, if
r_{m}:\Omega_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
is another family of independent Rademacher random variables, independent of the first family, then

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}r_{m}(\omega_{1})s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}.

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}r_{m}(\omega_{1})\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)},

and this function lies in
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}r_{m}(\omega_{1})\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left(\left\|\mathcal{R}_{j}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|\mathcal{H}\mathcal{R}_{j}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\right).

It now follows from the orthonormality of the functions
r_{m}
and
s_{n}
, Khinchin’s inequality, Minkowski’s inequality, and Khinchin’s inequality that

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}(g)\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(\int_{\Omega_{1}}\int_{\Omega_{2}}\left|\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}r_{m}(\omega_{1})s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}(g)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\left(\int_{\Omega_{1}}\left|\int_{\Omega_{2}}\left|\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}r_{m}(\omega_{1})s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{\Omega_{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega_{1}}\left|\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}r_{m}(\omega_{1})s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}(g)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\int_{\Omega_{2}}\int_{\Omega_{1}}\left|\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}r_{m}(\omega_{1})s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}.

By integrating over
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and using Fubini’s theorem and the estimates above, we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}(g)\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\iint_{\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}r_{m}(\omega_{1})\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{n}(\omega_{2})f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\iint_{\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\left(\left\|\mathcal{R}_{j}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}+\left\|\mathcal{H}\mathcal{R}_{j}f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{Riesz}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})},

as required.
∎

The reader who is uncomfortable with our formal calculations may take sums over finite subsets of
\mathbb{Z}
in the arguments above, and then allow these subsets to become arbitrarily large.
It may also be worth observing that

f\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}=f\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1,m)}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2,n)}
in the proof above.

6.2.
Conclusion of the proof of Theorem A and remarks

We have already shown in Section
2
that various singular integral operators, including the Riesz transformations, map
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
into
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and in Section
4
that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{dis}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})=\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{atom}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
(and corresponding norm inequalities hold).
Theorem
6.3
therefore completes our characterisation of the Hardy space by singular integrals, and completes our proof of Theorem A.

We are now able to sharpen the results about certain singular integral operators.

Corollary 6.4
.

The Hilbert transformation
\mathcal{H}
and simple singular integral operators, as defined in Section 2.6, are bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Proof.

Take a family of simple homogeneous singular integral operators
\mathcal{R}_{j}
that characterise the Folland–Stein–Christ–Geller space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and
f
in
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Then
\mathcal{R}_{j}f
and
\mathcal{R}_{j}\mathcal{H}f
all lie in
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
As
\mathcal{H}^{2}
is the identity,
\mathcal{R}_{j}(\mathcal{H}f)
and
\mathcal{R}_{j}\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{H}f)
all lie in
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, so that
\mathcal{H}f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Similarly, if
\mathcal{K}
is a simple homogeneous singular integral operator, and
f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, then
\mathcal{R}_{j}\mathcal{K}f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
since the composition
\mathcal{R}_{j}\mathcal{K}
is a linear combination of the identity and another simple homogeneous singular integral operator.
Further, commutativity and the same argument shows that
\mathcal{R}_{j}\mathcal{H}\mathcal{K}f=\mathcal{R}_{j}\mathcal{K}\mathcal{H}f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
since
\mathcal{H}f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
We therefore conclude that
\mathcal{K}f\in\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

On the one hand, the main theorem tells us that
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
may be characterised by homogeneous flag singular integrals.
On the other hand, the definition of an atom involves cancellations, in the sense that we impose conditions of the form
a=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b
for some function
b
, and these imply that
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}a(g)p(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=0
for certain polynomials of low degree.

It should now be apparent that, provided that
M\geq 1
and
N\geq 1
, we end up with the same atomic Hardy space.
Indeed, if
f
belongs to the flag Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
defined with
M
and
N
equal to
1
, then
\mathcal{K}f\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
for all flag singular integral operators, by Theorem
2.23
.
Then
f
lies in the Hardy space defined using discrete square functions, and hence has an atomic decomposition into atoms where the particles
a_{R}
are of the form
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}b_{R}
, for arbitrarily large
M
and
N
.

In a future paper, we propose to show that even less cancellation is needed.

Similarly, the other versions of the Hardy space involve auxiliary functions (usually written
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
).
It is now also clear that the Hardy space is largely independent of these functions, as long as they satisfy the relevant decay, cancellation and invertibility conditions.

7.
Applications of the main theorem

The main results in this section are an isomorphism of our Hardy space with the flag Hardy space of Han, Lu and Sawyer
[
30
]
, and the consequent identifications of the dual space of
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and of the interpolation space between
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and proofs of Theorems D and E.

7.1.
Characterisation of
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
by more general heat semigroups

In the proofs of Theorems
2.17
and
3.6
, we did not need to use much about the functions
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
.
However, we did use translation and dilation arguments to reduce estimates involving particles to estimates involving particles centred at the origin and of a certain size.
This was for convenience and to simplify the geometry.

We assert that our methods also work in settings that are not translation-invariant.
Hence, if we have self-adjoint operators
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
whose associated heat semigroups have kernels with Gaussian upper bounds, analogous estimates for derivatives, and the conservation property
\mathrm{e}^{-s\mathcal{L}_{1}}1=1
and
\mathrm{e}^{-t\mathcal{L}_{2}}1=1
, then we may define the flag area function associated to
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
by the formula

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}}(f)(g):=\biggl(\iiint_{\Gamma(g)}\bigl|(r^{2}\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathrm{e}^{-r^{2}\mathcal{L}_{1}}s^{2}\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathrm{e}^{-s^{2}\mathcal{L}_{2}})f(g^{\prime})\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}r}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\biggr)^{1/2},

and define the flag Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
associated to
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
to be the completion in the norm
\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}}(f)\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}
of the space
\{f\in\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}):\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}}(f)\in\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})\}
.
Our arguments (appropriately extended) show that the Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
coincides with our
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

This develops the theory of singular integrals with nonsmooth kernels and function spaces associated to operators, due to Duong and McIntosh
[
12
]
, Duong and Yan
[
13
,
14
]
, Hofmann and Mayboroda
[
32
]
, and others.
We expect that our Hardy space may be applied to the study of various types of differential equations on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.

7.2.
Comparison with the Han–Lu–Sawyer Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{HLS}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})

By definition and Theorem A, the reflection map
\mathcal{R}:f\mapsto f((\cdot)^{-1})
is a linear bijection of our flag Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and the Han–Lu–Sawyer
[
30
]
space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{HLS}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
This correspondence has several interesting consequences.

Proposition 7.1
.

The complex interpolation spaces between our flag Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and the Lebesgue space
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
are the Lebesgue spaces
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
where
1<p<2
.

Proof.

The map
\mathcal{R}
from our flag Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to the Han–Lu–Sawyer
[
30
]
space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{HLS}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is an isometry of all the Lebesgue spaces
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
Our interpolation theorem is now an immediate corollary of theirs.
∎

We define the space
\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to be the reflected version of the space of
[
30
]
.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
\breve{\varphi}^{(1)}
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
\breve{\varphi}^{(2)}
on
\mathbb{R}
be Schwartz functions,, and define
\varphi^{(1)}:=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\breve{\varphi}^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}:=\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}\breve{\varphi}^{(2)}
, where
M
and
N
are sufficiently large.
The flag BMO space
\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is the set of (equivalence classes of) measurable functions
b
on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
for which there exists a constant
C
such that

\left(\frac{1}{|E|}\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{M}(E)}\int_{0}^{\operatorname{h}(R)}\int_{0}^{\operatorname{w}(R)}\int_{R}\left|b\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(1)}_{r}\ast_{(2)}\varphi^{(2)}_{s}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g\,\frac{dr}{r}\,\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{1/2}\leq C

for all open subsets
E
of finite measure in
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
.
The “norm”
\left\|b\right\|_{\mathsf{BMO}_{F}}
is the least possible value of
C
in the expression above.

Note that there is a finite dimensional space of polynomials whose “norm” is
0
.
Note also that our conditions on
\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(2)}
are somewhat imprecise and probably much stronger than is actually needed.
To appeal to the results of
[
30
]
, we need to match their conditions.

Proposition 7.3
.

The dual space of the flag Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is the space
\mathsf{BMO}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Proof.

This follows from the identification of our flag Hardy space with that of
[
30
]
, and their proof of duality.
∎

Corollary 7.4
.

Suppose that
1<p<\infty
.
Then

\left\|\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(f)\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\qquad\forall f\in\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu}).

Proof.

From Theorem A and Proposition
3.4
,
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
is bounded from
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F,\mathrm{area}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and from
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and hence by interpolation
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
is bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p<2
.
A standard duality argument shows that
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{area},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
is also bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
2<p<\infty
.
∎

Corollary 7.5
.

Suppose that
(\varphi^{(1)},\varphi^{(2)})
is a Poisson bounded pair, as in Definition
4.1
.
Then the sublinear maps
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{cts},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{F,\mathrm{dis},\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
are bounded on
\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
when
1<p<\infty
.

Proof.

This also follows from Theorem A, interpolation and duality.
∎

7.3.
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is a proper subspace of the Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})

The singular integral characterisation of our Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
shows immediately that it is the subspace of the Folland–Stein–Christ–Geller Hardy space
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
of functions
f
such that both
f
and its Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}f
lie in
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

To see that it is a proper subspace, we consider the function
a
, defined by

a(z,t)=\psi(z)\,\varphi(t)\qquad\forall(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu},

where
\varphi
in
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is supported in
(-1,1)
and is nonnegative, while
\psi
in
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{\nu})
is supported in the unit ball of
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
; further

\int_{\mathbb{C}^{\nu}}\psi(z)\,\mathrm{d}z=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\int_{\mathbb{C}^{\nu}}|\psi(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z=1\qquad\text{while}\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=1.

Then
a
is a multiple of an
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{FSCG}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
atom, but
\left\|a^{*}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}=\infty
, where
a^{*}
is the radial maximal function of
a
, and hence
a\notin\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

This proves Theorem C.

7.4.
Sharp endpoint boundedness of certain flag singular integrals

Nagel, Ricci, Stein and Wainger
[
47
,
48
]
examined flag singular integral operators in considerable detail.

Definition 7.6
.

A general flag singular integral kernel is a distribution of the form

\sum_{j,k=\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi^{(1,j)}_{2^{j}}\ast_{(1)}\varphi^{(2,k)}_{2^{k}}\qquad\text{or equivalently}\qquad\left(\sum_{j=\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi^{(1,j)}_{2^{j}}\right)\ast_{(1)}\left(\sum_{k=\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi^{(2,k)}_{2^{k}}\right),

where the
\varphi^{(1,j)}
, where
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, form a uniformly bounded family of functions with mean
0
in
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
, and the
\varphi^{(2,k)}
, where
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, form a uniformly bounded family of functions with mean
0
in
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

A general flag singular integral operator is a convolution with such a distribution.

It is possible to show that the collection of all flag singular integral kernels is an algebra under convolution, by showing the collection of distributions of the form
\sum_{j=\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi^{(1,j)}_{2^{j}}
is an algebra, that the collection of distributions of the form
\sum_{k=\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi^{(2,k)}_{2^{k}}
is also an algebra, and observing that the distributions from the first collection commute with those from the second collection.

Both the Phong–Stein and Cauchy–Szegõ operators are convolutions with general flag singular integral kernels.
Hence their boundedness on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
is an immediate consequence of the following result.

Theorem D
.

General flag singular integral operators are bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Proof.

A slight modification of the proof of Theorem
2.23
(see the remark following the proof of this theorem) shows that general flag singular operators map
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to
\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.
The fact that flag singular integral operators form an algebra then implies that general flag singular operators map
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
to itself, by an argument like that of the proof of Corollary
6.4
, and proves Theorem D.
∎

7.5.
Sharp endpoint boundedness of Marcinkiewicz multipliers

In this section, we prove Theorem E; we begin with a lemma.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
d_{(1)}
be the gauge distance on
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and
d_{(2)}
be the Euclidean distance on
\mathbb{R}
.
Suppose that
\Phi\in\mathsf{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\operatorname{supp}\Phi\subseteq[1/2,2]
, that
E
and
E^{\prime}
are closed subsets of
\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
, and that
E_{\mathbb{R}}
and
E^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
are closed subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.
Then for any
s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
,

(7.1)

\displaystyle\|\Phi(2^{-j}\mathcal{L}_{(1)})\|_{\mathsf{L}^{p}(E)\to\mathsf{L}^{p}(E^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s}(1+2^{j/2}d_{(1)}(E,E^{\prime}))^{-s},

(7.2)

\displaystyle\|\Phi(2^{-j}i\mathcal{T})\|_{\mathsf{L}^{p}(E_{\mathbb{R}})\to\mathsf{L}^{p}(E^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s}(1+2^{j}d_{(2)}(E_{\mathbb{R}},E^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}))^{-s}.

Proof.

The convolution kernel
k_{\Phi(\mathcal{L}_{(1)})}
of
\Phi(\mathcal{L}_{(1)})
is a Schwartz function.
Hence

\left|k_{\Phi(2^{-j}\mathcal{L}_{(1)})}(g)\right|\lesssim_{s}2^{\delta j/2}(1+2^{j/2}\left\|g\right\|)^{-2s}

for all
g\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}
and all
s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
We assume that
s>\delta
and that
p\in(1,\infty)
.
If
f\in\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\operatorname{supp}f\subseteq E
while
f^{\prime}\in\mathsf{L}^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
and
\operatorname{supp}f^{\prime}\subseteq E^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle(1+2^{j/2}d_{(1)}(E,E^{\prime}))^{s}\left|\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\Phi(2^{-j}\mathcal{L}_{(1)})(f)(g^{\prime})f^{\prime}(g^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad=(1+2^{j/2}d_{(1)}(E,E^{\prime}))^{s}\left|\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}k_{\Phi(2^{-j}\mathcal{L}_{(1)})}(g^{-1}g^{\prime})f(g)f^{\prime}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim_{s}\iint_{E\times E^{\prime}}\frac{2^{\delta j/2}(1+2^{j/2}d_{(1)}(E,E^{\prime}))^{s}}{(1+2^{j/2}d_{(1)}(g,g^{\prime}))^{2s}}\left|f(g)\right|\left|f^{\prime}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\iint_{E\times E^{\prime}}\frac{2^{\delta j/2}}{(1+2^{j/2}d_{(1)}(g,g^{\prime}))^{s}}\left|f(g)\right|\left|f^{\prime}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left(\iint_{E\times E^{\prime}}2^{\delta j/2}(1+2^{j/2}d_{(1)}(g,g^{\prime}))^{-s}|f(g)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}g\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left(\iint_{E\times E^{\prime}}2^{\delta j/2}(1+2^{j/2}d_{(1)}(g,g^{\prime}))^{-s}|f^{\prime}(g)|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}g\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}2^{\delta j/2}(1+2^{j/2}d_{(1)}(g,g^{\prime}))^{-s}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\left|f(g)\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}g\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}2^{\delta j/2}(1+2^{j/2}d_{(1)}(g,g^{\prime}))^{-s}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\left|f^{\prime}(g)\right|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}g\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}(1+d_{(1)}(e,g^{\prime}))^{-s}\,\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\left\|f^{\prime}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})},

as required.
If
p=1
or
p=\infty
the modifications are easy.
The proof of (
7.2
) is similar.
∎

We restate Theorem E for convenience.
First we define the Marcinkiewicz norm of a function
\mathrm{M}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{C}
.
Fix
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and set
\alpha=\nu+\varepsilon
and
\beta=(1+\varepsilon)/2
, and set

\left\|\mathrm{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{Mar}}:=\sup\{\left\|\Theta\,\mathrm{M}(r\cdot,s\cdot)\right\|_{L_{\alpha,\beta}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}:r\in\mathbb{R}^{+},s\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\}.

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
\Theta
be a nonzero compactly supported function in
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Suppose that
\mathrm{M}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{C}
and
\left\|\mathrm{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{Mar}}<\infty
.
Then both the operators
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/\left|\mathcal{T}\right|,i\mathcal{T})
and
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
are bounded on
\mathsf{H}^{1}_{F}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})
.

Proof.

As remarked by Müller, Ricci and Stein
[
44
]
, a partition of unity argument shows that the choice of
\Theta
is immaterial; further, if
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/\left|\mathcal{T}\right|,i\mathcal{T})
is bounded, then so is
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})
.

It is clear that if
\mathrm{M}
satisfies the condition of the theorem, so do the functions
\mathrm{M}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}
and
\mathrm{M}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{-}}
.
It therefore suffices to treat functions
\mathrm{M}
supported in the first or the second quadrant.
We treat functions
\mathrm{M}
supported in the first quadrant; the modifications to treat the second case are very simple.

Take a smooth,
[0,1]
-valued function
\mathrm{H}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, whose support is contained in
(1/2,2)
, such that
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|\mathrm{H}(2^{-j}\cdot)\right|=1
in
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and set

\mathrm{M}_{j,\ell}(\lambda/\mu,\mu):=\mathrm{H}(2^{-j}\lambda/\mu)\,\mathrm{H}(2^{-\ell}\mu)\,\mathrm{M}(\lambda/\mu,\mu)\qquad\forall\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Thus

(7.3)

\mathrm{M}_{j,\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}):=\mathrm{H}(2^{-j}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|)\,\mathrm{H}(2^{-\ell}\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T})\,\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}).

and

(7.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,\ell}\mathrm{M}_{j,\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}).

The joint spectrum of
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
and
\mathcal{T}
is the Heisenberg fan (see Section
1.9
), and therefore
\mathrm{H}(2^{-j}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|)=0
if
j
is negative.
We assume that
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
in what follows.

Suppose that
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We define the smooth compactly supported function
\mathrm{H}^{(m)}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
by
\mathrm{H}^{(m)}(\mu):=\mu^{m}\mathrm{H}(\mu)
.
Then
\mathrm{H}^{(m)}
and
\mathrm{H}
satisfy similar Sobolev estimates and their supports coincide, and

\lambda^{m}\,\mu^{n}\,\mathrm{H}(2^{-j}\lambda/\mu)\,\mathrm{H}(2^{-\ell}\mu)=2^{m(j+\ell)+n\ell}\,\mathrm{H}^{(m)}(2^{-j}\lambda/\mu)\,\mathrm{H}^{(m+n)}(2^{-\ell}\mu),

so by functional calculus,

\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}(i\mathcal{T})^{n}\mathrm{M}_{j,\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T})=:2^{m(j+\ell)+n\ell}\mathrm{M}_{j,\ell}^{m,n}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}),\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}),

where

(7.5)

\mathrm{M}_{j,\ell}^{m,n}(\lambda/\mu,\mu)=\mathrm{H}^{(m)}(2^{-j}\lambda/\mu)\mathrm{H}^{(m+n)}(2^{-\ell}\mu)\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}).

Further, if
a_{R}
is a particle associated to the shard
R
, of width
w
and height
h
, then there is a function
c_{R}
supported in
R^{*}
such that
a_{R}=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{N}c_{R}
, and for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T})^{n}a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\lesssim_{m,n}w^{-2m}h^{-n}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

It is consistent to define
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-m}(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T})^{-n}a_{R}
to be
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{M-m}(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T})^{2N-n}c_{R}
when
0\leq m\leq M
and
0\leq n\leq 2N
.

Hence, as the algebra of operators generated by
\mathcal{L}_{(1)}
and
\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}
is commutative, for all
m
and
n
such that
|m|\leq M
and
|n|\leq 2N
,

(7.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{M}_{j,\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)},i\mathcal{T})a_{R}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{n}\,\mathrm{M}_{j,\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T})\,\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-n}a_{R}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{m}\mathrm{H}(2^{-j}\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|)\,\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{n}\mathrm{H}(2^{-\ell}i\mathcal{T})\,\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T})\,\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-n}a_{R}

\displaystyle=2^{m(j+\ell)+n\ell}\mathrm{M}_{j,\ell}^{m,n}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T})\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-n}a_{R}

\displaystyle=2^{m(j+\ell)+n\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-n}a_{R})\ast_{(1)}k^{m,n}_{j,\ell},

where
k^{m,n}_{j,\ell}
is the kernel of the operator
\mathrm{M}_{j,\ell}^{m,n}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T})
.
In order to exploit the factor
2^{m(j+\ell)+n\ell}
, we choose
m
and
n
to be positive when
j+\ell
and
\ell
are negative, and
0
otherwise.

Observe that, if
a_{R}
is a particle associated to a shard
R
, and
R\subset S
, then

(7.7)

\displaystyle\int_{(S^{*})^{c}}\left|a_{R}\ast_{(1)}k_{j,\ell}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=2^{m(j+\ell)+n\ell}\int_{(S^{*})^{c}}\left|(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-n}a_{R})\ast_{(1)}k^{m,n}_{j,\ell}(g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle\leq 2^{m(j+\ell)+n\ell}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-n}a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}\sup\Bigl\{\int_{(S^{*})^{c}}\left|k^{m,n}_{j,\ell}(g_{1}^{-1}g)\right|\,\mathrm{d}g:g_{1}\in R^{*}\Bigr\},

and

(7.8)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-n}a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\leq\left|R\right|^{1/2}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{(1)}^{-m}\mathcal{L}_{(2)}^{-n}a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\operatorname{w}^{2m}(R)\operatorname{h}^{2n}(R)\left|R\right|^{1/2}\left\|a_{R}\right\|_{\mathsf{L}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{\nu})}.

We propose to estimate the supremum in (
7.7
) so as to be able to sum over
j
and
\ell
and apply Proposition
2.16
.
Some simplifications are possible: first, by dilation and translation invariance, it suffices to suppose that the shard
R
has centre
o
, width
1
, and height
h
, where
h>1
.
By Proposition
2.16
, it suffices to take shards
S
with centre
o
whose width and height are much greater than
1
and
h
.
By doing so, and then reducing the width of
S
a little if necessary, we may also assume that
\operatorname{h}(S)\geq 4h+4\nu\operatorname{w}^{2}(S)+4\nu
.
Finally, we may assume that the enlargement parameter
\kappa
is equal to
2
.

We are going to use weighted
\mathsf{L}^{2}
estimates, which go back to work of Müller, Ricci and Stein
[
44
]
, and we now introduce the relevant weight functions and a mild variation of a result of Müller, Ricci and Stein
[
44
]
.

Definition 7.9
.

For
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we define the
weight function

w_{j,\ell}^{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{H}^{\nu}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
by

w_{j,\ell}^{\varepsilon}(z,t):=2^{-\nu(j+\ell)}(1+2^{j+\ell}|z|^{2})^{\nu(1+\varepsilon)}2^{-\ell}(1+2^{\ell}|t|)^{1+\varepsilon}\qquad\forall(z,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\nu}.

Lemma 7.10
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and set
\alpha=\nu+\varepsilon
and
\beta=(1+\varepsilon)/2
.
Let
k^{m,n}_{j,\ell}
be the convolution kernel of the operator
\mathrm{M}^{m,n}_{j,\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{(1)}/|\mathcal{T}|,i\mathcal{T})
given by (
7.5
).
Then

(7.9)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{\nu}}\left|k^{m,n}_{j,\ell}(g)\right|^{2}w_{j,\ell}^{\varepsilon}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\right)^{1/2}\lesssim_{c,\varepsilon,m,n}\left\|\mathrm{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{Mar}}.

Proof.

See Proposition 5.3 and Lemma 2.5 of
[
44
]
.
∎

By Proposition
2.16
and Theorem A, it suffices to find
